Integral Hamiltonian Floer Theory: Foundations Thanks: The first author is supported by NSF DMS-2404843 Thanks: The second author is supported by NSF DMS-2345030, NSF DMS-2506403, and Simons Foundation’s Travel Support for Mathematicians.
Shaoyun Bai, Guangbo Xu

Abstract
. Continuing our previous work on the integral Arnold conjecture [ 14 ] , we establish the foundation of Hamiltonian Floer theory over integer coefficients on any compact symplectic manifold. This package produces a well-defined chain homotopy class of Floer complexes, chain-level continuation maps, the Piunikhin–Salamon–Schwarz (PSS) isomorphism, and an associative pair-of-pants product. When the Hamiltonian is the 𝗉 {\sf p} -th iteration for a prime number 𝗉 {\sf p} , we also establish the ℤ 𝗉 {{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}} -equivariant Floer theory in characteristic 𝗉 {\sf p} , including the equivariant Floer complex and the ℤ 𝗉 {{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}} -equivariant pair-of-pants product. Moreover, we define quantum Steenrod operations for any compact symplectic manifold and compare them with the equivariant pair-of-pants products via the ℤ 𝗉 {{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}} -equivariant PSS map. We also obtain chain-level invariants such as spectral numbers and barcodes and prove quantitative properties of the equivariant pair-of-pants product. The counting theory is based on Fukaya–Ono’s normally polynomial perturbation scheme [ 29 ] and its realization by the authors in [ 13 ] , termed FOP perturbations, adapted in the abstract setting of flow categories, flow multimodules, and homotopies of flow bimodules. Along the way, we construct global Kuranishi charts for the relevant moduli spaces following Abouzaid–McLean–Smith’s framework and its adaptation to the Hamiltonian Floer setting due to the authors, which may be of independent interest.



1.
Introduction

Floer theory is one of the most influential discoveries in geometry and topology. In symplectic topology, the
Hamiltonian Floer homology

[
24
]
provides a powerful framework for studying Hamiltonian dynamics. Most notably, it leads to proofs of various versions of the famous Arnold conjecture
[
6
]
. Several variations, such as quantitative Floer theory and local Floer theory, played important roles in many other applications in Hamiltonian dynamics.

The development of Floer homology was accompanied by the advancement of the understanding of pseudoholomorphic curves
[
32
]
and regularizations of their moduli spaces. In earlier works
[
24
]
[
35
]
[
50
]
where certain vanishing or positivity conditions were assumed, when defining the Floer complexes, one only needs to consider moduli spaces of virtual dimensions at most one; one can achieve transversality of such moduli spaces by perturbing geometrically and can obtain Floer complexes with coefficients in
{\mathbb{Z}}
. When the semipositivity condition was removed, there is no known way to achieve transversality via geometric perturbations without imposing further conditions (cf.
[
20
]
). The abstract perturbation methods used in
[
43
]
[
30
]
[
36
]
[
53
]
require one to regularize the moduli spaces via
Kuranishi models
or
polyfolds
and to apply virtual perturbations or integrations. One then has to deal with moduli spaces of negative virtual dimensions and to organize all the constructions in a compatible way which can be formalized using Cohen–Jones–Segal’s notion of
flow categories

[
22
]
. The lack of geometric transversality is closely intertwined with the orbispace structure of the moduli spaces, which naturally leads to fractional counts of curves and chain-level structures only defined over
{\mathbb{Q}}
.

The main purpose of the current work is to extend Floer theory from rational to integer coefficients for general closed symplectic manifolds. In
[
14
]
we have already established a portion of the integral Floer theory which provides a proof of the Arnold conjecture over
{\mathbb{Z}}
. The motivation to go beyond the scope of
[
14
]
, besides a complete package of the integral Hamiltonian Floer theory, resides in the potential applications in Hamiltonian dynamics. For this purpose, we will construct quantum Steenrod operations for general symplectic manifolds (see
[
72
]
[
59
]
[
60
]
[
62
]
under semipositivity or stronger assumptions). In an upcoming joint work with Shelukhin and Wilkins
[
12
]
, we will extend many dynamical applications (see
[
63
]
[
64
]
[
65
]
[
21
]
[
7
]
) of quantum Steenrod operations to greater generality.

The integral extension is possible due to the recent developments in the virtual technique. The most crucial idea was given by Fukaya–Ono
[
29
]
. Roughly speaking, the moduli spaces carry natural stable complex structures, which allow a specific type of single-valued perturbations (normally polynomial perturbations) resulting in well-defined integer counts. Fukaya–Ono discussed the original idea for a local Kuranishi model, while B. Parker
[
54
]
further explored strategies to globalize. In
[
13
]
we provide a rigorous treatment of Fukaya–Ono’s proposal and define the so-called “FOP transversality condition” for normally polynomial perturbations.

Another crucial concurrent development is the Abouzaid–McLean–Smith construction of global Kuranishi charts (see
[
3
,
34
,
4
]
). This new technique helps avoid the complicated inductive arguments involving multiple charts (such as having good coordinate systems
[
25
]
). Meanwhile, the smoothing argument of
[
3
]
replaces the hard analytic approach
[
28
]
by a soft topological approach. These novel ideas helped significantly in the case of Hamiltonian Floer theory. One of the main challenges in Floer theory is to carry out AMS construction for infinitely many moduli spaces compatibly.

We hope that the foundational work laid in this paper can serve as a useful package for applications of Floer theory to Hamiltonian dynamics on general compact symplectic manifolds. Our joint paper with Shelukhin and Wilkins
[
12
]
showcases how the technical work done here can efficiently lead to interesting statements for Hamiltonian maps on general compact symplectic manifolds. Meanwhile, we hope the technical procedures can serve as a model to be adopted in other situations such as in Lagrangian Floer theory or contact topology.

Our main results fall into three parts:

(1)

Integral Hamiltonian Floer theory at the chain level (Theorems
A
—
G
).

(2)

Equivariant Floer theory and quantum Steenrod operations (Theorems
H
—
J
).

(3)

Quantitative refinements, including spectral invariants and barcodes (Theorems
K
—
M
) as well as results about Floer complexes of homologically nontrivial orbits (Theorem
1.11
and Theorem
1.12
).

Below we state these main results in a way that readers can directly cite.

1.1.
Main results on Floer complexes

1.1.1.
Floer complexes and Floer homology

First and foremost, by applying the FOP perturbation scheme to the moduli spaces of Floer trajectories, we construct a Floer complex with integer coefficients, which is well-defined up to chain homotopy equivalence. Under certain restrictions of the Hamiltonian, such a complex was constructed by the authors
[
14
]
without proving the homotopy equivalence between different choices.

We start by recalling the basic setup. Let
(X,\omega)
be a compact symplectic manifold. Let

\Pi:=\frac{\pi_{2}(X)}{{\rm ker}(\omega)\cap{\rm ker}(c_{1})},

(1.1)

which is a finitely generated free abelian group. Let
\Lambda^{\Pi}
be the Novikov ring with integer coefficients:

\Lambda^{\Pi}=\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}a_{i}T^{A_{i}}\ |\ a_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}},\ A_{i}\in\Pi,\ \lim_{i\to\infty}\omega(A_{i})=+\infty\Big\}.

(1.2)

If
H
is a nondegenerate 1-periodic Hamiltonian generating a 1-periodic family of Hamiltonian vector fields
X_{H_{t}}
, a 1-periodic orbit is a map
x:S^{1}\to X
solving

x^{\prime}(t)=X_{H_{t}}(x(t)).

A
capped 1-periodic orbit
is a pair
p=(x,\tilde{x})
where
x
is a 1-periodic orbit and
\tilde{x}
is an equivalence class of extensions of
x
to the disk
{\mathbb{D}}
, where two extensions are equivalent if their difference, regarded as a map from
S^{2}
to
X
, has zero symplectic area and zero Chern number. Each capped 1-periodic orbit
p
has a well-defined symplectic action

{\mathcal{A}}_{H}(p)=-\int_{\mathbb{D}}\tilde{x}^{*}\omega-\int_{S^{1}}H_{t}(x(t))dt

(1.3)

and a well-defined Conley–Zehnder index

{\rm deg}p:={\rm CZ}(p)\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

Denote the corresponding cohomological grading of capped periodic orbits by

|p|:=\frac{1}{2}{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{R}}X-{\rm CZ}(p).

Let
{\mathcal{O}}(H)
be the set of all contractible 1-periodic orbits and
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(H)
be the set of all capped 1-periodic orbits.

Theorem A

(Floer chain complex)
.

Let
(X,\omega)
be a compact symplectic manifold. Let
H
be a nondegenerate 1-periodic Hamiltonian. Then for each
1
-periodic family of
\omega
-compatible almost complex structures
J
and a certain choice
\Xi
, there is a
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Pi}
-linear differential on the chain group

CF_{*}(H):=\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}a_{i}p_{i}\ |\ a_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}},\ p_{i}\in\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(H),\ \lim_{i\to\infty}{\mathcal{A}}_{H}(p_{i})=-\infty\Big\}

(which has the structure of a module over
\Lambda^{\Pi}
), making it a chain complex of finitely generated free
\Lambda^{\Pi}
-modules

CF_{*}(H,J,\Xi;\Lambda^{\Pi})

with a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading defined by the Conley–Zehnder index. Moreover, the chain homotopy type of
CF_{*}(H,J,\Xi;\Lambda^{\Pi})
does not depend on the choice
\Xi
.

Next we establish the continuation map with expected properties.

Theorem B

(Continuation Map)
.

Let
(H_{1},J_{1},\Xi_{1})
and
(H_{2},J_{2},\Xi_{2})
be two triples such that the Floer chain complexes

\displaystyle\ CF_{*}(H_{1},J_{1},\Xi_{1};\Lambda^{\Pi}),\

\displaystyle\ CF_{*}(H_{2},J_{2},\Xi_{2};\Lambda^{\Pi})

are defined.

(1)

There exists a
\Lambda^{\Pi}
-linear chain homotopy equivalence, called the continuation map

\Phi^{12}(\Theta):CF_{*}(H_{1},J_{1},\Xi_{1};\Lambda^{\Pi})\to CF_{*}(H_{2},J_{2},\Xi_{2};\Lambda^{\Pi})

whose definition further depends on a system of choices
\Theta
.

(2)

The chain homotopy class of the continuation map does not depend on the system of choices
\Theta
.

(3)

For
(H_{1},J_{1},\Xi_{1})=(H_{2},J_{2},\Xi_{2})
, any continuation map is homotopic to the identity map of
CF_{*}(H_{1},J_{1},\Xi_{1};\Lambda^{\Pi})
.
1
1

1

However we do not know if the identity map on the Floer chain complex can be realized as a continuation map.

(4)

If
(H_{3},J_{3},\Xi_{3})
is another triple for which the complex
CF_{*}(H_{3},J_{3},\Xi_{3};\Lambda^{\Pi})
is defined, and

\Phi^{23}(\Theta^{\prime}):CF_{*}(H_{2},J_{2},\Xi_{2};\Lambda^{\Pi})\to CF_{*}(H_{3},J_{3},\Xi_{3};\Lambda^{\Pi})

is a continuation map. Then the composition
\Phi^{23}(\Theta^{\prime})\circ\Phi^{12}(\Theta)
is homotopic to a continuation map from
CF_{*}(H_{1},J_{1},\Xi_{1};\Lambda^{\Pi})
to
CF_{*}(H_{3},J_{3},\Xi_{3};\Lambda^{\Pi})
.

Theorem
A
and Theorem
B
lead to the definition of a canonical object

HF_{*}(X,\omega;\Lambda^{\Pi})

which we call the
integral Floer homology
of
(X,\omega)
.

1.1.2.
Pair-of-pants product

Next we consider the pair-of-pants product.

Theorem C

(Pair-of-pants product)
.

Let
(H_{i},J_{i},\Xi_{i})
,
i=1,2,3
be triples for which the Floer chain complexes are defined as stated in Theorem
A
. Then there is a
\Lambda^{\Pi}
-linear chain map

CF_{*}(H_{1},J_{1},\Xi_{1};\Lambda^{\Pi})\underset{\Lambda^{\Pi}}{\otimes}CF_{*}(H_{2},J_{2},\Xi_{2};\Lambda^{\Pi})\to CF_{*}(H_{3},J_{3},\Xi_{3};\Lambda^{\Pi})

(1.4)

of degree
-\frac{1}{2}{\rm dim}X
whose definition depends on a system of choices. Moreover, the chain map satisfies the following properties.

(1)

The homotopy class of (
1.4
) is independent of the choices.

(2)

This chain map is compatible with continuation maps. Hence it induces a canonical linear map

HF_{*}(X,\omega;\Lambda^{\Pi})\underset{\Lambda^{\Pi}}{\otimes}HF_{*}(X,\omega;\Lambda^{\Pi})\to HF_{*}(X,\omega;\Lambda^{\Pi})

called the pair-of-pants product.

(3)

The map (
1.4
) is associative up to homotopy. Hence
HF_{*}(X,\omega;\Lambda^{\Pi})
is a graded ring.

(4)

There exists a multiplicative identity

{\bf 1}\in HF_{*}(X,\omega;\Lambda^{\Pi}),

of cohomological degree zero called the Floer fundamental class.
3
3

3

We do not know if it coincides with the image of the classical fundamental class under the PSS map.

1.1.3.
Poincaré duality

We then discuss Poincaré-type pairings on Hamiltonian Floer homology.

Theorem D

(Poincaré duality)
.

Let
CF_{*}(H_{1},J_{1},\Xi_{1})
and
CF_{*}(H_{2},J_{2},\Xi_{2})
be Floer chain complexes provided by Theorem
A
. Then up to a choice
\Theta
, there is a
\Lambda^{\Pi}
-bilinear pairing

\Phi^{\rm PD}(\Theta):CF_{*}(H_{1},J_{1},\Xi_{1}){\underset{\Lambda^{\Pi}}{\otimes}}CF_{*}(H_{2},J_{2},\Xi_{2})\to\Lambda^{\Pi}

(1.5)

called the
chain-level Poincaré pairing
which satisfies the following conditions.

(1)

The homotopy type of the pairing is well-defined.

(2)

The pairing is compatible with continuation maps. Namely, suppose
CF_{*}(H_{3},J_{3},\Xi_{3})
is another Floer chain complex and
\Phi^{31}:CF_{*}(H_{3},J_{3},\Xi_{3})\to CF_{*}(H_{1},J_{1},\Xi_{1})
is a chain-level continuation map. Then the pairing

\Phi^{\rm PD}(\Phi^{31}(\cdot),\cdot):CF_{*}(H_{3},J_{3},\Xi_{3}){\underset{\Lambda^{\Pi}}{\otimes}}CF_{*}(H_{2},J_{2},\Xi_{2})\to\Lambda^{\Pi}

is homotopic to a chain-level Poincaré pairing.

(3)

In field coefficients, the induced Poincaré pairing on homology is nondegenerate. More precisely, let
{\bf K}
be a field and
\Lambda_{\bf K}^{\Pi}
be the Novikov field. Then the induced bilinear pairing

\Phi^{\rm PD}:\left(HF_{*}(X)\widehat{\underset{\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Pi}}{\otimes}}HF_{*}(X)\right)\underset{\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Pi}}{\otimes}\Lambda_{\bf K}^{\Pi}\to\Lambda_{\bf K}^{\Pi}

is nondegenerate.

(4)

The Poincaré duality is compatible with the pair-of-pants product. More precisely, the following diagram commutes.

\textstyle{HF_{*}(X,\omega)\widehat{\underset{\Lambda^{\Pi}}{\otimes}}HF_{*}(X,\omega)\widehat{\underset{\Lambda^{\Pi}}{\otimes}}HF_{*}(X,\omega)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi^{\rm pants}\otimes{\rm Id}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Id}\otimes\Phi^{\rm pants}}
\textstyle{HF_{*}(X,\omega)\widehat{\underset{\Lambda^{\Pi}}{\otimes}}HF_{*}(X,\omega)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi^{\rm PD}}
\textstyle{HF_{*}(X,\omega)\widehat{\underset{\Lambda^{\Pi}}{\otimes}}HF_{*}(X,\omega)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi^{\rm PD}}
\textstyle{\Lambda^{\Pi}}

1.1.4.
PSS map and isomorphism

Second, we consider the Piunikhin–Salamon–Schwarz (PSS) map (cf.
[
55
]
) from the Morse complex to the Floer complex and a map in the reversed direction called the SSP map.

Theorem E

(PSS and SSP maps)
.

Let
(f,g)
be a Morse–Smale pair on
X
with the associated Morse–Smale–Witten complex
CM_{*}(f,g)
.

(1)

Let
CF_{*}(H,J,\Xi;\Lambda^{\Pi})
be a Floer chain complex provided by Theorem
A
. Then there exists a
\Lambda^{\Pi}
-linear chain map

\Phi^{\rm PSS}:CM_{*}(f,g)\underset{\mathbb{Z}}{\otimes}\Lambda^{\Pi}\to CF_{*}(H,J,\Xi;\Lambda^{\Pi})

of degree
-n:=-\frac{1}{2}{\rm dim}X

and a
\Lambda^{\Pi}
-linear chain map

\Phi^{\rm SSP}:CF_{*}(H,J,\Xi;\Lambda^{\Pi})\to CM_{*}(f,g)\underset{\mathbb{Z}}{\otimes}\Lambda^{\Pi}

of degree
n
whose definitions depend on various choices.

(2)

The chain homotopy classes of
\Phi^{\rm PSS}
and
\Phi^{\rm SSP}
are independent of these choices.

(3)

The PSS and SSP maps are compatible with continuation maps, hence induce well-defined maps

\Phi^{\rm PSS}:H_{*}(X;\Lambda^{\Pi})\to HF_{*-n}(X,\omega;\Lambda^{\Pi})

\Phi^{\rm SSP}:HF_{*}(X,\omega;\Lambda^{\Pi})\to H_{*+n}(X;\Lambda^{\Pi}).

Theorem F
.

The PSS map on homology is an isomorphism of graded
\Lambda^{\Pi}
-modules.

Remark 1.1
.

We point out that we do not know if the PSS and the SSP maps are inverses to each other up to homotopy. In particular, the proof of Theorem
F
involves new moduli spaces beyond those appearing in the constructions of Theorem
E
.

Of course, the Arnold conjecture over integer coefficients now follows from Theorem
F
as a corollary, although in
[
14
]
we proved it by only considering the Floer chain complex for special
H
and proving only the injectivity part of Theorem
F
.

Corollary 1.2

(Integral Arnold conjecture, cf.
[
14
]
)
.

Let
\phi:X\to X
be a nondegenerate Hamiltonian diffeomorphism. Let
N
be the minimal Chern number of
(X,\omega)
. Then the number of fixed points of
\phi
is no less than the minimal number of generators of a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2N
-graded complex of free abelian groups whose homology is isomorphic to
H_{*}(X;{\mathbb{Z}})
viewed as a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2N
-graded abelian group.

If we compose the Floer-theoretic Poincaré pairing with the PSS map, then it induces a nondegenerate bilinear pairing on the homology
H_{*}(X;\Lambda^{\Pi})
.

Definition 1.3
.

The
FOP Poincaré pairing
on
H(X;\Lambda^{\Pi})
is the bilinear pairing

\langle\Phi^{\rm PSS}(\cdot),\Phi^{\rm PSS}(\cdot)\rangle:H(X;\Lambda^{\Pi}){\underset{\Lambda^{\Pi}}{\otimes}}H(X;\Lambda^{\Pi})\to\Lambda^{\Pi}.

If we use the PSS map defined using the technology of
[
30
]
[
43
]
[
53
]
, then over rational coefficients, such a pairing coincides with the classical Poincaré pairing on homology. This is because the orbifold Euler classes satisfy properties needed for
S^{1}
-localization, so there is no contribution from moduli spaces of curves with nonzero energy. However, the FOP Euler characteristic classes arising in our perturbation scheme do not necessarily satisfy these properties and we do not know if the pairing is classical or not. This phenomenon is similar to the case of quantum
K
-theory (see
[
41
]
) or quantum generalized cohomology developed by Abouzaid–McLean–Smith
[
4
]
. The point of introducing the quantum-corrected Poincaré pairing is to define an associative product analogous to the quantum product on
H_{*}(X;\Lambda^{\Pi})
in our context, where we use the pairing to turn three-pointed Gromov–Witten type counts into a bilinear operation on
H_{*}(X;\Lambda^{\Pi})
to dualize a marked point into an output marked point.

Another corollary is the invertibility of the Seidel map with integer coefficients defined in
[
11
]
. Recall that for each loop of Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms
\phi:S^{1}\to{\rm Ham}(X)
, one has a Hamiltonian fibration
\widetilde{M}_{\phi}\to S^{2}
with fibres being
M
. By counting holomorphic sections
u:S^{2}\to\widetilde{M}_{\phi}
(via FOP perturbation), one can define a map

S(\phi):H(X;\Lambda^{\Pi})\to H(X;\Lambda^{\Pi})

which only depends on the homotopy class
[\phi]\in\pi_{1}({\rm Ham}(X))
.
4
4

4

One uses the classical Poincaré duality in the definition. Hence we do not expect that
S(\phi)S(\psi)=S(\phi\psi)
. However, the main use in
[
11
]
of the Seidel map is its invertibility.
In the semipositive case, using classical perturbation method, one can prove that
S(\phi)
is invertible by showing
S(\phi\psi)=S(\phi)S(\psi)
and
S(1)={\rm Id}
. However, the invertibility of the Seidel maps can only be shown using the Hamiltonian model for the quantum cohomology.

Corollary 1.4

(Seidel representation)
.

The Seidel map
S(\phi)
is invertible.

Proof.

Fix an almost complex structure
J
. Choose a nondegenerate Hamiltonian
H_{t}
. Let
H_{t}^{\prime}
be the pullback by the loop
\phi_{t}
. On
S^{2}={\mathbb{R}}\cup S^{1}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
one can turn on a family of Hamiltonian connections parametrized by
S\in[0,\infty)
which is
H_{t}dt
on
[-S,S]\times S^{1}
and which vanishes near
\pm\infty
. We regard this as a family of Hamiltonian connections
\sigma_{S}
on
\widetilde{M}_{\phi}\to S^{2}
written with respect to the negative trivialization
\psi_{-}
. Then with respect to the positive trivialization, the connection reads
H^{\prime}dt
. Then consider the family of moduli spaces of holomorphic sections with respect to this family of Hamiltonian connections. When
S=0
, one obtains a bimodule defining the Seidel map
S(\phi)
, given as a chain map

\tilde{S}(\phi):CM(f)\to CM(f).

The induced map on homology is the Seidel map
S(\phi)
. When
S=+\infty
, one obtains a triple concatenation of a bimodule for PSS map, a bimodule for continuation map, and a bimodule for SSP map. Therefore,
\tilde{S}(\phi)
is chain homotopic to the composition of these three maps. By Theorem
B
and Theorem
E
, on cohomology level
S(\phi)
is invertible.
∎

1.1.5.
Comparison with known construction

When
(X,\omega)
is weakly monotone (also called semi-positive), then by perturbing either the almost complex structure
J
or the Hamiltonian
H
, Hofer–Salamon
[
35
]
and Ono
[
50
]
defined the Floer chain complex over integers. Such constructions can also be carried out to define continuation maps, pair-of-pants products, and Künneth isomorphisms with integer coefficients for weakly monotone
(X,\omega)
. It is natural to wonder if our constructions agree with the classical definitions in the weakly monotone setting. As a proof of principle, we demonstrate that this is the case for the Floer homology.

Theorem G
.

When
(X,\omega)
is weakly monotone, our Floer chain complex
CF_{*}(H,J,\Xi)
is chain homotopy equivalent to the one defined using the classical method.

We expect that the proof methods can be generalized to establish the identification between our Floer-theoretic invariants and the classical versions.

Remark 1.5
.

On the other hand, for a general compact
(X,\omega)
, Floer chain complex
CF_{*}^{\mathbb{Q}}(H,J)
over rational coefficients is constructed using the classical virtual technique (see
[
43
]
[
30
]
[
53
]
). Using the setup similar to here,
CF_{*}^{\mathbb{Q}}(H,J)
is a module over the rational Novikov field
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\Pi}
. Although there is an isomorphism

HF_{*}(X,\omega;\Lambda^{\Pi})\underset{\Lambda^{\Pi}}{\otimes}\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\Pi}\cong HF_{*}^{\mathbb{Q}}(X,\omega)

via the PSS maps in the two setups and the universal coefficient theorem for ordinary homology, one does not have an obvious relation on the chain level, between

CF_{*}(H,J,\Xi;\Lambda^{\Pi})\underset{\Lambda^{\Pi}}{\otimes}\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\Pi}\ \ \ {\rm and}\ \ \ CF_{*}^{\mathbb{Q}}(H,J)

without going through Morse theory.

1.2.
Main results on equivariant Floer theory and Steenrod operations

To discuss Steenrod operations and other aspects of equivariant Floer theory, we switch to the cohomological convention, partially to match with the fact that classical Steenrod operations are cohomological operations. In this way, by considering ascending flows rather than descending flows, one obtains the Floer cochain complex
CF^{*}(H,J,\Xi)
associated to a Floer flow category whose cochains are formal infinite linear combinations of capped 1-periodic orbits of
H
whose actions go to
+\infty
.

Let
{\sf p}
be a prime number. Abbreviate
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}:={\mathbb{Z}}/{\sf p}{\mathbb{Z}}
. When we view it as a field serving as the coefficient field of cohomology, we use
{\bf F}_{\sf p}
instead. In characteristic
{\sf p}
, on any topological space, there are the Steenrod power operations which can be organized as a single nonlinear map.

We will primarily use the “Tate” version of equivariant theory. Recall that the more familiar “Borel” version of the
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-equivariant cohomology of a point is

H^{\rm Borel}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}({\rm pt};{\bf F}_{\sf p})={\bf F}_{\sf p}[\mathtt{U}]\langle\uptheta\rangle

over
{\bf F}_{\sf p}
generated by a degree
2
variable
\mathtt{U}
and a degree
1
variable
\uptheta
satisfying

\displaystyle\ \mathtt{U}\uptheta=\uptheta\mathtt{U},\

\displaystyle\ \uptheta^{2}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathtt{U},&{\sf p}=2,\\
0,&{\sf p}>2.\end{array}\right.

It is defined as the ordinary cohomology of the classifying space
B{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
and admits a nice Morse model. We often need to take the completion by allowing formal power series.

The Tate version of the equivariant cohomology of a point differs from the Borel one by inverting the variable
\uptheta
(in characteristic
2
) resp.
\mathtt{U}
(in odd characteristics), i.e. (after completion)

H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}^{\rm Tate}({\rm pt};{\bf F}_{\sf p})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\bf F}_{2}{(\!(\uptheta)\!)},&\ {\sf p}=2,\\
{\bf F}_{\sf p}{(\!(\mathtt{U})\!)}\langle\uptheta\rangle,&\ {\sf p}>2.\end{array}\right.

Remark 1.6
.

There are two main reasons why we prefer the Tate version. First, all the nice properties of the equivariant Floer theory hold for the Tate version but not the Borel one, for example, localization. Second, the inversion of the variable
\mathtt{U}
follows more naturally from our geometric construction. The Borel version, instead, is regarded as a truncation of the Tate version. From now on, all equivariant cochain complexes or cohomology, without further declaration, will be the Tate version.

We introduce the following notions. Let

{\bf K}_{\sf p}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\bf F}_{2}{(\!(\uptheta)\!)},&\ {\sf p}=2,\\
{\bf F}_{\sf p}{(\!(\mathtt{U})\!)},&\ {\sf p}>2\end{array}\right.

The chain-level construction we use will always produce algebraic structures linear over
{\bf K}_{\sf p}
. On the other hand, denote

\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\bf K}_{\sf p},&\ {\sf p}=2,\\
{\bf K}_{\sf p}\langle\uptheta\rangle,&\ {\sf p}>2\end{array}\right..

The resulting cohomology groups (not complexes) will be modules over
\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}
. However, such module structure is not an algebraic consequence, but needs to be constructed geometrically.

1.2.1.
The quantum Steenrod operations

The classical Steenrod square resp. power operations are cohomological operations which can be formulated as a single map (for
{\sf p}=2
)

St_{2}:H^{*}(X;{\bf F}_{2})\to H^{*}(X;{\bf F}_{2}[\uptheta])\subseteq H^{*}(X;\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p})

resp. (
{\sf p}>2
)

St_{\sf p}:H^{*}(X;{\bf F}_{\sf p})\to H^{*}(X;{\bf F}_{\sf p}[\mathtt{U}]\langle\uptheta\rangle)\subseteq H^{*}(X;\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}).

The map
St_{\sf p}
is additive and linear over
{\bf F}_{\sf p}
. Moreover, if we regard
\mathtt{U}
of degree
2
and
\uptheta
of degree
1
, then
St_{\sf p}
multiplies the grading by
{\sf p}
. Fukaya proposed the idea of defining quantum Steenrod operation
[
31
]
. It has been adapted in the Floer setting by Seidel
[
60
]
and Shelukhin–Zhao
[
62
]
and in the quantum cohomology setting by Wilkins
[
72
]
and Seidel–Wilkins
[
59
]
. A key point in the construction is the integrality of curve counts, which then admit well-defined mod
{\sf p}
reduction. Hence the existing constructions are all restricted to monotone or semipositive settings. The FOP perturbation method developed in
[
13
]
allows one to extend to the general setting.

Theorem H

(Quantum Steenrod operation on general symplectic manifold)
.

For any compact symplectic manifold
(X,\omega)
, for each prime
{\sf p}
, there are well-defined additive maps

\begin{split}QSt_{2}:&\ H^{k}(X;{\bf F}_{2})\to H^{2k}(X;\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{2}{[\![\uptheta]\!]}}^{\Pi})\subseteq H^{2k}(X;\Lambda_{\tilde{\bf K}_{2}}^{\Pi}),\ ({\sf p}=2)\\
QSt_{\sf p}:&\ H^{k}(X;{\bf F}_{\sf p})\to H^{{\sf p}k}(X;\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}{[\![\mathtt{U}]\!]}}^{\Pi}\langle\uptheta\rangle)\subseteq H^{{\sf p}k}(X;\Lambda_{\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi})\ ({\sf p}>2)\end{split}

satisfying the following conditions.

(1)

The classical part (i.e., setting the Novikov parameter to be zero) of
QSt_{\sf p}
coincides with the classical Steenrod operation.

(2)

When
(X,\omega)
is semipositive,
QSt_{\sf p}
coincides with the quantum Steenrod operation defined by Seidel–Wilkins
[
59
]
(which, in
{\sf p}=2
and monotone case, agrees with Wilkins’ definition of quantum Steenrod square in
[
72
]
).

1.2.2.
Equivariant Floer cohomology

The Floer-theoretic version of the Steenrod operation has played important roles in many recent dynamical applications of Floer theory, see
[
60
]

[
63
,
64
,
65
]

[
21
]
etc. In this paper, we also generalize the construction of the Floer-theoretic Steenrod operation to general compact symplectic manifolds. Let
CF^{*}(H)
be a Floer chain complex associated to a nondegenerate Hamiltonian
H
, a compatible almost complex structure
J
, and a system of choices
\Xi
; to simplify notation, we suppress
J
and
\Xi
from the notation.

Theorem I

(Equivariant Floer cohomology)
.

Let
(X,\omega)
be a compact symplectic manifold and
{\sf p}
be a prime.

(1)

Let
H
be a 1-periodic Hamiltonian such that its
{\sf p}
-th iteration
H^{\natural{\sf p}}
is nondegenerate. Then there exists a cochain complex (the Tate
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-equivariant Floer complex)

CF_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(H^{\natural{\sf p}},\Xi^{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}})

of finitely generated
\Lambda_{{\bf K}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi}
-modules whose definition depends on a choice
\Xi^{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
.

(2)

For Hamiltonians
H_{1},H_{2}
with both
H_{1}^{\natural{\sf p}}
and
H_{2}^{\natural{\sf p}}
nondegenerate and choices
\Xi_{1}^{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
and
\Xi_{2}^{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
to define their equivariant Floer cochain complex, there is a chain homotopy equivalence

CF_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}(H_{1}^{\natural{\sf p}},\Xi_{1}^{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}})\to CF_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(H_{2}^{\natural{\sf p}},\Xi_{2}^{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}})

called the equivariant continuation map.

(3)

The equivariant continuation maps are well-defined up to chain homotopy. Moreover, the composition of two equivariant continuation maps is homotopic to an equivariant continuation map. Hence there is a well-defined symplectic invariant of
(X,\omega)
, denoted by

HF_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}(X)

called the (Tate)
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-equivariant Hamiltonian Floer cohomology
.

(4)

There is a natural equivariant PSS map

\Phi_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}^{\mathbb{MF}}:H^{*}(X;\Lambda_{\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi})\to HF_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}^{*}(X)

and an equivariant SSP map

\Phi_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}^{\mathbb{FM}}:HF_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}^{*}(X)\to H^{*}(X;\Lambda_{\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi})

which are isomorphisms of
\Lambda_{{\bf K}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi}
-modules.

Remark 1.7
.

The equivariant cohomology
HF_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(X)
is naturally a module over the field
\Lambda_{{\bf K}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi}
. When
{\sf p}=2
, this is the same as the ring
\Lambda_{\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi}
. When
{\sf p}>2
, there is no
\Lambda_{\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi}
-module structure on the complex
CF_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(H^{\natural{\sf p}})
as “multiplying by
\uptheta
” is not a chain map. However, one could define a
\Lambda_{\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi}
-module structure on the cohomology
HF_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(H^{\natural{\sf p}})
. However, we do not consider this additional structure in the current paper.

There is an algebraically defined
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-equivariant chain complex

C_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(CF(H)^{\otimes{\sf p}})

whose underlying cochain group is a suitable completion of

\Big(CF(H)\underset{\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi}}{\otimes}\cdots\underset{\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi}}{\otimes}CF(H)\Big)\otimes\Lambda_{\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi}.

The differential is linear over
\Lambda_{{\bf K}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi}
. The quasi-Frobenius map is an
{\bf F}_{\sf p}
-linear map

qF:HF(H;\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi})\to H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(CF(H)^{\otimes{\sf p}})

which is, roughly speaking, induced by the chain-level map
x\mapsto x\otimes\cdots\otimes x
.

1.2.3.
Floer-theoretic Steenrod operation

Theorem J

(Floer-theoretic Steenrod operation)
.

Let
(X,\omega)
be a compact symplectic manifold and
J
be an
\omega
-compatible almost complex structure.

(1)

For any pair of Hamiltonians
H_{-}
and
H_{+}
with
H_{-}
nondegenerate and
H_{+}^{\natural{\sf p}}
nondegenerate, there exists a cochain map called the
equivariant pair-of-pants product

\widetilde{P}:C_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(CF(H_{-})^{\otimes{\sf p}})\to CF_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}(H_{+}^{\natural{\sf p}})

It is well-defined up to chain homotopy.

(2)

The map on cohomology, denoted by

P:H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(CF(H_{-})^{\otimes{\sf p}})\to HF_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(H_{+}^{\natural{\sf p}})

is an isomorphism of
\Lambda_{{\bf K}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi}
-modules.

(3)

The composition defining the
Floer-theoretic Steenrod operation
is given by

\textstyle{HF(X;\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H(CF(H_{-})\otimes\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{qF}
\textstyle{H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(CF(H_{-})^{\otimes{\sf p}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P}
\textstyle{HF_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(H_{+}^{\natural{\sf p}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{HF_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(X)}

denoted by

{\it FSt}_{{\sf p}}:HF(X;\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi})\to HF_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(X)

is independent of choices.

(4)

The following diagram commutes: when
{\sf p}=2
;

\textstyle{H(X;\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{2}}^{\Pi})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\it QSt}_{{\sf p}}}
\scriptstyle{\Phi^{\mathbb{MF}}}
\textstyle{H(X;\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}{[\![\uptheta]\!]}}^{\Pi})}
\textstyle{HF(X;\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{2}}^{\Pi})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\it FSt}_{2}}
\textstyle{HF{}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}(X;\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{2}{[\![\uptheta]\!]}}^{\Pi})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}^{\mathbb{FM}}}

(1.6)

when
{\sf p}>2
,

\textstyle{H(X;\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\it QSt}_{{\sf p}}}
\scriptstyle{\Phi^{\mathbb{MF}}}
\textstyle{H(X;\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}{[\![\mathtt{U}]\!]}}^{\Pi}\langle\uptheta\rangle)}
\textstyle{HF(X;\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\it FSt}_{{\sf p}}}
\textstyle{HF{}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}(X;\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}{[\![\mathtt{U}]\!]}}^{\Pi}\langle\uptheta\rangle)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}^{\mathbb{FM}}}

(1.7)

1.3.
Main results on quantitative Floer theory

Many successful applications of Hamiltonian Floer theory have a quantitative flavor. We also have the quantitative package under our framework. As many applications take advantage of using different coefficients, we phrase the statements using a general Novikov ring/field.

1.3.1.
Action filtration and spectral invariants

We state the following theorem for the convenience for readers to cite. The proof has no difference from cases proved using other approaches is hence omitted.

Theorem K

(Filtered Floer complex and continuation map)
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring with unit and let
CF(H;\Lambda_{R}^{\Pi})
be a Floer complex provided by Theorem
A
tensored with
\Lambda_{R}^{\Pi}
.

(1)

The differential of the Floer complex preserves the energy filtration. More precisely, for each
\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the differential preserves the subgroups

CF(H;\Lambda_{R}^{\Pi})^{\leq\tau}:=\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}a_{i}p_{i}\in CF(H;\Lambda_{R}^{\Pi})\ |\ {\mathcal{A}}_{H}(p_{i})\leq\tau\Big\}

and

CF(H;\Lambda_{R}^{\Pi})^{<\tau}:=\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}a_{i}p_{i}\in CF(H;\Lambda_{R}^{\Pi})\ |\ {\mathcal{A}}_{H}(p_{i})<\tau\Big\}.

(2)

Let
CF(H_{1};\Lambda_{R}^{\Pi})
and
CF(H_{2};\Lambda_{R}^{\Pi})
be two Floer complexes. Denote

c:=\int_{S^{1}}\sup_{X}(H_{1,t}-H_{2,t})dt.

Then there exists a continuation map
\Phi^{12}(\Theta)
such that for all
\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

\displaystyle\ \Phi^{12}(\Theta)(CF(H_{1};\Lambda_{R}^{\Pi})^{\leq\tau})\subseteq CF(H_{2};\Lambda_{R}^{\Pi})^{\leq\tau+c},\

\displaystyle\ \Phi^{12}(\Theta)(CF(H_{1};\Lambda_{R}^{\Pi})^{<\tau})\subseteq CF(H_{2};\Lambda_{R}^{\Pi})^{<\tau+c}.

The filtered version of continuation map implies that for any nondegenerate Hamiltonian
H
, the filtered homology groups

\displaystyle\ HF(H;\Lambda_{R}^{\Pi})^{\leq\tau},\

\displaystyle\ HF(H;\Lambda_{R}^{\Pi})^{<\tau}

only depend on
H
. When
R
is fixed in the local context, we omit the coefficient from the notations. Moreover, there are well-defined maps

HF(H)^{<\tau}\to HF(X)

compatible with maps (for
\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}
)

HF(H)^{<\tau_{1}}\to HF(H)^{<\tau_{2}}.

This allows us to define the
spectral invariants
of Floer homology classes.

Definition 1.8
.

Given a Hamiltonian
H
, the
spectral invariant
of
x\in HF(X;\Lambda_{R}^{\Pi})
is

c_{H}(x):=\lim_{i\to\infty}\Big\{\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}\ |\ x\in{\rm Im}\big(HF(H_{i})^{<\tau}\to HF(X)\big)\Big\}\in{\mathbb{R}}\cup\{-\infty\}.

Here the limit is taken for a sequence of nondegenerate Hamiltonians
H_{i}
converging to
H
in
C^{0}
-norm.

Theorem L

(Spectral invariants)
.

Fix a Noetherian ring
R
. The spectral numbers
c_{H}(x)
for
x\in HF(X;\Lambda_{R}^{\Pi})
satisfy the following properties.

(1)

(Spectrality)
For any
x\in HF(X;\Lambda_{R}^{\Pi})
, the number

c_{H}(x)=-\infty\Longrightarrow x=0

and for
x\neq 0
, there exists a cycle
{\mathfrak{x}}
representing
x
such that

{\mathcal{A}}_{H}({\mathfrak{x}})=c_{H}(x).

(2)

(Shifting Property)
For
x\in HF(X;\Lambda_{R}^{\Pi})
and
a\in\Lambda_{R}^{\Pi}
, one has

c_{H}(ax)=c_{H}(x)-{\mathfrak{v}}(a).

(3)

(Lipschitz Continuity)

c_{H}(x)
is Lipschitz continuous with respect to the Hofer metric on the space of 1-periodic Hamiltonians.

(4)

(Isotopy Invariance)

c_{H}(x)
only depends on the element
\tilde{\phi}
in
{\rm H}\widetilde{\rm am}(X,\omega)
, the Hamiltonian isotopy induced from
H
as an element of the universal cover of the Hamiltonian diffeomorphism group. Hence we can denote
c_{H}(x)
by
c_{\tilde{\phi}}(x)
.

(5)

(Symplectic Invariance)
For any symplectomorphism
\rho:X\to X
, one has
c_{H\circ\rho}(x)=c_{H}(\rho_{*}(x))
where
\rho_{*}
is the automorphism

\rho_{*}:HF(X)\to HF(X)

induced by
\rho
.

(6)

(Triangle Inequality)
For any
x,y\in HF(X)
,
\tilde{\phi},\tilde{\psi}\in{\rm H}\widetilde{\rm am}(X,\omega)
, one has

c_{\tilde{\phi}\tilde{\psi}}(x\star y)\leq c_{\tilde{\phi}}(x)+c_{\tilde{\psi}}(y).

Here
\star
is the pair-of-pants product on Floer homology defined by Theorem
C
.

(7)

(Poincaré Duality)
Let
R={\bf K}
be a field. Let
\Phi_{0}^{\rm PD}
be the composition

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 61.81519pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-61.81519pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{HF(X;\Lambda_{\bf K}^{\Pi})\otimes HF(X;\Lambda_{\bf K}^{\Pi})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 62.95053pt\raise 6.20831pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.20831pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\Phi^{\rm PD}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 85.81519pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 85.81519pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Lambda_{\bf K}^{\Pi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 130.28323pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 130.28323pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\bf K}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Let
\overline{H}
be the reversal of the Hamiltonian
H
. Then one has

c_{H}(x)=-\inf\Big\{c_{\overline{H}}(y)\ |\ y\in HF(X;\Lambda_{\bf K}^{\Pi}),\ \Phi_{0}^{\rm PD}(x,y)\neq 0\Big\}.

1.3.2.
Barcodes

Another quantitative structure widely used in applications is the barcode. We follow the approach of Usher–Zhang
[
69
]
which requires slightly modified setup.

For a symplectic manifold
X
with the abelian group
\Pi
defined by (
1.1
), a (concise)
barcode
(see
[
69
]
) is a finite multiset of elements of
({\mathbb{R}}/\omega(\Pi))\times(0,+\infty]
.

It is most useful when considering Floer barcodes with field coefficients. Then the complex

CF(H;\Lambda_{\bf K}^{\Pi}):=CF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Pi})\otimes\Lambda_{\bf K}^{\Pi}

is a (slight generalization of) Floer-type complex in the sense of
[
69
, Definition 4.1]
. It follows from
[
69
, Definition 6.3, Theorem 7.1]
that there is a barcode canonically associated to the complex
CF(H;\Lambda_{\bf K}^{\Pi})
.

Corollary 1.9

(Floer barcodes)
.

The barcode associated to the Floer complex
CF(H;\Lambda_{{\bf K}}^{\Pi})
only depends on the Hamiltonian
H
. Moreover, the dependence on
H
is Lipschitz with respect to the bottleneck distance on barcodes and the Hofer metric on the space of
1
-periodic Hamiltonians.

Proof.

It follows from applying Proposition
7.38
and Theorem
7.40
to the filtered Floer complex constructed in Theorem
K
.
∎

1.3.3.
Quantitative features of equivariant Floer theory

The equivariant Floer cochain complex also has an energy filtration. Notice that the equivariant cochain complex
CF_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(H^{\natural{\sf p}})
is a module over
\Lambda_{{\bf K}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi}
. We can prove that it is a Floer-type complex. On the other hand, the ordinary Floer complex
CF(H;\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi})
also induces an equivariant complex coming from its own
{\sf p}
-fold tensor power, denoted by

C_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(CF(H)^{\otimes{\sf p}})

which is also a Floer-type complex.

Theorem M

(Barcode stretching under prime iteration)
.

Let
X
be a compact symplectic manifold,
{\sf p}
be a prime, and
H
be a Hamiltonian on
X
such that the
{\sf p}
-th iteration
H^{\natural{\sf p}}
is nondegenerate.

(1)

The equivariant Floer cochain complex
CF_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(H^{\natural{\sf p}})
is a Floer-type complex over the Novikov field
\Lambda_{{\bf K}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi}
.

(2)

The equivariant pair-of-pants product has a chain-level realization

P:C_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(CF(H)^{\otimes{\sf p}})\to CF_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(H^{\natural{\sf p}})

which is a filtered chain homotopy equivalence for Floer-type complexes.

(3)

Suppose the bar-length spectrum of
CF(H)
is
0<\beta_{1}(H)\leq\cdots\leq\beta_{m}(H)
. Then the bar-length spectrum of
CF_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(H^{\natural{\sf p}})
is

\beta_{i,{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}(H^{\natural 2})=2\beta_{i}(H),\ i=1,\ldots,m

when
{\sf p}=2
and

\beta_{2i-1,{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(H^{\natural{\sf p}})=\beta_{2i,{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(H^{\natural{\sf p}})={\sf p}\beta_{i}(H),\ i=1,\ldots,m

when
{\sf p}>2
.

1.3.4.
Quantitative Hamiltonian Floer theory for noncontractible orbits

We state the main quantitative results about Floer theory for noncontractible orbits. We follow very much the treatment of Sugimoto
[
67
]
. Since the resulting homology group always vanishes, there are no spectral invariants; hence the barcodes are the main quantitative aspects here.

Choose a possibly noncontractible loop
\gamma:S^{1}\to X
which represents a nonzero class

[\gamma]\neq 0\in H_{1}(X;{\mathbb{Z}})_{\rm free}.

One can consider noncontractible periodic orbits of a Hamiltonian
H
which are homologous (over
{\mathbb{Z}}
) to
\gamma
.

Definition 1.10
.

Let
H
be a 1-periodic Hamiltonian on
(X,\omega)
.

(1)

Let
x:S^{1}\to X
be a 1-periodic orbit of
H
. A
\gamma
-capping
of
x
is an oriented cobordism
u:\Sigma\to X
from
\gamma
to
x
(where
\Sigma
could be a higher genus surface).

(2)

Two
\gamma
-cappings are equivalent if their difference, which is a class in
H_{2}(X;{\mathbb{Z}})
, has zero symplectic area. A
\gamma
-capped orbit
of
H
is a 1-periodic orbit together with an equivalence class of
\gamma
-cappings.

(3)

Let

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{\gamma}(H)

denote the set of
\gamma
-capped orbits of
H
, on which there is a free action by the group

\Gamma:=H_{2}(X;{\mathbb{Z}})/{\rm ker}(\omega).

In our setup we do not keep the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading but only consider the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-grading on
\gamma
-capped orbits. Each
\gamma
-capped orbit
p
has a well-defined symplectic action
{\mathcal{A}}_{H,\gamma}(p)\in{\mathbb{R}}
. The construction of the filtered Floer complex is summarized below and will be proved in Section
28
.

Theorem 1.11

(Filtered Floer chain complex for noncontractible periodic orbits)
.

Let
(X,\omega)
be a compact symplectic manifold and let
\gamma:S^{1}\to X
be a smooth loop with
[\gamma]\neq 0\in H_{1}(X;{\mathbb{Z}})_{\rm free}
. Let
H
be a nondegenerate
1
-periodic Hamiltonian. Then for each
1
-periodic family of
\omega
-compatible almost complex structures
J
, there is a differential on the
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded abelian group

CF_{\gamma}(H):=\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}a_{i}p_{i}\ |\ a_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}},\ p_{i}\in\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{\gamma}(H),\ \lim_{i\to\infty}{\mathcal{A}}_{H,\gamma}(p_{i})=-\infty\Big\}

leading to a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded complex of abelian groups

CF_{\gamma}(H,J,\Xi).

Moreover, for each field
{\bf K}
, the tensor product

CF_{\gamma}(H)\otimes\Lambda_{\bf K}^{\Gamma}

is a Floer-type complex over
\Lambda_{\bf K}^{\Gamma}
whose filtered isomorphism class only depends on
H
.

Under the assumption that
[\gamma]\neq 0\in H_{1}(X)
, we expect that a continuation map argument can prove the vanishing of the resulting homology group. The main use of the noncontractible Floer chain complex is on the quantitative side. The main purpose of developing this theory is to generalize the work of Sugimoto
[
67
]
, which uses the equivariant pair-of-pants product as a primary tool. We state the main result regarding the quantitative implications, which is also proved in Section
28
.

Theorem 1.12
.

Under the assumption of Theorem
1.11
, let
{\sf p}
be a prime and suppose
H^{\natural{\sf p}}
is nondegenerate.

(1)

There exists a Floer-type complex

CF_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(H^{\natural{\sf p}},\gamma)

over
\Lambda_{{\bf K}_{\sf p}}^{\Gamma}
which is freely generated by
{\sf p}
-periodic orbits homologous to
\gamma
. Its filtered isomorphism class only depends on
H
.

(2)

There exists a filtered homotopy equivalence

P_{\gamma}:C_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(CF_{\gamma}(H)^{\otimes{\sf p}})\to CF_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(H^{\natural{\sf p}},\gamma^{({\sf p})})

of Floer-type complexes over
\Lambda_{{\bf K}_{\sf p}}^{\Gamma}
.

(3)

Let

\beta_{1}^{\gamma}(H)\leq\cdots\leq\beta_{N}^{\gamma}(H)

be the bar-length spectrum of
CF_{\gamma}(H)\otimes\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}^{\Gamma}
. Then it only depends on
H
and the homology class of
\gamma
. Moreover, the bar-length spectrum of
CF_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}},\gamma^{({\sf p})}}(H^{\natural{\sf p}})
is

2\beta_{1}^{\gamma}(H),\ldots,2\beta_{N}^{\gamma}(H)

when
{\sf p}=2
(with the same number of finite bars) and is

{\sf p}\beta_{1}^{\gamma}(H),{\sf p}\beta_{1}^{\gamma}(H),\ldots,{\sf p}\beta_{N}^{\gamma}(H),{\sf p}\beta_{N}^{\gamma}(H)

when
{\sf p}>2
(with twice the number of finite bars).

1.4.
What we do “virtually”

We would like to explain the general principle of our construction (on the virtual side) along with some historical remarks.

In a very general sense, the Floer complexes and other chain-level structures are constructed via counting solutions to the Floer equation. To count beyond the cases where transversality can be achieved by geometric perturbations (
[
24
]
[
35
]
[
50
]
), the so-called virtual technique was invented (see
[
42
]
[
30
]
[
27
]
[
36
]
[
53
]
etc.). Such constructions, depending on the extent of applications, usually require building a complete package of abstract theory (such as Kuranishi theory and polyfold theory); the application of these abstract theories can typically be broken down into two main components: (virtual) regularization and (virtual) perturbation/integration.

From now on we only discuss under the finite-dimensional/Kuranishi approach, the one upon which the current paper is based, rather than the infinite-dimensional/polyfold approach. The issues and challenges we will address in the finite-dimensional approach may not be manifested in the infinite-dimensional situation.

A central issue in the finite-dimensional construction is the choice-dependence. Unlike the algebro-geometric situation, one rarely obtains canonical objects such as a perfect obstruction theory (
[
15
]
). To obtain choice-independent results, one needs to include various kinds of equivalence relations in the abstract package, or perhaps higher categorical structures. The choice-independence is not yet a significant challenge in cases where one deals with a single or finitely many moduli spaces, such as Gromov–Witten theory. But it causes much more serious difficulties in Floer-type theories. In Floer theory, essentially one obtains virtual fundamental chains rather than cycles; the virtual fundamental chains satisfy expected properties (such as
d^{2}=0
) only if we make the constructions for infinitely many moduli spaces compatibly. It could be a delicate issue when developing the abstract package incorporating such compatibility conditions and when fulfilling these conditions in the concrete regularization procedure.
5
5

5

The work of Abouzaid
[
5
]
takes a different approach where the choices are made universally in his notion of “theories of virtual counts.”

We would like to emphasize two important developments which precede the current piece of work. The first one, regarding the perturbation component, starts from
[
29
]
which proposed the principle leading to integer-valued curve counts even when the orbifold locus of a moduli space is obstructed. After technical exploration by Parker
[
54
]
, the authors rigorously established this new perturbation method in
[
13
]
, which we call the FOP perturbation scheme. The second one, regarding the regularization component, is the construction of global Kuranishi charts starting from
[
3
]
. It provides a powerful method producing global Kuranishi models regularizing any single moduli space and greatly reduces the technicality when discussing the compatibility among local Kuranishi charts. In
[
14
]
we produced the global Kuranishi chart construction for Floer complexes and PSS maps, which is being extended to other chain-level structures in the current paper (with certain upgrades coming from
[
4
]
and
[
34
]
).

Now we summarize the technical framework underlying our implementation of the virtual technique. Compared to the regularization component, the perturbation component is to simply apply a blackbox theorem (Theorem
N
) which was already established in
[
13
]
. However, the delicate prerequisite for the blackbox theorem, along with other requirements, demands careful fine-tunings of the regularization component. Since each stage produces different kinds of objects whose compatibility conditions all look similar to flow category structure, each stage-wise construction is phrased as producing a flow category enriched in a particular “regular stratification category.” The objects of such a category are stratified in a way similar to manifolds with corners, with a monoidal structure corresponding to (direct) product and disjoint union.

To be more concrete, the whole procedure we are describing can be summarized in the following zig-zag of functors

\hbox to0.0pt{$\overbrace{\phantom{\underline{\smash{\bf Top}}\leftarrow\underline{\smash{\bf Kur}}_{\rm finite}^{\rm NC}\rightarrow\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}^{\rm NC}}}^{{\rm regularization}}$\hss}\underline{\smash{\bf Top}}\leftarrow\underline{\smash{\bf SKur}}^{\rm NC}\rightarrow\underbrace{\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}^{\rm NC}\leftarrow\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}^{\rm FOP}\rightarrow\underline{\smash{{\bf\Psi}{\bf Man}}}}_{\rm perturbation}.

The items are all regular stratification categories where

•

\underline{\smash{\bf Top}}
is the category of stratified topological spaces;

•

\underline{\smash{\bf SKur}}^{\rm NC}
is the category of
normally complex smooth Kuranishi charts
;

•

\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}^{{\rm NC}}
is the category of
normally complex derived orbifolds
;

•

\underline{\smash{{\bf\Psi}{\bf Man}}}
is the category of
pseudomanifolds
.

In the case of Floer complex, the geometric data of Floer theory (the Hamiltonian and the almost complex structure) provides a flow category enriched in
\underline{\smash{\bf Top}}
. The regularization component can be summarized as constructing a “lift” of this flow category to
\underline{\smash{\bf SKur}}^{\rm NC}
. This step is basically accomplished by repackaging the
AMS construction

[
3
,
4
]
(where the construction of the normal complex structure also borrows ideas from
[
2
]
), which contains several delicate steps. Once this lift is constructed, it automatically descends to a flow category enriched in
\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}^{\rm NC}
, where the blackbox theorem on FOP perturbations can be directly applied. After the FOP perturbation, the flow category becomes enriched in
\underline{\smash{{\bf\Psi}{\bf Man}}}
, which automatically produces a chain complex over integers.

To obtain chain-level structures such as continuation maps, pair-of-pants products etc., we also consider two “higher” analogues of flow categories, i.e., flow multimodules and flow homotopies. Typically, flow categories induce chain complexes, multimodules induce chain maps, and flow homotopies induce chain homotopies. These chain-level structures are also obtained from compatibly implementing the AMS+FOP procedure, namely, producing multimodules and homotopies enriched in categories appearing in the above zig-zag diagram. The differences from the flow category cases only lie in various sub-technical levels.

1.4.1.
Other AMS-type regularization schemes

There have been several related works which carry out AMS-type constructions in Floer theory, which aim at constructing not only a single global chart construction, but a compatible system for all involved moduli spaces. These works include
[
57
]
,
[
33
]
,
[
17
]
,
[
44
]
, and
[
56
]
, as well as our previous work
[
14
]
.

One distinct feature of our work is the formal description of the compatibility condition using the notion of “lifts” of flow categories to various “regular stratification categories.” Having realized that each step of the AMS construction requires a certain compatibility for the choices made for individual moduli spaces (such as the thickening data, stable smoothing, normal complex structure etc.), the formal notions of regular stratification categories and lifts are more convenient and rigorous, although one needs to carefully define each regular stratification category.

The compatibility condition we require here is also most refined since we need to construct genuine perturbations (essentially the same level of refinedness should be established in
[
57
]
and
[
56
]
, both of which use the FOP perturbation method developed by us). The specific requirement is contained in the definition of various regular stratification categories, most notably
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf SKur}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm NC}
, the category of
collared rigidified normally complex smooth Kuranishi spaces
. Here the collaredness and rigidifiedness are what make the inductive perturbation possible, from low-energy moduli spaces to high-energy ones. Some other works such as
[
33
]
and
[
17
]
where genuine perturbations are not needed, the requirement of compatibility can be relaxed. On the other hand, the work
[
44
]
, which also relies on constructing compatible (multi-valued) perturbations, uses a different strategy to interpolate not-so-compatible choices over the collar regions.

1.5.
Structure of the paper

This paper is divided into four main parts. In Part 1, we introduce a series of formal notions, including various categories such as those of pseudomanifolds with corners, Kuranishi spaces, derived orbifolds, and their variants; the notions of flow categories, multimodules, and homotopies; the abstract construction of chain-level objects; and the blackbox of FOP perturbation method. In Part 2, we introduce flow categories, multimodules, and homotopies appearing in (non-equivariant) Floer theory, state the main technical theorems about AMS regularization, and prove most of the main results stated in the introduction. In Part 3, we provide the details of the AMS construction asserted in Part 2. In Part 4, we modify all the previous constructions in the equivariant setting and establish results related to the quantum Steenrod operations.

1.6.
Acknowledgements

We thank Egor Shelukhin and Nicolas Wilkins for related discussions involving our collaboration
[
12
]
. We thank Zihong Chen, Helmut Hofer, Rohil Prasad, Sobhan Seyfaddini for their interest in our work and encouragement.

Part I
FORMAL NOTIONS

Outline of Part 1

We introduce the abstract framework underlying the formulation of Floer theory in various levels. Several concepts related to Floer theory and the virtual construction are described in more abstract ways, including the abstract notion of flow categories/multimodules/homotopies/concatenations enriched in so-called regular stratification categories and their lifts to different categories. Concrete examples of such regular stratification categories will appear in the geometric construction, including Kuranishi spaces, derived orbifolds, pseudomanifolds, etc. We also formulate the abstract mechanism for deriving chain-level structures from flow categories/multimodules/homotopies. Lastly, we provide the FOP perturbation method as a blackbox theorem which can be directly applied.

2.
Regular Stratification Categories

2.1.
Regular posets

The purpose of this subsection is to introduce notions associated with partially ordered sets which will be used to define the index sets of stratifications of various moduli spaces coming from geometric constructions.

2.1.1.
Partially ordered sets

The term “partially ordered set” is abbreviated as
poset
, which is always assumed to be countable. The partial order relations are denoted by
\leq
,
\preceq
, etc. A poset map is a map of the underlying sets which respects the partial orders.

For a poset
A
, let
A^{\max}=A^{\rm top}\subseteq A
be the subset of maximal elements. A poset
A
has a canonical
Alexandrov topology
: a subset
U\subseteq A
is Alexandrov open if
\alpha\in U
and
\alpha\leq\beta
imply that
\beta\in U
. By definition, poset maps are continuous under the Alexandrov topology. A special class of closed subset indexed by
\alpha\in A

\partial^{\alpha}A=\{\beta\in A\ |\ \beta\leq\alpha\}

is used frequently in this paper.

Remark 2.1
.

Our convention is opposite from that of Abouzaid
[
5
]
, where he regards top strata as minimal elements of the labelling poset.

The product of finitely many posets carries a canonically induced partial order from the lexicographic order. Indeed, if
A_{1},\ldots,A_{k}
are posets, then the relation

(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k})\leq(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k})\ \text{if and only if}\ \alpha_{i}\leq\beta_{i}\ \forall i=1,\ldots,k,

is a partial order on
A_{1}\times\cdots\times A_{k}
.

Notation 2.2
.

Let
S
be a countable subset. Let
A^{(S)}
denote the set of all finite subsets of
S
whose partial order is
\alpha\leq\beta\Longrightarrow\beta\subseteq\alpha
. So its maximal element is the empty set.

Now we introduce the kind of posets which can model manifolds with corners.

Definition 2.3
.

(Homogeneous posets)

(1)

A poset
A
is called
homogeneous
if for each
\alpha\in A
, the length of a maximal sequence of elements
\alpha=\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{1}<\cdots<\alpha_{k}
with
\alpha_{k}\in A^{\max}
, is finite and only depends on
\alpha
(but not the maximal string and
\alpha_{k}
). This length is called the
depth
or
codimension
of
\alpha
, denoted by
{\rm depth}(\alpha)
. In particular,

\alpha\in A^{\max}\Longleftrightarrow{\rm depth}(\alpha)=0.

(2)

A poset map between homogeneous posets is called
homogeneous of degree
k
if the map increases the depth function by
k\geq 0
.

(3)

Given a homogeneous poset
A
, define

\mathbb{Face}^{\gamma}:=\{\beta<\gamma\ |\ {\rm depth}(\beta)={\rm depth}(\gamma)+1\}

and

\mathbb{Face}_{\alpha}^{\gamma}:=\{\beta\in\mathbb{Face}^{\gamma}\ |\ \alpha\leq\beta<\gamma\}.

Definition 2.4
.

A homogeneous poset
A
is called
regular
if every
\gamma\in A
satisfies the following conditions.

(1)

For any
\alpha\leq\gamma
,
\mathbb{Face}_{\alpha}^{\gamma}
is always a finite set (but not necessarily
\mathbb{Face}^{\gamma}
).

(2)

The natural map to the set of subsets of
\mathbb{Face}^{\gamma}

f_{\gamma}:\partial^{\gamma}A\to A^{(\mathbb{Face}^{\gamma})},\ \alpha\mapsto\mathbb{Face}_{\alpha}^{\gamma}

is injective.

(3)

For any finite subset
S\subseteq\mathbb{Face}^{\gamma}

\bigcap_{\beta\in S}\partial^{\beta}A=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\emptyset,&S\notin{\rm Im}f_{\gamma},\\
\partial^{\alpha}A,&S=f_{\gamma}(\alpha)\end{array}\right.

We often need to identify the faces that are only adjacent to one top stratum.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
A
be a regular poset.

(1)

A
true boundary stratum
of
A
is an element
\beta\in A
of depth
1
which has only one top stratum above. The
true boundary
of
A
is the Alexandrov closure of the set of true boundary strata.

(2)

A regular poset is called
normal
if each depth-one element is either adjacent to one top stratum (true boundary) or adjacent to two top strata (fake boundary).

Remark 2.6
.

The poset
A^{(S)}
in Notation
2.2
is regular with depth function given by
{\rm depth}(\alpha)=\#\alpha
. On the other hand, the poset of three elements
\alpha<\beta<\gamma
is homogeneous but not regular.

We prove several important properties of regular posets.

Lemma 2.7

(Properties of regular posets)
.

Let
A
be a regular poset. Then the following is true.

(1)

Any Alexandrov closed subset or Alexandrov open subset is also regular.

(2)

For
\alpha\leq\gamma
, the natural map

f_{\gamma\alpha}:\{\beta\in A\ |\alpha\leq\beta\leq\gamma\}\to A^{(\mathbb{Face}_{\alpha}^{\gamma})},\ \beta\mapsto\mathbb{Face}_{\beta}^{\gamma}

(2.1)

is a homogeneous bijection.

(3)

For any
\alpha\leq\gamma
,

{\rm depth}(\alpha)={\rm depth}(\gamma)+\#\mathbb{Face}_{\alpha}^{\gamma}.

(4)

When
\alpha\leq\beta\leq\gamma
, there is a canonical bijection

\mathbb{Face}_{\alpha}^{\gamma}\setminus\mathbb{Face}_{\beta}^{\gamma}\cong\mathbb{Face}_{\alpha}^{\beta}.

Moreover, when
\alpha\leq\beta\leq\gamma\leq\delta
, the composition
\mathbb{Face}_{\alpha}^{\beta}\to\mathbb{Face}_{\alpha}^{\gamma}\to\mathbb{Face}_{\alpha}^{\delta}
coincides with the inclusion
\mathbb{Face}_{\alpha}^{\beta}\to\mathbb{Face}_{\alpha}^{\delta}
defined from the above bijection.

(5)

If
i:A\to A^{\prime}
is a homogeneous poset injection to another regular poset
A^{\prime}
, then for any
\alpha\leq\gamma
in
A
, the induced map
\mathbb{Face}_{\alpha}^{\gamma}\to\mathbb{Face}_{i(\alpha)}^{i(\gamma)}
is a bijection.

Proof.

(1) follows from the definition of the Alexandrov topology and the definition of regular posets. For (2), we show that the
f_{\gamma\alpha}
is bijective. By the definition of regular posets, this is an injection. For surjectivity, assume on the contrary that
S\subseteq\mathbb{Face}_{\alpha}^{\gamma}
is not in the range of
f_{\gamma\alpha}
. Then it is not in the range of
f_{\gamma}
either. By the definition of regular posets, we must have

\bigcap_{\beta\in S}\partial^{\beta}A=\emptyset.

However, the above intersection at least contains
\alpha
, a contradiction. Hence
f_{\gamma\alpha}
is surjective. Moreover, it is clear that
f_{\gamma\alpha}
is a poset map. One can verify that
f_{\gamma\alpha}
is homogeneous by an easy inductive argument. Then (3) follows from the homogeneity condition.

For (4), for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{Face}_{\alpha}^{\gamma}\setminus\mathbb{Face}_{\beta}^{\gamma}
, consider

\partial^{\lambda}A\cap\partial^{\beta}A=\partial^{\lambda}A\cap\bigcap_{\tau\in\mathbb{Face}_{\beta}^{\gamma}}\partial^{\tau}A.

Then the subset
\{\lambda\}\cup\mathbb{Face}_{\beta}^{\gamma}\subseteq\mathbb{Face}_{\alpha}^{\gamma}
, under the inverse of
f_{\gamma\alpha}
, gives an element
\kappa
between
\alpha
and
\beta
whose depth is one bigger than that of
\beta
, hence defines a map from
\mathbb{Face}_{\alpha}^{\gamma}\setminus\mathbb{Face}_{\beta}^{\gamma}
to
\mathbb{Face}_{\alpha}^{\beta}
. One can verify that this is a bijection.

For (5), the homogeneity condition implies that
i
induces an injection from
\mathbb{Face}_{\alpha}^{\gamma}
to
\mathbb{Face}_{i(\alpha)}^{i(\gamma)}
. Then by (3), we have

\#\mathbb{Face}_{\alpha}^{\gamma}={\rm depth}(\gamma)-{\rm depth}(\alpha)={\rm depth}(i(\gamma))-{\rm depth}(i(\alpha))=\#\mathbb{Face}_{i(\alpha)}^{i(\gamma)},

which shows that the induced map is a bijection.
∎

It is straightforward to check that regular posets admit finite products (Cartesian product) and finite coproducts (disjoint union).

Definition 2.8
.

The category of regular posets, denoted by
\underline{\smash{\bf RegPos}}
, has objects being countable regular posets and morphisms being injective homogeneous poset maps.

Proposition 2.9
.

\underline{\smash{\bf RegPos}}
is a distributive monoidal category with respect to the direct product and disjoint union, whose units are posets with a single element.

Proof.

This is straightforward from checking the definitions.
∎

2.2.
Regular stratification categories and presentations

Definition 2.10
.

A
regular stratification category
is a distributive monoidal category
\underline{\smash{\bf C}}
together with a distributive monoidal functor
\underline{\smash{\bf C}}\to\underline{\smash{\bf RegPos}}
, called the
forgetful functor
, satisfying the following properties. In the following, we say an object
K
of
\underline{\smash{\bf C}}
an
A
-stratified object if the forgetful functor sends
K
to
A
.

(1)

There is an initial object which is
\emptyset
-stratified.

(2)

For any morphism
f:A\to B
in
\underline{\smash{\bf RegPos}}
and any
B
-stratified object
K
, there is a canonical “pullback,” which is an
A
-stratified object
f^{*}K
, together with a canonical morphism
\tilde{f}:f^{*}K\to K
. The pullback needs to satisfy the functoriality condition. Namely, the composition of pullbacks is the pullback by the composition.

In particular, for any
A
-stratified object
K
and
\alpha\in A
there is a
restriction

\partial^{\alpha}K
for each
\alpha\in A
.

As we will see below, there are a few examples of regular stratification categories such as those of regularly stratified topological spaces (with morphisms being embeddings), regularly stratified topological/smooth manifolds, regularly stratified Kuranishi charts, and Kuranishi presentations. On the other hand, if
\underline{\smash{\bf C}}
is any category, then the product
\underline{\smash{\bf RegPos}}\times\underline{\smash{\bf C}}
is a regular stratification category with respect to the obvious forgetful functor.

2.3.
Stratified spaces, manifolds, and pseudomanifolds

2.3.1.
Regularly stratified spaces

Although we can define stratified spaces in general, in this paper all such spaces have strata indexed by regular posets. Therefore, we restrict to this situation. We will specify a regular stratification category
\underline{\smash{\bf Top}}
of regularly stratified topological spaces.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
A
be a countable regular poset. An
A
-stratified space
(
A
-space for short) is a locally compact, Hausdorff and second countable topological space
X
endowed with a map

s:X\to A

which is continuous with respect to the Alexandrov topology on
A
whose range is finite.

In particular, we can write

X=\bigsqcup_{\alpha\in A}X_{\alpha},\ {\rm where}\ X_{\alpha}:=s^{-1}(\alpha),

which satisfies the following conditions.

(1)

Each
X_{\alpha}
(called a
stratum
) is locally closed.

(2)

All but finitely many strata are empty.

(3)

For each
\alpha\in A
, the subset

\partial^{\alpha}X:=\bigsqcup_{\beta\leq\alpha}X_{\beta}

is a closed set (which may contain the closure of
X_{\alpha}
properly
6
6

6

This condition is weaker than the “axiom of frontier:”
X_{\alpha}\cap\overline{X_{\beta}}\neq\emptyset\Longrightarrow X_{\alpha}\subseteq\overline{X_{\beta}}
, which is part of the usual definition of a stratification.
). Note that this condition follows from the continuity of
s
.

Remark 2.12
.

An open subset of an
A
-stratified space is still regarded as
A
-stratified, although the subset may have more empty strata.

We introduce the following notions for stratified spaces.

Definition 2.13
.

(Category of regularly stratified spaces)

(1)

A map from an
A_{1}
-space
X_{1}
to an
A_{2}
-space
X_{2}
is a commutative diagram

\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{s_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{2}}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{A_{2}}

where
i:A_{1}\to A_{2}
is a morphism in
\underline{\smash{\bf RegPos}}
and
f
is a continuous map. We usually call such a map a
stratified map
to emphasize that it respects the stratifications.

(2)

A stratified map
f:X_{1}\to X_{2}
as above is called an
embedding
if
f
is a homeomorphism onto its image.
f
is called an
open embedding
if it is an embedding with an open image; it is called a homeomorphism if
f
is a homeomorphism of topological spaces and the underlying poset map is an isomorphism.

(3)

The category
\underline{\smash{\bf Top}}
is the category of topological spaces stratified by regular posets whose morphisms are stratified embeddings.

(4)

Let
G
be a topological group. A
G
-action on an
A
-space
X
is a continuous
G
-action on
X
which preserves each stratum. In this case, the
\partial^{\alpha}A
-space
\partial^{\alpha}X
has an induced
G
-action.

2.3.2.
Notations for corners

In order to define stratified pseudomanifolds, we need to fix the notations for corners. Let
\epsilon>0
be a positive real number. The model for collars is the space
[0,\epsilon)^{k}
which is stratified by subsets of
\{1,\ldots,k\}
. More generally, for any finite set
S
, consider

[0,\epsilon)^{S}=\{(x_{a})_{a\in S}\ |\ x_{a}\in[0,\epsilon)\}

which is stratified by
A^{(S)}
.

Definition 2.14
.

Let
A
be a countable regular poset.

(1)

An
A
-space
X
is said to
have corners
if for each
x\in X_{\alpha}
and each maximal
\gamma\in A^{\max}
with
\alpha\leq\gamma
, there exists a
corner chart
, i.e., a stratified open embedding onto an open neighborhood of
x
in
X

\textstyle{U_{x,\alpha}\times[0,\epsilon)^{\mathbb{Face}_{\alpha}^{\gamma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{\partial^{\gamma}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\{\alpha\}\times A^{(\mathbb{Face}_{\alpha}^{\gamma})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\partial^{\gamma}A}

where
U_{x,\alpha}
is an open neighborhood of
x
in
X_{\alpha}
(which is stratified by
\{\alpha\}
). Here the underlying poset map, whose image is
\{\beta\in A\ |\ \alpha\leq\beta\leq\gamma\}\subseteq\partial^{\gamma}A
, is the inverse of the isomorphism (
2.1
).

(2)

An
A
-stratified manifold
is an
A
-stratified space
X
which has corners such that each
X_{\alpha}
(the “interior” of
\partial^{\alpha}X
) is a topological manifold.
7
7

7

It follows from this condition that each
\partial^{\alpha}X
is a topological manifold with boundary whose interior is
X_{\alpha}
.
A
smooth structure
on an
A
-stratified manifold
X
consists of smooth structures on all
X_{\alpha}
and a maximal atlas of corner charts which are smoothly compatible.

Then we can define the notion of smooth functions, tensors, vector bundles, Lie group actions, on smooth
A
-manifolds. We omit the details. Moreover, we obtain a regular stratification category
\underline{\smash{\bf Man}}
of topological manifolds and a regular stratification category
\underline{\smash{\bf SMan}}
of smooth manifolds.

2.3.3.
Stratified pseudomanifolds

Definition 2.15
.

An
n
-dimensional
pseudomanifold
is a topological space
M
with a partition
M={\rm Int}M\sqcup\delta M
satisfying

(1)

{\rm Int}M
is open, dense, and itself an
n
-dimensional topological manifold.

(2)

\delta M
admits a partition into topological manifolds of dimensions at most
n-2
.

Definition 2.16
.

Let
A
be a countable regular poset. An
A
-stratified
n
-dimensional pseudomanifold
is an
A
-stratified space
X
together with partitions
X_{\alpha}={\rm Int}X_{\alpha}\sqcup\delta X_{\alpha}
such that

(1)

For each
\alpha\in A
,
X_{\alpha}={\rm Int}X_{\alpha}\sqcup\delta X_{\alpha}
is a pseudomanifold of dimension
n-{\rm depth}(\alpha)
.

(2)

For
x\in X_{\alpha}
and each maximal
\gamma\in A^{\rm max}
with
\alpha\leq\gamma
, there exists a corner chart

U_{x,\alpha}\times[0,\epsilon)^{\mathbb{Face}_{\alpha}^{\gamma}}\to\partial^{\gamma}X

which sends
{\rm Int}U_{x,\alpha}\times[0,\epsilon)^{\mathbb{Face}_{\alpha}^{\gamma}}
resp.
\delta U_{x,\alpha}\times[0,\epsilon)^{\mathbb{Face}_{\alpha}^{\gamma}}
into
{\rm Int}\partial^{\gamma}X
resp.
\delta(\partial^{\gamma}X)
.

Notice that a stratified zero-dimensional pseudomanifold is a discrete collection of points and a stratified 1-dimensional pseudomanifold is a disjoint union of 1-manifolds (with boundary).

Definition 2.17
.

An embedding of stratified pseudomanifolds from
X_{1}
to
X_{2}
is an embedding of stratified spaces
f:X_{1}\to X_{2}
such that
f({\rm Int}X_{1})\subseteq{\rm Int}(X_{2})
and
f(\delta X_{1})\subseteq\delta X_{2}
. The category of stratified pseudomanifolds, denoted by
\underline{\smash{{\bf\Psi}{\bf Man}}}
, has objects being stratified pseudomanifolds and morphisms being embeddings.

One can check that
\underline{\smash{{\bf\Psi}{\bf Man}}}
is a regular stratification category (Definition
2.10
).

3.
Kuranishi Spaces

The Kuranishi theory for moduli spaces of pseudoholomorphic curves originated from the efforts of extending the definition of symplectic Gromov–Witten invariants and Hamiltonian Floer homology to full generality. It is based on the fundamental fact that the Cauchy–Riemann operator is elliptic. Fukaya–Ono
[
30
]
firstly introduced the package of Kuranishi structures, alongside another finite-dimensional reduction approach of Li–Tian
[
42
]
(see also
[
66
]

[
58
]
). While the method of Kuranishi structures was further developed by Fukaya–Oh–Ohta–Ono (see the comprehensive treatment in
[
27
]
) together with closely related works by McDuff–Wehrheim
[
49
,
47
,
48
]
, there are alternate approaches with considerable novelties, including the algebraic version of Pardon
[
53
]
as well as the more recent “global chart construction” of Abouzaid–McLean–Smith
[
3
]
(followed up by
[
34
]
and
[
4
]
).

The AMS construction was adopted by the authors in
[
13
]
in the Gromov–Witten setting and
[
14
]
in the Hamiltonian Floer setting.

We use
\underline{\smash{\bf Kur}}
,
\underline{\smash{\bf SKur}}
, and
\underline{\smash{\bf SKur}}^{\rm NC}
to denote the category of Kuranishi charts in topological, smooth, or stably complex categories. When the discussion can be unified in the three cases, we simply use
\underline{\smash{\bf Kur}}
. See below for the detailed definitions.

3.1.
Kuranishi spaces

Definition 3.1
.

Let
A
be a countable regular poset.

(1)

An
A
-stratified
(topological) Kuranishi space
(K-space for short) is a quadruple
K=(G,V,E,S)
where
G
is a compact Lie group,
V
is an
A
-manifold with a continuous
G
-action,
E\to V
is a
G
-equivariant vector bundle, and
S:V\to E
is a
G
-equivariant section, such that the
G
-action on
V
has only finite stabilizers.

(2)

A Kuranishi space
K=(G,V,E,S)
is said to be
smooth
if
V
is a smooth
A
-manifold, the
G
-action is smooth, and
E\to V
is a smooth equivariant vector bundle (we do not impose any smoothness condition on
S
).

3.1.1.
Morphisms of Kuranishi spaces

In order to define a regular stratification category (see Definition
2.10
), we need to specify morphisms. We first define a general form of maps.

Definition 3.2
.

A
strict map
of smooth/topological Kuranishi spaces from
K_{1}=(G_{1},V_{1},E_{1},S_{1})
to
K_{2}=(G_{2},V_{2},E_{2},S_{2})
, denoted by
\iota_{21}:K_{1}\to K_{2}
, consists of a Lie group homomorphism
\iota_{21}^{G}:G_{1}\to G_{2}
and a commutative diagram

\textstyle{E_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{21}^{E}}
\textstyle{E_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{21}^{V}}
\textstyle{V_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{2}}

where
\iota_{21}^{V}
is an equivariant smooth/continuous map and
\iota_{21}^{E}
is an equivariant smooth/continuous bundle map.

Notice that strict maps can be composed.

We list a few special kinds of strict maps.

Definition 3.3

(Conjugation)
.

Let
K=(G,V,E,S)
be a K-chart. The
conjugation
of
K
by
g\in G
is the strict map
\iota_{g}:K\to K
where
\iota_{g}^{G}:G\to G
is the conjugation
h\mapsto g^{-1}hg
and
\iota_{g}^{V}
resp.
\iota_{g}^{E}
is the action of the form
x\mapsto g^{-1}x
. Two strict maps
\iota_{21},\iota_{21}^{\prime}:K_{1}\to K_{2}
are said to be
conjugate
if there exists
g_{2}\in G_{2}
such that
\iota_{21}^{\prime}=\iota_{g_{2}}\circ\iota_{21}
.

Definition 3.4

(Stabilization)
.

Let
K=(G,V,E,S)
be an
A
-stratified K-chart. Let
\pi_{N}:N\to V
be a
G
-equivariant continuous resp. smooth vector bundle and let
D\subseteq N
be a
G
-invariant open (disk) subbundle.
8
8

8

When we talk about disk bundles, we always assume that it is a subset of a certain vector bundle, although it may not appear so in notations.
The
stabilization
of
K
by
D
, denoted by
{\rm Stab}_{D}(K)
, is the
A
-stratified K-chart

{\rm Stab}_{D}(K)=(G,D,\pi_{N}^{*}E\oplus\pi_{N}^{*}N,\pi_{N}^{*}S\oplus\tau_{N})

where
\tau_{N}:N\to\pi_{N}^{*}N
is the tautological section. By abuse of language, we also call the morphism

\iota_{D}:K\to{\rm Stab}_{D}(K)

induced by the zero section
V\to D
and the obvious bundle embedding
E\to\pi_{N}^{*}E\oplus\pi_{N}^{*}N
a
stabilization morphism
.

Definition 3.5

(Shrinking and open embedding)
.

(1)

Let
K=(G,V,E,S)
be a K-chart. A
shrinking
of
K
is a K-chart
K^{\prime}=(G,V^{\prime},E^{\prime},S^{\prime})
where
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
is a
G
-invariant open neighborhood of
S^{-1}(0)
,
E^{\prime}=E|_{V^{\prime}}
, and
S^{\prime}=S|_{V^{\prime}}
.

(2)

A morphism
\iota_{21}:K_{1}\to K_{2}
is called an
open embedding
if the underlying poset map is an isomorphism onto an Alexandrov open subset, the map
V_{1}\to V_{2}
is an isomorphism onto an open subset, and the bundle map is an isomorphism of vector bundles when restricted to the open subset identified with
V_{1}
.

Definition 3.6

(Group enlargement)
.

Let
K=(G,V,E,S)
be a K-chart. A
group enlargement
of
K
by a Lie group embedding
G\hookrightarrow G^{\prime}
is a K-chart

G^{\prime}\times_{G}K=(G^{\prime},G^{\prime}\times_{G}V,G^{\prime}\times_{G}E,S^{\prime})

where
