be an ungraded Novikov group and
{\bf K}
be a field.

(1)

A nonarchimedean normed vector space
(C,\ell_{C})
over
\Lambda_{\bf K}^{\Pi}
is called
orthogonalizable
if the tensor product
C\otimes\Lambda_{\bf K}^{|\Pi|}
is orthogonalizable over
\Lambda_{\bf K}^{|\Pi|}
.

(2)

A
Floer-type complex
over
\Lambda_{\bf K}^{\Pi}
consists of a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-graded orthogonalizable
\Lambda_{\bf K}^{\Pi}
-space
C
together with an odd
\Lambda_{\bf K}^{\Pi}
-differential
d_{C}:C\to C
satisfying

\ell_{C}(d_{C}x)\leq\ell_{C}(x).

Remark 7.35
.

There is another notion called Floer–Novikov complex defined in
[
70
]
. It requires that the differential strictly decreases the nonarchimedean norm. On the other hand, that notion allows one to consider Novikov rings with coefficients in a general Noetherian ring and the exponents
\Gamma
to be a general finite-rank free abelian group. In the Floer setup, the Floer complex can be made both a Floer–Novikov complex and a Floer-type complex with appropriate Novikov coefficients.

Next we have two notions about chain maps and chain homotopies.

Definition 7.36
.

(cf.
[
69
, Definition 4.5]
)
Let
C
and
D
be two Floer-type complexes over
\Lambda_{\bf K}^{\Pi}
.

(1)

A linear map
\Phi:C\to D
is said to
preserve the filtration
if

\ell_{D}(\Phi(x))\leq\ell_{C}(x)\ \forall x\in C.

(2)

C
and
D
are said to be
filtered isomorphic
if there is an isomorphism
\Phi:C\to D
of chain complexes such that

\ell_{D}(\Phi(x))=\ell_{C}(x)\ \forall x\in C.

(3)

Two chain maps
\Phi,\Psi:C\to D
are said to be
filtered chain homotopic
if 1) both
\Phi
and
\Psi
preserve the filtration and 2) there exists a homotopy
K:C\to D
between
\Phi
and
\Psi
which also preserves the filtration. Two Floer-type complexes are
filtered chain homotopy equivalent
if there exists a filtered chain homotopy
\Phi:C\to D
and a filtered homotopy inverse
\Psi:D\to C
such that
\Phi\circ\Psi
is filtered chain homotopic to
{\rm Id}_{D}
and
\Psi\circ\Phi
is filtered chain homotopic to
{\rm Id}_{C}
.

One uses the following notion to compare Floer type complexes.

Definition 7.37
.

Let
(C,\ell_{C},d_{C})
and
(D,\ell_{D},d_{C})
be two Floer-type complexes and
\delta\geq 0
. A
\delta
-quasiequivalence
between
C
and
D
is a quadruple
(\Phi,\Psi,K_{C},K_{D})
where
\Phi:C\to D
,
\Psi:D\to C
are chain maps,
K_{C}
,
K_{D}
are chain homotopies as

\displaystyle\ \Psi\circ\Phi-{\rm Id}_{C}=d_{C}\circ K_{C}+K_{C}\circ d_{C},\

\displaystyle\ \Phi\circ\Psi-{\rm Id}_{D}=d_{D}\circ K_{D}+K_{D}\circ d_{D}

satisfying for all
x\in C
and
y\in D
,

\displaystyle\ \ell_{D}(\Phi(x))\leq\ell_{C}(x)+\delta,\

\displaystyle\ \ell_{C}(\Psi(y))\leq\ell_{D}(y)+\delta,

and

\displaystyle\ \ell_{C}(K_{C}(x))\leq\ell_{C}(x)+2\delta,\

\displaystyle\ \ell_{D}(K_{D}(y))\leq\ell_{D}(y)+2\delta.

The
quasiequivalence distance
between two Floer-type complexes is the infimum of
\delta
for which there exists a
\delta
-quasiequivalence between them.

Below we show that the complex obtained from a Novikov flow category is indeed a Floer-type complex, once we use field coefficients.

Proposition 7.38
.

Let
\Pi
be a finitely generated Novikov group and let
({\mathbb{F}},{\mathcal{A}})
be a
\Pi
-equivariant Novikov flow category enriched in
\underline{\smash{{\bf\Psi}{\bf Man}}}
satisfying in addition

(1)

The action
\lambda
on
\Pi
is
{\mathbb{R}}
-valued and
{\mathcal{A}}
is
{\mathbb{R}}
-valued.

(2)

{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{F}}/\Pi
is finite.

Then these data together with the differential on the chain complex
C({\mathbb{F}})
define a filtered Floer–Novikov complex in the sense of
[
70
, Definition 1.1]
.

Moreover, for any field
{\bf K}
, the complex

C({\mathbb{F}})\otimes\Lambda_{\bf K}^{\Pi}

together with the norm
\ell:={\mathcal{A}}
is a Floer-type complex over
\Lambda_{\bf K}^{\Pi}
in the sense of Definition
7.34
.

Proof.

The first claim is straightforward where we take
{\rm Ob}(\mathring{\mathbb{F}})
under the
\Pi
-action equipped with the action
{\mathcal{A}}
, and we use the incidence coefficients of the differential. For the second, see
[
69
, Section 12]
.
∎

7.5.2.
Filtration and spectral invariants

For any Floer-type complex
(C,\ell_{C},d_{C})
, one can consider the filtration defined by

\displaystyle\ C^{\leq\tau}=\Big\{x\in C\ |\ \ell_{C}(x)\leq\tau\Big\},\

\displaystyle\ C^{<\tau}:=\Big\{x\in C\ |\ \ell_{C}(x)<\tau\Big\}.

As
d_{C}
respects the norm,
C^{\leq\tau}
is a subcomplex. Then for any interval
I\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
, one has a corresponding complex defined in a typical way. For example, when
I=[a,b]
,
C^{I}
is the quotient complex

C^{I}:=C^{\leq b}/C^{<a}

For each
x\in H(C)
, define the
spectral number
to be

c(x):=\inf\Big\{\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}\ |\ x\in{\rm Im}\Big(H(C^{<\tau})\to H(C)\Big)\Big\}\in{\mathbb{R}}\cup\{-\infty\}.

When the complex
C
comes from a flow category
{\mathbb{F}}
enriched in
\underline{\smash{{\bf\Psi}{\bf Man}}}
, denote the spectral number by

c_{{\mathbb{F}}}(x)

where the coefficient ring is understood from the context.

Usher
[
70
]
provided an abstract argument showing the finiteness of spectral numbers and existence of spectral carriers.

Theorem 7.39

(cf.
[
70
]
)
.

Under the assumption of Proposition
7.38
, for any Noetherian ring
R
, and
a\in H_{*}({\mathbb{F}})\neq 0
,
\tau(a):=c_{\mathbb{F}}(a)>-\infty
. Moreover,
a
is represented by a cycle contained in
C_{*}({\mathbb{F}})^{\leq\tau(a)}
.

Proof.

The theorem follows from Proposition
7.38
and
[
70
, Theorem 1.3]
.
∎

7.5.3.
Barcodes

By the nonarchimedean Gram–Schmidt process established in
[
69
, Definition 6.3]
, given a Floer-type complex
(C,\ell_{C},d_{C})=(C,\ell,d)
, the map
d:C\to C
of the ungraded complex factors through
C\to\ker(d)\subseteq C
, so we can find an orthogonal basis
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}
of
C
and an orthogonal basis of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}
of
{\rm ker}(d)
such that one can write
d:C\to\ker(d)
in the block-matrix form

\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}I_{r}&0\\
0&0\end{array}\right]

where
r\leq n
is the rank of
d
and such that

\ell(y_{1})-\ell(x_{1})\geq\cdots\geq\ell(y_{r})-\ell(x_{r}).

The
verbose barcode
of
(C,d,\ell)
is the multiset of elements
({\mathbb{R}}/\Gamma)\times[0,+\infty]
consisting of

(1)

a pair
(\ell(x_{i})\ {\rm mod}\ \Gamma,\ell(y_{i})-\ell(x_{i}))
for
i=1,\ldots,r
.

(2)

a pair
(\ell(x_{i})\ {\rm mod}\ \Gamma,\infty)
for
i=r+1,\ldots,m
.

Each element of this multiset is called a bar while the second variable is called the bar-length. The
concise barcode
is the submultiset obtained by removing bars of length zero. It was proved (
[
69
, Theorem 7.1]
) that the verbose barcode of a Floer-type complex does not depend on the choice of the singular value decomposition, hence is well-defined.

Theorem 7.40
.

Let
C
and
D
be two Floer-type complexes over
\Lambda_{\bf K}^{\Pi}
.

(1)

If
C
and
D
are filtered isomorphic, then they have identical verbose barcodes.

(2)

If
C
and
D
are filtered homotopy equivalent, then they have identical concise barcodes.

Proof.

By
[
69
, Theorem A, B]
, this is true if the Novikov group
\Pi
is contained in
{\mathbb{R}}
. In our setting, the conclusion holds after tensoring with
\Lambda_{\bf K}^{|\Pi|}
.
∎

7.5.4.
Continuity

Now we consider the case of tensor product and discuss quantitative aspects of chain maps. Let
({\mathbb{F}}_{1},{\mathcal{A}}_{1}),\ldots,({\mathbb{F}}_{m},{\mathcal{A}}_{m})
be locally finite flow categories enriched in
\underline{\smash{{\bf\Psi}{\bf Man}}}
so there are the associated chain complexes
C({\mathbb{F}}_{j})
. Recall that one can consider the completed tensor product (over
{\mathbb{Z}}
)

C({\mathbb{F}}_{1})\widehat{\otimes}\cdots\widehat{\otimes}C({\mathbb{F}}_{m})

whose elements are formal sums

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}a_{i}\big(p_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes p_{i_{m}}\big)

with
a_{i}\in R
such that

\lim_{i\to\infty}\big({\mathcal{A}}_{1}(p_{i_{1}})+\cdots+{\mathcal{A}}_{m}(p_{i_{m}})\big)=-\infty.

Then there is a similar filtration on the tensor product and for
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{m}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, an inclusion

C^{\leq\tau_{1}}({\mathbb{F}}_{1})\widehat{\otimes}\cdots\widehat{\otimes}C^{\leq\tau_{m}}({\mathbb{F}}_{m})\to\big(C({\mathbb{F}}_{1})\widehat{\otimes}\cdots\widehat{\otimes}C({\mathbb{F}}_{m})\big)^{\leq\tau_{1}+\cdots+\tau_{m}}

Definition 7.41
.

Let
{\mathbb{F}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{F}}_{m},{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}
be Novikov flow categories with
{\mathbb{R}}
-valued actions
{\mathcal{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{A}}_{m},{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
.

(1)

Let
{\mathbb{X}}
be a Novikov multimodule over
({\mathbb{F}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{F}}_{m};{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime})
. The
threshold
of
{\mathbb{X}}
is the number

\epsilon^{\mathbb{X}}:=\inf\Big\{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}(p_{1})+\cdots+{\mathcal{A}}_{m}(p_{m})-{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(p^{\prime})\ |\ M_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{X}}\neq\emptyset\Big\}\in[-\infty,\infty].

(2)

Let
{\mathbb{X}}_{0}
and
{\mathbb{X}}_{1}
be two Novikov multimodules over
({\mathbb{F}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{F}}_{m};{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime})
. Let
{\mathbb{H}}
be a Novikov homotopy from
{\mathbb{X}}_{0}
to
{\mathbb{X}}_{1}
. The
threshold
of
{\mathbb{H}}
is the number

\delta^{\mathbb{H}}:=\inf\Big\{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}(p_{1})+\cdots+{\mathcal{A}}_{m}(p_{m})-{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(p^{\prime})\ |\ M_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{H}}\neq\emptyset\Big\}\in[-\infty,+\infty].

By the Novikov property of multimodules (see Definition
7.2
),
\epsilon^{\mathbb{X}}\neq-\infty
and
\delta^{\mathbb{H}}\neq-\infty
.

Proposition 7.42
.

In the situation of Definition
7.41
, the chain map

\Phi^{\mathbb{X}}:C({\mathbb{F}}_{1})\widehat{\otimes}\cdots\widehat{\otimes}C({\mathbb{F}}_{m})\to C({\mathbb{F}}^{\prime})

satisfies

\Phi^{\mathbb{X}}\left(\big(C({\mathbb{F}}_{1})\widehat{\otimes}\cdots\widehat{\otimes}C({\mathbb{F}}_{m})\big)^{\leq\tau}\right)\subseteq C_{*}^{\leq\tau-\epsilon^{\mathbb{X}}}({\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}).

The chain homotopy

\Phi^{\mathbb{H}}:C({\mathbb{F}}_{1})\widehat{\otimes}\cdots\widehat{\otimes}C({\mathbb{F}}_{m})\to C({\mathbb{F}}^{\prime})

satisfies

\Phi^{\mathbb{H}}\left(\big(C({\mathbb{F}}_{1})\widehat{\otimes}\cdots\widehat{\otimes}C({\mathbb{F}}_{m})\big)^{\leq\tau}\right)\subseteq C_{*}^{\leq\tau-\delta^{\mathbb{H}}}({\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}).

Proof.

Straightforward check.
∎

8.
FOP Perturbations

In this section we review the technique of FOP perturbations. The original idea was proposed by Fukaya–Ono in
[
29
]
, and further explored by Parker
[
54
]
. In
[
13
]
the authors rigorously established this technique and defined integer-valued Gromov–Witten invariants for general compact symplectic manifolds. In
[
14
]
the authors further proved the integral Arnold conjecture; see
[
11
]
for another application.

8.1.
FOP perturbations

Definition 8.1

(Perturbations on derived orbifolds)
.

Given a derived orbifold
{\mathcal{D}}=({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
, a
perturbation
of
{\mathcal{D}}
(or of
{\mathcal{S}}
) is a smooth section
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
such that if
{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(0)
is compact, then
({\mathcal{S}}^{\prime})^{-1}(0)
is also compact.

We recall the main “black-box” type theorem proved in
[
13
]
.

Theorem 8.2
.

[
13
, Theorem 1.1]

Suppose
{\mathcal{U}}
is an NC orbifold without boundary
14
14

14

When discussing FOP transverse sections for orbifolds with boundaries and corners, the behavior near boundary and corner strata are predetermined.
and
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
is an NC vector bundle. Let
\Gamma({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})
be the space of smooth sections. Then there is a
C^{0}
-dense subset
\Gamma^{\rm FOP}({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})\subseteq\Gamma({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})
whose elements are called
FOP transverse sections
satisfying the following properties.

(1)

(Classical transversality)
If
{\mathcal{U}}
is a manifold, FOP transversality is equivalent to classical transversality.

(2)

(Locality Property)
The restrictions of FOP transverse sections to open subsets are still FOP transverse.

(3)

(Extension Property)
For any pair of closed subsets
{\mathcal{Y}}\subseteq{\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime}
and open neighborhoods
{\mathcal{V}}\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}
of
{\mathcal{Y}}
and
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}
of
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime}
, if
{\mathcal{S}}\in\Gamma^{\rm FOP}({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{E}}|_{{\mathcal{V}}})
, then there exists
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\rm FOP}({\mathcal{V}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{E}}|_{{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}})
which agrees with
{\mathcal{S}}
near
{\mathcal{Y}}
.

(4)

(Product Property)
Let
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
be another normally complex orbifold and
{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
be a normally complex vector bundle. Then the product map

\Gamma({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})\times\Gamma({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime})\to\Gamma({\mathcal{U}}\times{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{E}}\boxplus{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime})

sends products of FOP transverse sections to FOP transverse sections.

(5)

(Stabilization Property)
If
\pi_{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{F}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
is rigid complex vector bundle
15
15

15

This condition was stated in
[
13
]
for more general normally complex vector bundles. When
{\mathcal{F}}
is a rigid bundle, the total space
{\mathcal{F}}
carries a canonical NC structure.
, then the stabilization map

\Gamma({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})\to\Gamma({\mathcal{F}},\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\oplus\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}),\quad\quad{\mathcal{S}}\mapsto\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}{\mathcal{S}}\oplus\tau_{\mathcal{F}},

where
\tau_{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{F}}\to\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}
is the tautological section, sends FOP transverse sections to FOP transverse sections.

(6)

(Main stratum)
For each
{\mathcal{S}}\in\Gamma^{\rm FOP}({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})
, the closure

\overline{{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(0)\cap{\mathcal{U}}_{\rm free}}

is a pseudomanifold (whose dimension is
{\rm dim}{\mathcal{U}}-{\rm rank}{\mathcal{E}}
).

Definition 8.3
.

Let
\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}^{\rm FOP}
resp.
\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}^{\rm FOP}_{\rm rig}
be the category whose objects are

\mathring{\mathcal{D}}:=({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime})

where
{\mathcal{D}}:=({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
is an NC derived orbifold and
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
is an FOP transverse perturbation. The morphism from
\mathring{\mathcal{D}}_{1}:=({\mathcal{U}}_{1},{\mathcal{E}}_{1},{\mathcal{S}}_{1},{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{\prime})
to
\mathring{\mathcal{D}}_{2}:=({\mathcal{U}}_{2},{\mathcal{E}}_{2},{\mathcal{S}}_{2},{\mathcal{S}}_{2}^{\prime})
is an embedding of underlying stratified orbifolds covered by a bundle embedding
{\bm{\iota}}_{21}=(\iota_{21},\widehat{\iota}_{21}):({\mathcal{U}}_{1},{\mathcal{E}}_{1})\to({\mathcal{U}}_{2},{\mathcal{E}}_{2})
such that it induces both a morphism of
\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}^{\rm NC}
resp.
\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}^{\rm NC}_{\rm rig}
from
{\mathcal{D}}_{1}:=({\mathcal{U}}_{1},{\mathcal{E}}_{1},{\mathcal{S}}_{1})
to
{\mathcal{D}}_{2}:=({\mathcal{U}}_{2},{\mathcal{E}}_{2},{\mathcal{S}}_{2})
and a morphism with
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
replaced by
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}^{\prime}
.

Notice that there are natural forgetful functors

by forgetting the perturbation part
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
resp. taking the closure of
({\mathcal{S}}^{\prime})^{-1}(0)\cap{\mathcal{U}}_{\rm free}
.

By using Theorem
8.2
inductively, one can construct FOP transverse perturbations on stratified derived orbifolds which respect collars.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
A
be a regular poset and
{\mathcal{D}}=({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
be an
A
-stratified object of
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}^{\rm NC}
. Let
A^{\prime}\subseteq A
be an Alexandrov closed subset and

\partial^{A^{\prime}}{\mathcal{D}}

be the corresponding
A^{\prime}
-stratified derived orbifold. Suppose
{\mathcal{S}}_{A^{\prime}}:\partial^{A^{\prime}}{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
is an FOP transverse perturbation so that

(\partial^{A^{\prime}}{\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{S}}_{A^{\prime}})

is an
A^{\prime}
-stratified object of
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}^{\rm FOP}
. Then there exists an FOP transverse perturbation
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
which extends
{\mathcal{S}}_{A^{\prime}}
such that
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime})
is an object of
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}^{\rm FOP}
.

8.2.
FOP perturbations on flow categories, multimodules, and homotopies

Now we prove a major result of this paper stating the general procedure of constructing FOP perturbations on flow categories, multimodules, and homotopies.

Theorem N
.

Flow categories, multimodules, and homotopies in this proposition are assumed to be unobstructed (Definition
7.1
) and locally compact (Definition
7.2
,
7.5
,
7.6
, and
7.8
). We also assume that all charts have compact zero locus.

(1)

(FOP perturbation for equivariant flow categories)
Let
\mathbb{\Pi}\rtimes G
be a twisted Novikov group (Definition
7.21
) and let
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}
be a free
\mathbb{\Pi}\rtimes G
-equivariant flow category enriched in
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm NC}
. Then there exists a
\mathbb{\Pi}\rtimes G
-equivariant FOP perturbation
\mathring{\mathbb{F}}
on
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}
.

(2)

(FOP perturbation for equivariant multimodules)
Let
\mathbb{\Pi}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{\Pi}_{m};\mathbb{\Pi}^{\prime}
be Novikov groups and
G
be a finite group acting on
\mathbb{\Pi}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{\Pi}_{m}
and on
\mathbb{\Pi}^{\prime}
. Let

\rho:\mathbb{\Pi}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{\Pi}_{m}\to\mathbb{\Pi}^{\prime}

be a
G
-equivariant morphism of Novikov groups. Let
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\mathbb{F}}_{m}
be
\mathbb{\Pi}_{i}
-equivariant flow categories enriched in
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm NC}
such that the weak flow category
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}_{1}\times\cdots\times\tilde{\mathbb{F}}_{m}
has a free
(\mathbb{\Pi}_{1}\times\cdots\times{\mathbb{\Pi}}_{m})\rtimes G
-action. Let
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}
be a free
\mathbb{\Pi}^{\prime}\rtimes G
-equivariant flow category enriched in
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm NC}
. Let
\tilde{\mathbb{X}}
be a
\rho
-equivariant multimodule over
(\tilde{\mathbb{F}}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\mathbb{F}}_{m};\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime})
. Let
\mathring{\mathbb{F}}_{i}
resp.
\mathring{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}
be a
\mathbb{\Pi}_{i}
-equivariant resp.
\mathbb{\Pi}^{\prime}
-equivariant FOP perturbation on
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}_{i}
resp.
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}
such that
\mathring{\mathbb{F}}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathring{\mathbb{F}}_{m}
resp.
\mathring{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}
is also
G
-equivariant. Then there exists a
\rho
-equivariant FOP perturbation
\mathring{\mathbb{X}}
on
\tilde{\mathbb{X}}
as an equivariant multimodule over
(\mathring{\mathbb{F}}_{1},\ldots,\mathring{\mathbb{F}}_{m};\mathring{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime})
.

(3)

(FOP perturbation for equivariant multimodule homotopies)
Let
\mathbb{\Pi}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{\Pi}_{m}
,
\mathbb{\Pi}^{\prime}
,
G
,
\rho
,
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\mathbb{F}}_{m},\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}
, and
\mathring{\mathbb{F}}_{1},\ldots,\mathring{\mathbb{F}}_{m},\mathring{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}
be as in the above situation. Let
\tilde{\mathbb{X}}_{0}
and
\tilde{\mathbb{X}}_{1}
be two
\rho
-equivariant multimodules over
(\tilde{\mathbb{F}}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\mathbb{F}}_{m};\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime})
and
\tilde{\mathbb{H}}
be a
\rho
-equivariant homotopy from
\tilde{\mathbb{X}}_{0}
to
\tilde{\mathbb{X}}_{1}
. Let
\mathring{\mathbb{X}}_{0}
and
\mathring{\mathbb{X}}_{1}
be FOP perturbations on
\tilde{\mathbb{X}}_{0}
and
\tilde{\mathbb{X}}_{1}
respectively as
\rho
-equivariant multimodules over
(\mathring{\mathbb{F}}_{1},\ldots,\mathring{\mathbb{F}}_{m};\mathring{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime})
. Then there exists a
\rho
-equivariant FOP perturbation
\mathring{\mathbb{H}}
on
\tilde{\mathbb{H}}
as a homotopy from
\mathring{\mathbb{X}}_{0}
to
\mathring{\mathbb{X}}_{1}
.

Proof of Theorem
N
.

We construct the FOP perturbations inductively. In general, the Novikov conditions of Definition
7.2
, Definition
7.5
, and Definition
7.24
allow us to choose a beginning of the induction process. The freeness assumed in Theorem
N
allows us to achieve equivariant transversality.

—
The case of flow categories
— We say a pair of objects
p<q
is minimal if
M_{pq}^{\tilde{\mathbb{F}}}\neq\emptyset
and there does not exist a third object
r
such that both
M_{pr}^{\tilde{\mathbb{F}}}
and
M_{rq}^{\tilde{\mathbb{F}}}
are nonempty. By (2) of Definition
7.24
, unless all morphism spaces are empty, minimal pairs exist. Moreover, the
\mathbb{\Pi}
-equivariance condition implies that minimal pairs form a union of
\mathbb{\Pi}
-orbits.

Now we perturb
M_{pq}^{\tilde{\mathbb{F}}}=({\mathcal{U}}_{pq},{\mathcal{E}}_{pq},{\mathcal{S}}_{pq})
for minimal pairs
p<q
. We claim that
{\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{-1}(0)
is strictly contained in the union of top strata of
{\mathcal{D}}_{pq}
. If it is not the case, then there exists a codimension one stratum which intersects
{\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{-1}(0)
. By the unobstructedness assumption, such a stratum comes from the product of two other morphism spaces. This contradicts the assumption that
p<q
is a minimal pair. Hence our claim is correct.

Then by Theorem
8.2
, there exists an FOP transverse perturbation
{\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{\prime}
. Then using the free
\mathbb{\Pi}
-action, we translate this perturbation to charts
M_{p^{\prime}q^{\prime}}^{\tilde{\mathbb{F}}}
with all
(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
in the same
\mathbb{\Pi}
-orbit of
(p,q)
. We apply such perturbation constructions independently for all
\mathbb{\Pi}
-orbits of minimal pairs.

Now we set up the induction. Recall that the Novikov flow category
{\mathbb{F}}
carries an
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
-valued action
{\mathcal{A}}=({\mathcal{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{A}}_{k})
. Define

|{\mathcal{A}}|={\mathcal{A}}_{1}+\cdots+{\mathcal{A}}_{m}:{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{F}}\to{\mathbb{R}}.

Choose an increasing sequence
c_{1}<c_{2}<\cdots
satisfying the following conditions.

(1)

c_{l}
goes to infinity.

(2)

For each pair
p<q
with
c_{l-1}\leq|{\mathcal{A}}|(p)-|{\mathcal{A}}|(q)<c_{l}
, if
p<r<q
, then
|{\mathcal{A}}|(p)-|{\mathcal{A}}|(r)<c_{l-1}
and
|{\mathcal{A}}|(r)-|{\mathcal{A}}|(q)<c_{l-1}
.

(3)

If
p<q
, then
|{\mathcal{A}}|(p)-|{\mathcal{A}}|(q)\geq c_{1}
.

Now we state the following induction hypothesis: suppose for
c_{l}
in this sequence, we have constructed FOP transverse perturbations

{\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{pq}

for all pairs
p<q
with
|{\mathcal{A}}|(p)-|{\mathcal{A}}|(q)<c_{l}
for all
\alpha=1,\ldots,k
, which satisfy the following conditions.

(1)

The perturbations are
\mathbb{\Pi}
-equivariant.

(2)

Whenever
p<r<q
, the restriction of
{\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{\prime}
to
\partial^{prq}{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}
coincides with the stabilization of the product of
{\mathcal{S}}_{pr}^{\prime}
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{rq}^{\prime}
under the compositions of
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}
.

(3)

If
p<q
is minimal, then
{\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{\prime}
is the one we have already constructed.

Our choice of
c_{1}
implies that the base case is established. Suppose the induction hypothesis holds for
c_{l-1}
.

Consider a pair
p<q
with
|{\mathcal{A}}|(p)-|{\mathcal{A}}|(q)\in[c_{l-1},c_{l})
. If the pair
(p,q)
is minimal, then we take
{\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{\prime}
to be the one we constructed before the induction setup. Suppose it is not minimal. By the induction hypothesis, for each
r
with
p<r<q
, one has constructed FOP transverse perturbations

\displaystyle\ {\mathcal{S}}_{pr}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}_{pr}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{pr},\

\displaystyle\ {\mathcal{S}}_{rq}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}_{rq}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{rq}.

As the structural map

M_{pr}^{\mathbb{F}}\times M_{rq}^{\mathbb{F}}\to\partial^{prq}M_{pq}^{\mathbb{F}}

is a rigidified embedding of normally complex derived orbifolds, there is a canonical extension of the product
{\mathcal{S}}_{pr}^{\prime}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{rq}^{\prime}
to
\partial^{prq}{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}
. The induction hypothesis also implies that for all such
r
, these canonical extensions agree on strata of the form
\partial^{pr_{1}r_{2}q}{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}
. Hence one obtains an object in
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm FOP}
stratified by the true boundary of
A_{pq}^{\tilde{\mathbb{F}}}
. Then using Lemma
8.4
, there exists an FOP transverse perturbation
{\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha}:\partial^{\alpha}{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{pq}
which is compatible with the existing ones.

As the
\mathbb{\Pi}
-action is free, one can translate
{\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{\prime}
to all pairs on the same
\mathbb{\Pi}
-orbit. Apply the same procedure for all
\mathbb{\Pi}
-orbits of pairs
p^{\prime},q^{\prime}
with
|{\mathcal{A}}|(p^{\prime})-|{\mathcal{A}}|(q^{\prime})\in[c_{l-1},c_{l})
. This implies that the induction can be carried out. Hence the case of flow categories of Theorem
N
is established.

—
The case of multimodules—
We follow a similar inductive process. Consider the set
{\rm Ob}\tilde{\mathbb{F}}_{1}\times\cdots\times{\rm Ob}\tilde{\mathbb{F}}_{m}\times{\rm Ob}\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}
, whose objects are denoted by
(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m};p^{\prime})
. Then
\mathbb{\Pi}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{\Pi}_{m}
acts freely on this set via the morphism
\rho
; in the situation of item (3), the group
\Gamma
also acts on this set and the
\Gamma
-action commutes with
\mathbb{\Pi}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{\Pi}_{m}
. We say a tuple
(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m};p^{\prime})
is minimal if 1) there does not exist
i
and
p_{i}<q_{i}
in
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}_{i}
such that
M_{p_{1}\cdots p_{i-1}q_{i}p_{i+1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}}^{\tilde{\mathbb{X}}}\neq\emptyset
and 2) there does not exist
q^{\prime}<p^{\prime}
in
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}
such that
M_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};q^{\prime}}^{\tilde{\mathbb{X}}}\neq\emptyset
. When
(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m};p^{\prime})
is minimal, similar to the flow category case, the unobstructedness assumption implies that one can construct an FOP transverse perturbation

{\mathcal{S}}_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}}

which can be translated to orbits of
\mathbb{\Pi}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{\Pi}_{m}\times\Gamma
.

Now we set up an induction scheme. For each tuple
(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m};p^{\prime})
, define

|p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}|=|{\mathcal{A}}_{1}|(p_{1})+\cdots+|{\mathcal{A}}_{m}|(p_{m})-|{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}|(p^{\prime})\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Lemma 8.5
.

There exists a sequences of real numbers

c_{1}<c_{2}<\cdots

satisfying the following conditions.

(1)

c_{l}
goes to infinity.

(2)

If
|p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}|\in[c_{l-1},c_{l})
with
p_{i}<q_{i}
in
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}_{i}
such that
M_{p_{1}\cdots p_{i-1}q_{i}p_{i+1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}}^{\tilde{\mathbb{X}}}\neq\emptyset
, then
|p_{1}\cdots p_{i-1}q_{i}p_{i+1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}|<c_{l-1}
.

(3)

If
|p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}|\in[c_{l-1},c_{l})
with
q^{\prime}<p^{\prime}
in
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}
such that
M_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};q^{\prime}}^{\tilde{\mathbb{X}}}\neq\emptyset
, then
|p_{1}\cdots p_{m};q^{\prime}|<c_{l-1}
.

(4)

If
M_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}}^{\tilde{\mathbb{X}}}\neq\emptyset
, then
|p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}|\geq c_{1}
.

Proof.

It follows from the definition of equivariant Novikov flow categories (Definition
7.24
) that for each
i
, one has

\inf\Big\{|{\mathcal{A}}_{i}|(p_{i})-|{\mathcal{A}}_{i}|(q_{i})\ |\ M_{p_{i}q_{i}}^{\tilde{\mathbb{F}}_{i}}\neq\emptyset\Big\}>0.

Similarly

\inf\Big\{|{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}|(p^{\prime})-|{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}|(q^{\prime})\ |\ M_{p^{\prime}q^{\prime}}^{\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset\Big\}>0.

Let
\epsilon
be the minimum of these lower bounds. Then we can take
c_{l}=l\epsilon
.
∎

Now we state the induction hypothesis: for a
c_{l}
in this sequence, we have constructed FOP transverse perturbations

{\mathcal{S}}_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}}

for all tuples
(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m};p^{\prime})
satisfying
|p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}|<c_{l}
which satisfy the following conditions.

(1)

The perturbations are
\mathbb{\Pi}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{\Pi}_{m}
-equivariant.

(2)

Whenever
p_{i}<q_{i}
in
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}_{i}
, the morphism

\iota_{p_{1}\cdots p_{i-1}(p_{i}q_{i})p_{i+1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}}^{\tilde{\mathbb{X}}}:M_{p_{i}q_{i}}^{\tilde{\mathbb{F}}_{i}}\times M_{p_{1}\cdots p_{i-1}q_{i}p_{i+1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}}^{\tilde{\mathbb{X}}}\to M_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}}^{\tilde{\mathbb{X}}}

is also a morphism in
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm FOP}
.

(3)

Whenever
q^{\prime}<p^{\prime}
in
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}
, the morphism

\iota_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};q^{\prime}p^{\prime}}^{\tilde{\mathbb{X}}}:M_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};q^{\prime}}^{\tilde{\mathbb{X}}}\times M_{q^{\prime}p^{\prime}}^{\tilde{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}}\to M_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}}^{\tilde{\mathbb{X}}}

is also a morphism in
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm FOP}
.

(4)

When
(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m};p^{\prime})
is minimal,
{\mathcal{S}}_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}}^{\prime}
is the one we have already constructed before the induction.

Our choice of
c_{1}
implies that the base case is established. Suppose the induction hypothesis holds for
c_{l-1}
. Consider a tuple
(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m};p^{\prime})
with
|p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}|\in[c_{l-1},c_{l})
. If it is minimal, then the perturbation has been constructed. If not, then the induction hypothesis and the conditions on the sequence
c_{l}
implies that an FOP transverse perturbation has been constructed compatibly on each lower stratum of
{\mathcal{U}}_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}}
. One can extend it using the collar structure to a neighborhood of the boundary. Then by (3) of Theorem
8.2
, one can extend to an FOP transverse perturbation
{\mathcal{S}}_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p^{\prime}}
.

Using the free
\mathbb{\Pi}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{\Pi}_{m}
-action, one can translate this perturbation to all tuples on the same orbit. Apply the same procedure to all orbits of tuples within this energy window. Therefore the induction can be carried out. Hence the multimodule case of Theorem
N
is established.

The homotopy case of Theorem
N
is almost identical to the case of multimodules, hence is omitted.

∎

8.3.
FOP perturbation and multimodule concatenations

Recall that although multimodule concatenation can be canonically done for multimodules enriched in
\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}
, in practice we will have non-canonical concatenations (involving extra stabilizations).

Proposition 8.6
.

Let
\mathring{\mathbb{F}}_{1},\ldots,\mathring{\mathbb{F}}_{m},\mathring{\mathbb{G}}_{1},\ldots,\mathring{\mathbb{G}}_{n},\mathring{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime}
be Novikov flow categories enriched in
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm FOP}
. Let
\mathring{\mathbb{X}}
be a Novikov multimodule over
(\mathring{\mathbb{F}}_{1},\ldots,\mathring{\mathbb{F}}_{m};\mathring{\mathbb{G}}_{i})
and
\mathring{\mathbb{Y}}
be a Novikov multimodule over
(\mathring{\mathbb{G}}_{1},\ldots,\mathring{\mathbb{G}}_{n};\mathring{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime})
. Let
\tilde{\mathbb{X}}
resp.
\tilde{\mathbb{Y}}
be their descents to
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm NC}
. Suppose
\tilde{\mathbb{X}}\circ_{i}\tilde{\mathbb{Y}}
is a concatenation of
\tilde{\mathbb{X}}
and
\tilde{\mathbb{Y}}
at
\tilde{\mathbb{G}}_{i}
. Then this concatenation has a lift to
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm NC}
as a concatenation
\mathring{\mathbb{X}}\circ_{i}\mathring{\mathbb{Y}}
. Moreover, the concatenation respects the functor

{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm FOP}\to{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{{\bf\Psi}{\bf Man}}}.

Proof.

By Definition
6.13
, the concatenation
\tilde{\mathbb{X}}\circ_{i}\tilde{\mathbb{Y}}
contains codimension zero morphisms

M_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};r_{i}}^{\tilde{\mathbb{X}}}\times M_{r_{1}\cdots r_{n};r^{\prime}}^{\tilde{\mathbb{Y}}}\to M_{r_{1}\cdots r_{i-1}p_{1}\cdots p_{m}r_{i+1}\cdots r_{n};r^{\prime}}^{\tilde{\mathbb{X}}\circ_{i}\tilde{\mathbb{Y}}}

(8.1)

where the three items above are objects of
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm NC}
and the morphism is a collared rigidified embedding of NC derived orbifolds. Then by the
(Product Property)
of Theorem
8.2
, the product of existing FOP perturbations on
M_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};r_{i}}^{\tilde{\mathbb{X}}}
and on
M_{r_{1}\cdots r_{n};r^{\prime}}^{\tilde{\mathbb{Y}}}
is an FOP perturbation on the source of the morphism (
8.1
). Then after certain stabilization by a rigid orbifold vector bundle, by the
(Stabilization Property)
of Theorem
8.2
, the product has a canonical germ of FOP transverse extensions to the (corresponding open stratum) of the target of (
8.1
). The condition that the FOP perturbations are collared and respect the rigidifications guarantees that the extensions agree on adjacent strata. Therefore, one obtains a concatenation
\mathring{\mathbb{X}}\circ_{i}\mathring{\mathbb{Y}}
lifting
\tilde{\mathbb{X}}\circ_{i}\tilde{\mathbb{Y}}
. It is easy to verify that the concatenation is compatible with the concatenation of multimodules enriched in
\underline{\smash{{\bf\Psi}{\bf Man}}}
and the forgetful functor
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm FOP}\to{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{{\bf\Psi}{\bf Man}}}
.
∎

Part II
GEOMETRIC FLOW CATEGORIES, BIMODULES, AND HOMOTOPIES

Outline of Part 2

In this part, we describe flow categories, bimodules, and homotopies that will appear in (most of) the settings of Hamiltonian Floer theory. We then state the main theorems about the construction of their lifts to the category of normally complex Kuranishi spaces, which induce lifts to normally complex derived orbifolds. Using the the counting scheme described in Section
8
, we prove the main theorems except for those in the
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-equivariant setting. The technical constructions of the regularization of the moduli spaces are deferred to the next Part.

9.
Geometric Flow Categories

9.1.
Morse flow category

Given a closed oriented Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
and a Morse function
f:M\to{\mathbb{R}}
, the Morse flow category, denoted by

\mathbb{M}(f,g)

and usually abbreviated as
\mathbb{M}
, has critical points of
f
as its objects and the moduli spaces of unparametrized (broken) downward gradient trajectories as its morphism spaces. We always assume that
(f,g)
is Morse–Smale and that near critical points
f
is a quadratic function and
g
is the Euclidean metric with respect to a certain coordinate system near each critical point. In this way, for any two critical points
p,q\in{\rm crit}f
, the moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{pq}^{\mathbb{M}}
is a smooth manifold with corners
[
71
]
.

The partial order of
{\rm Ob}\mathbb{M}
is defined as

p\leq q\Longleftrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{pq}^{\mathbb{M}}\neq\emptyset

and the poset stratifying
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{pq}^{\mathbb{M}}
(when
p\neq q
)

A_{pq}^{\mathbb{M}}=\{p<r_{1}<\cdots<r_{k}<q\}.

Then
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{pq}^{\mathbb{M}}
is an object of
\underline{\smash{\bf SMan}}
.

We also define composition maps as the natural inclusion

\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{pr}^{\mathbb{M}}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{rq}^{\mathbb{M}}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{pq}^{\mathbb{M}}

which is a morphism of
\underline{\smash{\bf SMan}}
. Therefore, we obtained a flow category enriched in
\underline{\smash{\bf SMan}}
. In fact, the standard exponential decay estimate implies that the moduli spaces are indeed objects of
\underline{\smash{\bf SMan}}_{\rm adm}
. Therefore, its outercollaring still lives in the same category.

The flow category
\mathbb{M}(f,g)
admits a grading which assigns to each critical point
p
the its Morse index. By assigning orientations on unstable manifolds and counting elements of zero-dimensional moduli spaces, one obtains a Morse–Smale–Witten complex
CM_{*}(f,g)
over integers freely generated by critical points, whose homology is isomorphic to the singular homology of
M
.

9.2.
Floer flow category

We now describe a central object considered in this paper. We will assign to each compact symplectic manifold together with a nondegenerate Hamiltonian and an almost complex structure a flow category, called the
Floer flow category
, typically denoted by
{\mathbb{F}}
. More precisely, let
(X,\omega)
be a compact symplectic manifold. Recall

\Pi=\frac{\pi_{2}(X)}{{\rm ker}c_{1}\cap{\rm ker}\omega}

which is an abelian group (monoid). We make it a Novikov monoid (Definition
7.21
) by defining

\lambda:\Pi\to{\mathbb{R}},\ \lambda(a)=\omega(a),

and

\mu:\Pi\to{\mathbb{Z}},\ \mu(a)=2c_{1}(a).

Let
H:M\times S^{1}\to X
be a (time-dependent)
1
-periodic family of Hamiltonians which induces a 1-parameter family of Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms

\phi_{H}^{t}\in{\rm Ham}(X,\omega).

We assume that
H
is nondegenerate. Let
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}(H)
be the set of pairs
(u,x)
where
u:{\mathbb{D}}^{2}\to X
is a smooth map and
x=u|_{\partial\mathbb{D}^{2}}:S^{1}\to X
is a 1-periodic orbit of
H
. Define

(u,x)\sim(u^{\prime},x^{\prime})\Longleftrightarrow x=x^{\prime},\ \int_{{\mathbb{D}}^{2}}u^{*}\omega=\int_{{\mathbb{D}}^{2}}(u^{\prime})^{*}\omega,\ \langle c_{1}(TX),u({\mathbb{D}}^{2})\cup(-u^{\prime}({\mathbb{D}}^{2}))\rangle=0.

Define

{\mathcal{P}}(H):=\tilde{\mathcal{P}}(H)/\sim

whose elements are called
capped 1-periodic orbits
. Notice that there are well-defined action

{\mathcal{A}}_{H}:{\mathcal{P}}(H)\to{\mathbb{R}}

and well-defined Conley–Zehnder index

{\rm deg}:{\mathcal{P}}(H)\to{\mathbb{Z}}.

Moreover,
\Pi
acts on
{\mathcal{P}}(H)
freely with a finite quotient set, satisfying

\displaystyle\ {\mathcal{A}}_{H}(ap)={\mathcal{A}}_{H}(p)-\lambda(a),\

\displaystyle\ {\rm deg}(ap)={\rm deg}(p)-\mu(a).

The Floer flow category
{\mathbb{F}}
has object set

{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{F}}={\mathcal{P}}(H).

Choose an
\omega
-compatible almost complex structure
J
. For each pair
p,q\in{\mathcal{P}}(H)
, one has the moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{pq}^{\mathbb{F}}
of stable Floer trajectories. A partial order on
{\mathcal{P}}(H)
is induced by:

p\leq q\Longleftrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{pq}^{\mathbb{F}}\neq\emptyset.

The moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{pq}^{\mathbb{F}}
is stratified by the regular poset
A_{pq}^{\mathbb{F}}
which is (when
p\neq q
)

A_{pq}^{\mathbb{F}}:=\{p<r_{1}<\cdots<r_{k}<q\}.

This provides a flow category which is denoted by
\mathbb{F}(X,\omega,H,J)
, often abbreviated by
{\mathbb{F}}
. At this moment this flow category is only enriched in
\underline{\smash{\bf Top}}
.

We summarize the above setup in the following situation.

Situation Flow(i)
.

(X,\omega)
is a compact symplectic manifold.
{\mathbb{F}}
is the Floer flow category associated to a nondegenerate
1
-periodic Hamiltonian and a 1-periodic family of
\omega
-compatible almost complex structure
J
.

We summarize the usual properties of the Floer flow category.

Proposition 9.1
.

In Situation
Flow(i)
, the Floer flow category
{\mathbb{F}}
, together with the symplectic action
{\mathcal{A}}_{H}
and the Conley–Zehnder index, is a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded
\Pi
-equivariant Novikov flow category.

9.3.
The pearly flow category

To compare Floer homology and Morse homology, we introduce a version of the pearly flow category.
A priori
, this flow category induces a chain complex which is not quasi-isomorphic to the Morse chain complex, as least not in the most naive way.

Let
(X,\omega,J)
be a compact almost Kähler manifold. For each
a\in\Pi
, consider the moduli space

{\mathcal{M}}{}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(a)

which is a variant of the moduli space
{\mathcal{M}}{}_{0,2}(a)
of
J
-holomorphic maps with two marked points in degree
d
. It is defined to be the moduli space of unparametrized Floer trajectories for the Hamiltonian
H\equiv 0
. By singularity removal,
{\mathcal{M}}{}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(a)
can be alternatively described as the moduli space of
J
-holomorphic spheres of class
a
with
2
marked points such that the
2
marked points are equipped with asymptotic markers with the same angular coordinate.

There is a natural compactification

\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(a)

which is naturally stratified by (ordered) decompositions
a=a_{1}+\cdots+a_{k}
with
\omega(a_{i})>0
. The strata of the above moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(a)
can be labelled by the so-called
linear trees
. These are oriented trees whose vertices have valence at most 2, hence have a unique incoming vertex (the input) and a unique outgoing vertex (the output). The degrees
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}\in\Pi
are viewed as extra decorations on the vertices. We then can allow the edges of the tree to acquire length, allowing the lengths to vary, and allowing the edges to break when the lengths approach infinity. Choose a Morse–Smale pair
(f,g)
on
X
. We then add additional variables which are negative gradient segments of
(f,g)
defined on the edges of varying length. For the input sphere resp. output sphere we also add a semi-infinite edge on which is supported a negative gradient ray. The natural matching conditions at nodes are required. By choosing an incoming resp. outgoing critical point
\underline{\smash{x}}
resp.
\underline{\smash{y}}
of
f
, one can consider the pearly moduli space and its compactification

\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{\underline{\smash{x}}\underline{\smash{y}}}^{\mathbb{P}}(a).

The underlying poset is not as simple as the Morse or Floer case. For
x=(\underline{\smash{x}},a)
and
y=(\underline{\smash{x}},b)
in
{\rm crit}f\times\Pi
, define
A_{xy}^{\mathbb{P}}
to be the set of (possibly broken) linear trees whose breakings are labelled by critical points of
f
, whose vertices are labelled by elements of
\Pi
(with sum being
a-b
), and whose finite edges have either zero or positive lengths. This is a regular poset, whose maximal elements are those unbroken trees whose edges all have positive length. The depth function is given by the number of breakings plus the number of edges with length zero.

Definition 9.2
.

The
pearly flow category

\mathbb{P}
associated to
X,J,f,g
as above, is the flow category enriched in
\underline{\smash{\bf Top}}
which is defined as follows.

(1)

The set of objects is
{\rm Ob}\mathbb{P}={\rm crit}f\times\Pi
.

(2)

For any pair of objects
x=(\underline{\smash{x}},a)
and
y=(\underline{\smash{y}},b)
, the morphism space is the moduli space

\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{\underline{\smash{x}}\underline{\smash{y}}}^{\mathbb{P}}(a-b).

(3)

The composition map is given by the concatenation of pearly solutions.

Remark 9.3
.

The pearly flow category
\mathbb{P}
should be distinguished from the pearly bimodule introduced in
[
14
, Section 4.4]
, which is a flow bimodule between Morse flow categories.
\mathbb{P}
here is used to set up the Morse–Bott homology of the constant Hamiltonian
H\equiv 0
.

9.4.
Coupling of geometric flow categories

Given symplectic manifolds
(X^{j},\omega^{j})
,
j=1,\ldots,k
and Floer flow categories
\mathbb{F}^{j}
associated to pairs
(H^{j},J^{j})
, their coupling is defined to be the Floer flow category of the product manifold with the product Hamiltonians and almost complex structure. However, it is difficult to describe this construction on the abstract level for a collection of flow categories. The more abstract packaging would require building a symmetric monoidal structure on the (infinity) category of flow categories, cf.
[
2
]
and its follow-up work. Here we describe certain “coupling” that arises geometrically.

Running Assumption.
Let
\mathbb{F}^{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,k
be a collection of flow categories. Each of them is either a Floer, Morse, or pearly flow category on a compact (symplectic) manifold.

We define their
coupling
, denoted as

{\mathbb{F}}:={\mathbb{F}}^{1}\ast\cdots\ast{\mathbb{F}}^{k},

as follows. Its set of objects is

{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{F}}={\rm Ob}{\mathbb{F}}^{1}\times\cdots\times{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{F}}^{k}

whose elements are written as
{\bf p}=(p^{1},\ldots,p^{k})
. The morphism space
M_{{\bf p}{\bf q}}
is the moduli space of solutions
{\bf u}=(u^{1},\ldots,u^{k})
where
u^{j}
is a (stable) Floer/Morse/pearly trajectory connecting
p^{j}
and
q^{j}
, depending on the geometric origin of
\mathbb{F}^{j}
. We take the quotient by the “synchronized”
{\mathbb{R}}
-action, i.e., for any
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we identify tuples of parametrized trajectories

(u^{1}(\cdot),\dots,u^{k}(\cdot))\sim(u^{1}(\cdot+s),\dots,u^{k}(\cdot+s)),

and compactify by allowing sphere bubbling and breaking of trajectories. Notice that this formulation means that breakings in each factor happen simultaneously. For example, when all
{\mathbb{F}}^{j}
are Floer flow categories, this coupling is the same as the Floer flow category of the product manifold with respect to the product Hamiltonian and product almost complex structure. Notice that if
{\mathbb{F}}^{j}
is equivariant with respect to an abelian group
\Pi^{j}
, then
{\mathbb{F}}
is equivariant with respect to the natural action by the product
\Pi=\Pi^{1}\times\cdots\times\Pi^{k}
.

In the discussion of equivariant Floer theory, one often needs to take self-coupling. For any
k
, denote

{\mathbb{F}}^{\ast k}:=\underbrace{{\mathbb{F}}\ast\cdots\ast{\mathbb{F}}}_{k},

which is equivariant with respect to the action by the product
\Pi\times\cdots\times\Pi
of
k
copies of
\Pi
. On the other hand,
{\mathbb{Z}}_{k}
acts on this flow category by cyclically permuting the factors. This
{\mathbb{Z}}_{k}
-action commutes with the action by the diagonal
\Pi
. Hence this coupled flow category is equivariant with respect to an action by
\Pi\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{k}
.

10.
Geometric Multimodules

Next, we describe bimodules and multimodules that lead to continuation maps, PSS and SSP maps, pair-of-pants products, and Künneth maps.

10.1.
Floer data and Floer domains

Definition 10.1
.

A
punctured surface
is a compact oriented surface (not necessarily connected) with finitely many points removed, usually denoted by
\Sigma
. We also include as part of data a decomposition of the punctures into
negative ends
and
positive ends
. The negative ends are usually denoted by
z_{-}^{1},\ldots,z_{-}^{k_{-}}
, and the positive ends are usually denoted by
z_{+}^{1},\ldots,z_{+}^{k_{+}}
.

A
cylindrical end
on a punctured surface
\Sigma
around a negative resp. positive puncture
z_{\pm}^{j}
is an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism between
{\mathbb{R}}^{\pm}\times S^{1}
and a punctured neighborhood of
z_{\pm}^{j}
; two cylindrical ends are regarded as equivalent if they differ by a translation. An equivalence class is called a
germ of cylindrical ends
.

A
cylindrical surface
is a punctured surface equipped with germs of cylindrical ends around all punctures. To save notations such an object is still denoted by
\Sigma
.

Definition 10.2
.

Let
(X,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold. A
Floer datum
over a cylindrical surface
\Sigma
, denoted by
\sigma=(j,J,\alpha)
, consists of a complex structure
j
on
\Sigma
, a smooth family
J_{z}
of
\omega
-compatible almost complex structures on
X
, and a smooth 1-form (called the Hamiltonian connection)

\alpha\in\Gamma(X\times\Sigma,T^{*}\Sigma)

such that

(1)

For any cylindrical end
\varphi_{\pm}^{j}:{\mathbb{R}}^{\pm}\times S^{1}_{t}\to\Sigma
at
z_{\pm}^{j}
, it is holomorphic with respect to
j
near infinity where
{\mathbb{R}}^{\pm}\times S^{1}
is equipped with the standard complex structure.

(2)

Near the infinity of the cylindrical end around
z_{\pm}^{j}
, we have
(\varphi_{\pm}^{j})^{*}\alpha=H_{\pm}^{j}dt
where
H_{\pm}^{j}
is either a nondegenerate 1-periodic Hamiltonian or zero. In the former resp. latter case we say this end is of
Floer type
resp.
Morse type
.

(3)

Near the infinity of the cylindrical end around
z_{\pm}^{j}
, the almost complex structure
J_{z}
coincides with a fixed almost complex structure
J_{\pm}^{j}
on
X
.

A cylindrical surface
\Sigma
equipped with a Floer datum
\sigma
is called a
Floer domain
, which we still denote by
\Sigma
for brevity.

Given a Floer domain
(\Sigma,\sigma=(j,J,\alpha))
and a smooth map
u:\Sigma\to X
, one can take the form

\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}u:=(du+X_{\sigma}(u))^{0,1}\in\Omega^{0,1}(\Sigma,u^{*}TX)

where
X_{\sigma}
is defined via

\omega(X_{\sigma},-)=d_{X}\alpha,

where
d_{X}
is the differentiation along the
X
direction, and
(\cdot)^{0,1}
is the projection onto the
(0,1)
-part determined by the domain complex structure
j
and the target almost complex structure
J
.

10.2.
Multimodules from smooth domains

Now we can define a particular kind of multimodules coming from Floer domains. Let
\Sigma
be a connected Floer domain with
m
negative ends and
n
positive ends such that all cylindrical ends are of Floer type (see Definition
10.2
), with limiting Hamiltonians
H_{1},\ldots,H_{m}
on the
m
negative ends and
H_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,H_{n}^{\prime}
on the positive ends. Let
J_{1},\ldots,J_{m}
be the limiting almost complex structures on the negative ends and
J_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,J_{n}^{\prime}
be the limiting almost complex structures on the positive ends. Let
{\mathbb{F}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{F}}_{m}
resp.
{\mathbb{F}}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,{\mathbb{F}}_{n}^{\prime}
be Floer flow category associated to
(H_{1},J_{1}),\ldots,(H_{m},J_{m})
resp.
(H_{1}^{\prime},J_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(H_{n}^{\prime},J_{n}^{\prime})
. Then the Floer domain
\Sigma
defines a multimodule
{\mathbb{X}}^{\Sigma}
over
({\mathbb{F}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{F}}_{m};{\mathbb{F}}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,{\mathbb{F}}_{n}^{\prime})
. More precisely, given any tuple
{\mathfrak{p}}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m})
of objects of
{\mathbb{F}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{F}}_{m}
and
{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}=(p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{n}^{\prime})
of objects of
{\mathbb{F}}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,{\mathbb{F}}_{n}^{\prime}
, consider the space of solutions to

u:\Sigma\to X,\ \overline{\partial}_{\sigma}u=0

which are asymptotic to
p_{i}
near the
i
-th negative end and to
p_{j}^{\prime}
near the
j
-th positive end. We compactify this space by allowing sphere bubbles and Floer trajectory breakings, and denote the compactified space by

M_{{\mathfrak{p}};{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}^{{\mathbb{X}}^{\Sigma}}.

Then there are natural structural maps

M_{p_{i}q_{i}}^{{\mathbb{F}}_{i}}\times M_{p_{1}\cdots p_{i-1}q_{i}p_{i+1}\cdots p_{m};{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}^{{\mathbb{X}}^{\Sigma}}\to M_{{\mathfrak{p}};{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}^{{\mathbb{X}}^{\Sigma}}

and

M_{{\mathfrak{p}};p_{1}^{\prime}\cdots p_{j-1}^{\prime}q_{j}^{\prime}p_{j+1}^{\prime}\cdots p_{n}^{\prime}}^{{\mathbb{X}}^{\Sigma}}\times M_{q_{j}^{\prime}p_{j}^{\prime}}^{{\mathbb{F}}_{j}^{\prime}}\to M_{{\mathfrak{p}};{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}^{{\mathbb{X}}^{\Sigma}}.

It is straightforward to see that the collection of moduli spaces
M_{{\mathfrak{p}};{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}^{{\mathbb{X}}^{\Sigma}}
and the above maps satisfy the requirement for a multimodule over
({\mathbb{F}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{F}}_{m};{\mathbb{F}}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,{\mathbb{F}}_{n}^{\prime})
. Moreover, both
{\mathbb{F}}_{i}
and
{\mathbb{F}}_{j}^{\prime}
have a free
\Pi
-action, hence
\prod{\mathbb{F}}_{i}
resp.
\prod{\mathbb{F}}_{j}^{\prime}
has a free action by
\Pi^{m}
resp.
\Pi^{n}
. Then it is easy to see that the multimodule
{\mathbb{X}}^{\Sigma}
is equivariant with respect to the diagonal embeddings
\Pi\to\Pi^{m}
and
\Pi\to\Pi^{n}
. We summarize the properties of this multimodule.

Situation Module(i)
.

(X,\omega)
is a compact symplectic manifold.
{\mathbb{F}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{F}}_{m};{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}

16
16

16

Here
m
is allowed to be
0
, the situation of the cigar bimodule described in Subsubsection
10.3.6
.
are Floer flow categories associated to pairs
(H_{1},J_{1}),\ldots,(H_{m},J_{m});(H^{\prime},J^{\prime})
.
{\mathbb{X}}
is the multimodule over
({\mathbb{F}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{F}}_{m};{\mathbb{F}})
associated to a smooth Floer domain
\Sigma^{\mathbb{X}}
with
m
negative cylindrical ends and one positive cylindrical end. Moreover, the Floer datum on
\Sigma^{\mathbb{X}}
coincides with
(H_{j}dt,J_{j})
on the
j
-th negative end and
(H^{\prime}dt,J^{\prime})
on the positive end.

Proposition 10.3
.

In Situation
Module(i)
, the multimodule
{\mathbb{X}}
is a
\Pi
-equivariant Novikov multimodule over
({\mathbb{F}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{F}}_{m};{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime})
.

We give examples of a few concrete cases which appear naturally in applications.

10.2.1.
Bimodules for continuation maps

The first one is the bimodule coming from the continuation map construction. Given Floer flow categories
\mathbb{F}^{\pm}
associated to a pair
(H_{\pm},J_{\pm})
on the same symplectic manifold
(X,\omega)
, a homotopy of the Floer data

(j_{0},H_{s}dt,J_{s,t}),\quad\quad\quad\lim_{s\to\pm\infty}H_{s}=H_{\pm},\ \lim_{s\to\pm}J_{s,t}=J_{\pm}

over the infinite cylinder, where
j_{0}
is the standard complex structure on
\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}
, is a special case of Floer data over an arbitrary punctured sphere. Hence one obtains an
({\mathbb{F}}^{-},{\mathbb{F}}^{+})
-module, usually called the bimodule of continuation maps.

There is a special one corresponding to the constant Floer datum on the infinite cylinder.

Definition 10.4
.

The
diagonal bimodule
of a Floer flow category
\mathbb{F}
associated to
(H,J)
is the bimodule for continuation maps associated to the
s
-independent Floer data
(j_{0},Hdt,J)
on the infinite cylinder. It is denoted by
\mathbb{\Delta}^{\mathbb{FF}}
.

10.2.2.
Poincaré duality multimodule

Consider a surface
\Sigma^{\rm PD}
with two negative cylindrical ends and no positive ends. Let
\sigma^{\rm PD}
be a Floer datum on
\Sigma^{\rm PD}
which converges to
H_{1}dt
and
H_{2}dt
on the two negative ends. Let
{\mathbb{F}}_{1}
and
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
be the Hamiltonian Floer flow categories associated to
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
. On the other hand, consider the trivial flow category
{\mathbb{O}}
whose objects are elements of
\Pi
and the only morphisms are identities. Then we describe a multimodule
{\mathbb{X}}^{\rm PD}
over
({\mathbb{F}}_{1},{\mathbb{F}}_{2};{\mathbb{O}})
. Given objects
p_{1}\in{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{F}}_{1}
,
p_{2}\in{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
, and
a\in\Pi\cong{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{O}}
, consider the moduli spaces of maps
u:\Sigma^{\rm PD}\to X
solving
\overline{\partial}_{\sigma^{\rm PD}}u=0
which converges to the (uncapped) orbits
\underline{\smash{p}}_{1}
and
\underline{\smash{p}}_{2}
at the two negative ends; moreover, the spherical class obtained by adding the cappings has symplectic area
a\in\Pi
. We compactify the space by allowing sphere bubbles as well as Floer breakings at the two negative ends, and denote the compactified space by

M_{p_{1}p_{2};a}^{{\mathbb{X}}^{\rm PD}}.

Then there are natural maps

M_{p_{1}q_{1}}^{{\mathbb{F}}_{1}}\times M_{q_{1}p_{2};a}^{{\mathbb{X}}^{\rm PD}}\to M_{p_{1}p_{2};a}^{{\mathbb{X}}^{\rm PD}}

and

M_{p_{2}q_{2}}^{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}\times M_{p_{1}q_{2};a}^{\mathbb{X}^{\rm PD}}\to M_{p_{1}p_{2};a}^{{\mathbb{X}}^{\rm PD}}

making it a multimodule over
({\mathbb{F}}_{1},{\mathbb{F}}_{2};{\mathbb{O}})
. We call it a
Poincaré duality multimodule
.

10.3.
PSS type bimodules

In order to establish the relation between the Floer homology and the integral homology of the symplectic manifold, one typically considers the PSS (Piunikhin–Salamon–Schwarz
[
55
]
) maps. In the current situation, we need to consider a few similar constructions. In terms of the formal terms, we describe bimodules among the three flow categories: Floer, Morse, and Pearly flow categories.

10.3.1.
Deformed Morse-to-Morse bimodule

Let
(f,g)
be a Morse–Smale pair on the almost Kähler manifold
(X,\omega,J)
. Let
\underline{\smash{\mathbb{M}}}
be the Morse flow category associated to
(f,g)
whose objects are critical points of
f
. Let
{\mathbb{M}}
be the trivial product of
\underline{\smash{\mathbb{M}}}
with
\Pi
. Instead of the identity bimodule from
{\mathbb{M}}
to itself, we consider a bimodule involving holomorphic spheres. Indeed, for each
a\in\Pi
, consider the space of
J
-holomorphic maps
u:S^{2}\to X
representing the class
a
with two fixed marked points
z_{-},z_{+}
. Denote its Gromov compactification by
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{0,2}^{\rm parametrized}(X,J,a)
, consisting of genus zero stable
J
-holomorphic spheres with two marked points with energy
a
with a rigid component. Let the two markings be
z_{-}
and
z_{+}
. There are two evaluation maps

{\rm ev}_{\pm}:\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{0,2}^{\rm parametrized}(X,J,a)\to X.

Then for each pair of ojects
x=(a_{x},\underline{\smash{x}}),y=(b_{y},\underline{\smash{y}})\in{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{M}}
, define

M_{x;y}^{\mathbb{MM}}:={\rm ev}_{-}^{-1}(\overline{W_{\underline{\smash{x}}}^{\rm us}})\cap{\rm ev}_{+}^{-1}(\overline{W_{\underline{\smash{y}}}^{\rm st}})\cap\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{0,2}^{\rm parametrized}(X,J,a_{x}-b_{y}).

There are obvious structural maps coming from breaking of Morse trajectories:

\displaystyle\ M_{xz}^{\mathbb{M}}\times M_{z;y}^{\mathbb{MM}}\to M_{x;y}^{\mathbb{MM}},\

\displaystyle\ M_{x;w}^{\mathbb{MM}}\times M_{wy}^{\mathbb{M}}\to M_{x;y}^{\mathbb{MM}}

forming a bimodule over
({\mathbb{M}},{\mathbb{M}})
. We call it the
deformed Morse-to-Morse bimodule
, denoted by
\mathbb{\Delta}^{\mathbb{MM}}
.

10.3.2.
Floer-to-Morse and Morse-to-Floer bimodules

We describe two bimodules relating the Morse flow category
{\mathbb{M}}
and the Floer flow category
{\mathbb{F}}
, which will be denoted by
{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{MF}}
and
\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{FM}}
. Fix a compact almost complex manifold
(X,\omega,J)
. Let
\underline{\smash{\mathbb{M}}}
be the Morse flow category associated to a Morse–Smale pair
(f,g)
on
X
whose objects are critical points of
f
. Let
{\mathbb{M}}
be the trivial product of
\underline{\smash{\mathbb{M}}}
with
\Pi
. Let
{\mathbb{F}}
be the Floer flow category associated to a nondegenerate Hamiltonian
H
.

Let
\Sigma^{\mathbb{MF}}
be
{\mathbb{C}}
with
\infty
viewed as a positive end with canonical cylindrical coordinates. Choose a Hamiltonian connection
\sigma^{\mathbb{MF}}
which is equal to
Hdt
near
\infty
and which is zero near
0
. Then for each object
p\in{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{F}}
, one can consider the equation

u:\Sigma^{\mathbb{MF}}\to X,\ \overline{\partial}_{\sigma^{\mathbb{MF}}}u=0,

such that
u
is asymptotic to the 1-periodic orbit underlying
p
and the map
u
represents the capped orbit
p
. Compactify this space by allowing sphere bubblings and Floer trajectory breakings on the positive ends. Denote the compactified moduli space by
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{p}^{\mathbb{MF}}
. There is an evaluation map

{\rm ev}_{0}:\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{p}^{\mathbb{MF}}\to X

at the point
0\in\Sigma^{\mathbb{MF}}
. Then for each pair of objects
x\in{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{M}}
,
p\in{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{F}}
, if
x=(a_{x},\underline{\smash{x}})
where
a_{x}\in\Pi
and
\underline{\smash{x}}\in{\rm crit}f
, then define

M_{x;p}^{\mathbb{MF}}:={\rm ev}_{0}^{-1}(\overline{W_{\underline{\smash{x}}}^{\rm us}})\cap\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{(-a_{x})\cdot p}^{\mathbb{MF}}.

Here
W_{\underline{\smash{x}}}^{\rm us}
is the unstable manifold of
\underline{\smash{x}}
and
\overline{W_{\underline{\smash{x}}}^{\rm us}}
is its compactification. Then there are natural structural maps

\displaystyle\ M_{xy}^{\mathbb{M}}\times M_{y;p}^{\mathbb{MF}}\to M_{x;p}^{\mathbb{MF}},\

\displaystyle\ M_{x;q}^{\mathbb{MF}}\times M_{qp}^{\mathbb{F}}\to M_{x;p}^{\mathbb{MF}}

defining a bimodule
\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{MF}}
over
({\mathbb{M}};{\mathbb{F}})
.

Similarly, if we let
\Sigma^{\mathbb{FM}}
be
{\mathbb{C}}
with
\infty
viewed as a negative end and if we choose a Hamiltonian connection
\sigma^{\mathbb{FM}}
on this domain which is equal to
Hdt
on the cylindrical end, then we can define moduli spaces
M_{p;x}^{\mathbb{FM}}
similarly, giving a bimodule
{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{FM}}
over
({\mathbb{F}};{\mathbb{M}})
.

10.3.3.
The Pearly-to-Floer and Floer-to-Pearly bimodules

Suppose we have fixed data for the Pearly flow category
{\mathbb{P}}
and the Floer flow category on the almost Kähler manifold
(X,\omega,J)
. In particular, one has a Morse–Smale pair
(f,g)
. Then for the moduli space of solutions to

\overline{\partial}_{\sigma^{\mathbb{MF}}}u=0,\ u:\Sigma^{\mathbb{MF}}\to X

we can take the “pearly compactification” in the same way as defining the pearly flow category. Indeed, we treat
0\in\Sigma^{\mathbb{MF}}
as a special marked point around which there is the standard cylindrical coordinates
(s,t)
. When compactifying the moduli space, sphere bubbles at
0
are taken only modulo translation in
s
. Then one can insert gradient segments to obtain the corresponding pearly compactification. For each pair of objects
x\in{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{P}}={\rm Ob}{\mathbb{F}}
,
p\in{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{F}}
, there is the moduli space
M_{x;p}^{\mathbb{PF}}
together with structural maps

\displaystyle\ M_{xy}^{\mathbb{P}}\times M_{y;p}^{\mathbb{PF}}\to M_{x;p}^{\mathbb{PF}},\

\displaystyle\ M_{x;q}^{\mathbb{PF}}\times M_{qp}^{\mathbb{F}}\to M_{x;p}^{\mathbb{PF}}

giving a bimodule
{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{PF}}
over
({\mathbb{P}},{\mathbb{F}})
. Similarly, one also has the Floer-to-Pearly bimodule
{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{FP}}
.

10.3.4.
Morse-to-Pearly and Pearly-to-Morse bimodules

We describe a bimodule
\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{PM}}
over
({\mathbb{P}},{\mathbb{M}})
and a bimodule
{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{MP}}
over
({\mathbb{M}},{\mathbb{P}})
. For simplicity, we assume
{\mathbb{M}}
and
{\mathbb{P}}
share the same Morse–Smale pair, though we can certainly take different ones.

Given a pair of objects
x=(a_{x},\underline{\smash{x}})\in{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{P}}
and
y=(b_{y},\underline{\smash{y}})\in{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{M}}
, consider the moduli space of
J
-holomorphic maps
u:{\mathbb{R}}\times S^{1}
of symplectic area
a_{x}-b_{y}\in\Pi
such that

u(-\infty)\in W_{\underline{\smash{x}}}^{\rm us},\ u(+\infty)\in W_{\underline{\smash{y}}}^{\rm st}.

When compactifying these moduli spaces, the bubbling at negative ends are taken modulo only the translation in
{\mathbb{R}}
while bubbling at positive ends are taken modulo both translation and rotations; moreover, we also insert gradient segments at nodes connecting
-\infty
and the principal component. In this way, one obtains a compact stratified space

M_{x;y}^{\mathbb{PM}}

with structural maps

\displaystyle\ M_{xw}^{\mathbb{P}}\times M_{w;y}^{\mathbb{PM}}\to M_{x;y}^{\mathbb{PM}},\

\displaystyle\ M_{x;w}^{\mathbb{PM}}\times M_{wy}^{\mathbb{M}}\to M_{x;y}^{\mathbb{PM}}

giving a bimodule
{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{PM}}
over
({\mathbb{P}},{\mathbb{M}})
. Similarly one has a bimodule
{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{MP}}
over
({\mathbb{M}},{\mathbb{P}})
by taking “half pearly” compactifications at the positive end instead of the negative one.

10.3.5.
Pearly-to-Pearly bimodule

We describe a bimodule
\mathbb{\Delta}^{\mathbb{PP}}
over
(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{P})
where
\mathbb{P}
is associated to a Morse–Smale pair
(f,g)
on
(X,\omega,J)
. Given a pair of critical points
\underline{\smash{x}},\underline{\smash{y}}\in{\rm crit}f
and
a\in\Pi
, consider the space of
J
-holomorphic maps
u:{\mathbb{R}}\times S^{1}\to X
with symplectic area
a
such that
u(-\infty)\in W_{\underline{\smash{x}}}^{\rm us}
and
u(+\infty)\in W_{\underline{\smash{y}}}^{\rm st}
. We take the “pearly compactification,” namely, when sphere bubbles at
\pm\infty
, the bubbles are taken modulo only the
{\mathbb{R}}
-translation. Moreover, we insert (possibly broken) gradient segments at nodes connecting the distinguished marked points
\pm\infty
. Denote the compactified moduli space by

\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{\underline{\smash{x}};\underline{\smash{y}}}^{\mathbb{PP}}(X,J,a).

A typical configuration of this moduli space is shown in Figure
3
.

Figure 3.
Configurations in
\mathbb{\Delta}^{\mathbb{PP}}
.

Then for each pair of objects
x=(\underline{\smash{x}},a_{x}),y=(\underline{\smash{y}},a_{y})
of
\mathbb{P}
, define

M_{x;y}^{\mathbb{PP}}:=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\underline{\smash{x}}\underline{\smash{y}}}^{\mathbb{PP}}(X,J,a_{x}-a_{y}).

Then there are natural maps

\displaystyle\ M_{xx^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{P}}\times M_{x^{\prime};y}^{\mathbb{PP}}\to M_{x;y}^{\mathbb{PP}},\

\displaystyle\ M_{x;y^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{PP}}\times M_{y^{\prime}y}^{\mathbb{P}}\to M_{x;y}^{\mathbb{PP}}

defining the structural maps of a bimodule over
(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{P})
. We call this bimoulde the
Pearly-to-Pearly bimodule
, denoted by
\mathbb{\Delta}^{\mathbb{PP}}
. Notice that there is a “classical part” which corresponds to the diagonal bimodule of the Morse flow category.

10.3.6.
Cigar module

To construct a multiplicative identity of the Floer homology, we consider an analogue of the PSS moduli space where we do not have interior markings or Morse trajectories. Let
\Sigma^{\rm cigar}\cong{\mathbb{C}}
be the open Riemann surface with a cylindrical end. Let
\mathbb{F}
be the Floer flow category on
(X,\omega,J)
associated to a nondegenerate Hamiltonian
H
. On the other hand, let
{\mathbb{O}}
be the “trivial” flow category consisting of a single object
o\in{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{O}}
with self-morphism space being a single point.

Choose a Floer datum on
\Sigma^{\rm cigar}
which coincides with
H
near the infinity. Let
{\mathbb{F}}
be the Floer flow category associated to
H
. The cigar module is a bimodule
{\mathbb{B}}^{\rm cigar}
over
({\mathbb{O}},{\mathbb{F}})
whose morphism spaces consist of solutions to the Floer equation on
\Sigma
. More precisely, for each object
p\in{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{F}}
, consider the space of solutions to

u:\Sigma^{\rm cigar}\to X,\ \overline{\partial}_{\sigma^{\rm cigar}}u=0

such that
u
converges to the 1-periodic orbit
\underline{\smash{p}}
underlying
p
and such that
u
represents the capped orbit
p
. Moreover, compactify this moduli space by allowing sphere bubbling and Floer trajectory breakings. Let the compactified space be
M_{o;p}^{\rm cigar}
. Then there are structural maps

M_{o;q}^{\rm cigar}\times M_{qp}^{\mathbb{F}}\to M_{o;p}^{\rm cigar},

making a bimodule over
({\mathbb{O}};{\mathbb{F}})
.

10.4.
Concatenations

We describe the situation corresponding to concatenating Floer data on different domains.

Situation Concatenation(i)
.

(X,\omega)
is a compact symplectic manifold.
{\mathbb{F}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{F}}_{m},{\mathbb{G}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{G}}_{n},{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime}
are Floer flow categories associated to pairs
(H_{1},J_{1}),\ldots,(H_{m},J_{m}),(G_{1},I_{1}),\ldots,(G_{n},I_{n}),(G^{\prime},I^{\prime})
respectively.
{\mathbb{X}}
resp.
{\mathbb{Y}}
is a multimodule over
({\mathbb{F}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{F}}_{m};{\mathbb{G}}_{i})
resp. over
({\mathbb{G}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{G}}_{n};{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime})
associated to smooth Floer domain
\Sigma^{\mathbb{X}}
resp.
\Sigma^{\mathbb{Y}}
as described in Situation
Module(i)
.

Proposition 10.5
.

In Situation
Concatenation(i)
, the concatenation
{\mathbb{X}}\circ_{i}{\mathbb{Y}}
is a
\Pi
-equivariant Novikov multimodule over
({\mathbb{G}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{G}}_{i-1},{\mathbb{F}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{F}}_{m},{\mathbb{G}}_{i+1},\ldots,{\mathbb{G}}_{n};{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Straightforward.
∎

11.
Homotopies of Multimodules

In this section we descibe several geometrically defined bimodule homotopies. As we only use bimodule homotopies to show certain chain maps are canonically defined up to homotopy, we will not provide the most general treatment. Hence, we only give a minimal descriptions of bimodules which will be used in this paper.

11.1.
Homotopies from varying Floer data

Recall the notions of Floer data and Floer domains (Definition
10.2
).

Definition 11.1
.

A smooth 1-parameter family of Floer domain consists of the following objects.

(1)

A smooth genus zero surface
\Sigma
.

(2)

A smooth 1-parameter family of domain complex structures
j_{\tau}
,
\tau\in[0,1]
, such that there exists an open and precompact region
\Sigma_{0}\subseteq\Sigma
and diffeomorphisms

\phi_{i}^{\pm}:{\mathbb{R}}_{\pm}\times S^{1}\to U_{i}^{\pm}\subseteq\Sigma\setminus\Sigma_{0},\ i=1,\ldots,l_{\pm},

where
U_{i}^{\pm}
are all open subsets of
\Sigma\setminus\Sigma_{0}
, such that
\phi_{i}^{\pm}
is biholomorphic with respect to all
j_{\tau}|_{U_{i}^{\pm}}
. Each
U_{i}^{\pm}
is called a positive/negative cylindrical end. Two cylindrical ends are germ-equivalent if they differ by a translation.

(3)

A smooth 1-parameter family of Hamiltonian connections
\sigma_{\tau}
on
\Sigma
such that on each
U_{i}^{\pm}
, using the cylindrical coordinates provided by
\phi_{i}^{\pm}
, we have
\sigma_{\tau}=H_{i}^{\pm}dt
near
\infty
, where
H_{i}^{\pm}
is a nondegenerate 1-periodic Hamiltonian on
X
.

(4)

A smooth 1-parameter family of domain-dependent,
\omega
-compatible almost complex structures
J_{\tau}
on
X
such that on each
U_{i}^{\pm}
, using the cylindrical coordinates,
J_{\tau}=J_{i}^{\pm}
near
\infty
where
J_{i}^{\pm}
is a fixed almost complex structure on
X
.

Denote such a 1-parameter family of Floer domains by
\Sigma_{t}^{\mathbb{H}}
, where
{\mathbb{H}}
stands for homotopy.

Given
\Sigma^{\mathbb{H}}
, which has the family of incoming Floer flow categories

{\mathbb{F}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{F}}_{m}

and the collection of outgoing Floer flow categories

{\mathbb{F}}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,{\mathbb{F}}_{n}^{\prime}.

For each pair of collections of objects

\displaystyle\ p_{j}\in{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{F}}_{j},\ j=1,\ldots,m,\

\displaystyle\ p_{i}^{\prime}\in{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{F}}_{i}^{\prime},\ i=1,\ldots,n

we consider the moduli space

M_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p_{1}^{\prime}\cdots p_{n}^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{H}}

which is the Gromov–Floer compactification of

\left\{(\tau,u)\ \left|\ \begin{array}[]{l}\tau\in[0,1],u:\Sigma\to X,\ \overline{\partial}_{\sigma_{\tau}}u=0,\\
\displaystyle\lim_{s\to-\infty}u|_{U_{j}}(s,t)=p_{j},j=1,\ldots,m,\\
\displaystyle\lim_{s\to+\infty}u|_{U_{i}^{\prime}}=p_{i}^{\prime},\ i=1,\ldots,n\end{array}\right.\right\}.

This is a compact topological space stratified by a regular poset
A_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p_{1}^{\prime}\cdots p_{n}^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{H}}
.

Notice that the subset of pairs
(\tau,u)
with
\tau=0
resp.
\tau=1
defines a multimodule
{\mathbb{X}}_{0}
resp.
{\mathbb{X}}_{1}
over
({\mathbb{F}}_{1},\cdots,{\mathbb{F}}_{m};{\mathbb{F}}_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,{\mathbb{F}}_{n}^{\prime})
. Then there are natural structural maps

M_{p_{j}q_{j}}^{{\mathbb{F}}_{j}}\times M_{p_{1}\cdots p_{j-1}q_{j}p_{j+1}\cdots p_{m};p_{1}^{\prime}\cdots p_{i-1}^{\prime}q_{i}^{\prime}p_{i+1}^{\prime}\cdots p_{n}^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{H}}\times M_{q_{i}^{\prime}p_{i}^{\prime}}^{{\mathbb{F}}_{i}^{\prime}}\to M_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p_{1}^{\prime}\cdots p_{n}^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{H}}

and

M_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p_{1}^{\prime}\cdots p_{n}^{\prime}}^{{\mathbb{X}}_{0}}\sqcup M_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p_{1}^{\prime}\cdots p_{n}^{\prime}}^{{\mathbb{X}}_{1}}\to M_{p_{1}\cdots p_{m};p_{1}^{\prime}\cdots p_{n}^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{H}}.

One can then check that
{\mathbb{H}}
together with these structural maps defines a homotopy from
{\mathbb{X}}_{0}
to
{\mathbb{X}}_{1}
, enriched in
\underline{\smash{\bf Top}}
.

We will mainly consider the case when
n=1
. We summarize the setting in the following situation.

Situation Homotopy(i)
.

(X,\omega)
is a compact symplectic manifold.
\Sigma_{t}^{\mathbb{H}},t\in[0,1]
is a smooth 1-parameter family of Floer domains with
m
negative cylindrical ends and
1
positive cylindrical end.
{\mathbb{H}}
is a homotopy from the multimodule
{\mathbb{X}}_{0}
over
({\mathbb{F}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{F}}_{m};{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime})
defined by
\Sigma_{0}^{\mathbb{H}}
to the multimodule
{\mathbb{X}}_{1}
defined by
\Sigma_{1}^{\mathbb{H}}
.

11.2.
Homotopies from gluing

Recall the notion of multimodule concatenations. Let
\Sigma^{\mathbb{X}}
and
\Sigma^{\mathbb{Y}}
be Floer domains defining Floer multimodules
{\mathbb{X}}
and
{\mathbb{Y}}
which can be composed to a multimodule
{\mathbb{X}}\circ_{i}{\mathbb{Y}}
. We assume that there is only one breaking on the surface
\Sigma^{\mathbb{X}}\circ_{i}\Sigma^{\mathbb{Y}}
, see Figure
4
. Then depending on choosing the cylindrical ends on both sides of that breaking, there is a canonical way to construct a 1-parameter family of Floer domains
\Sigma^{{\mathbb{X}}\circ_{S}{\mathbb{Y}}}
for
S\in[S_{0},+\infty)
, where
S
parametrizes the neck-length, such that when
S\to+\infty
, the Floer domain degenerates to the nodal
\Sigma^{\mathbb{X}}\circ_{i}\Sigma^{\mathbb{Y}}
. Denote this family by
\Sigma^{\mathbb{H}}
. Choose a smooth function
\tau\mapsto S
which sends
\tau=0
to
S_{0}
and
\tau=1
to
\infty
. Let
\Sigma^{\tau}
be the corresponding Floer domain. For
\tau\in[0,1)
, let
{\mathbb{X}}\circ_{S(\tau)}{\mathbb{Y}}
be the multimodule coming from the Floer domain
\Sigma^{\tau}
.

Then for any collection of incoming objects
{\mathfrak{p}}^{-}
and outgoing objects
{\mathfrak{p}}^{+}
, one has the moduli space
M_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{-}{\mathfrak{p}}^{+}}^{\mathbb{H}}
, which is the natural compactification of

\Big\{(\tau,u)\ |\ \tau\in[0,1],\ u:\Sigma^{\tau}\to X,\ \overline{\partial}_{\sigma^{\tau}}u=0,\ \lim_{s\to\pm\infty}u|_{U_{\pm}^{i}}(s,t)=p_{i}^{\pm}\Big\}.

Figure 4.
When breaking along the dotted line, the multimodule becomes the concatenation of two.

As usual, we have the natural structural maps

M_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{-}{\mathfrak{q}}^{-}}^{{\mathfrak{F}}^{-}}\times M_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{-}{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}}^{\mathbb{H}}\times M_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}{\mathfrak{p}}^{+}}^{{\mathfrak{F}}^{+}}\to M_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{-}{\mathfrak{p}}^{+}}^{{\mathbb{H}}}

and

M_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{-}{\mathfrak{p}}^{+}}^{{\mathbb{X}}\circ_{i}{\mathbb{Y}}}\sqcup M_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{-}{\mathfrak{p}}^{+}}^{{\mathbb{X}}\circ_{S_{0}}{\mathbb{Y}}}\to M_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{-}{\mathfrak{p}}^{+}}^{\mathbb{H}}.

These structural maps provide a homotopy
{\mathbb{H}}
from
{\mathbb{X}}\circ_{S_{0}}{\mathbb{Y}}
to
{\mathbb{X}}\circ_{i}{\mathbb{Y}}
.

Situation Glue(i)
.

(X,\omega)
is a compact symplectic manifold.
{\mathbb{X}}
and
{\mathbb{Y}}
are Floer multimodules as described in Situation
Concatenation(i)
.
\Sigma_{t}^{\mathbb{H}}
,
t\in[0,1]
is a smooth family of Floer domains such that for
t>0
,
\Sigma_{t}^{\mathbb{H}}
is smooth and
\Sigma_{0}
is the concatenation
\Sigma^{\mathbb{X}}\#\Sigma^{\mathbb{Y}}
.
{\mathbb{M}}
is the Floer multimodule associated to the Floer domain
\Sigma_{1}^{\mathbb{H}}
.
{\mathbb{H}}
is the homotopy between
{\mathbb{X}}\circ_{i}{\mathbb{Y}}
and
{\mathbb{M}}
.

11.3.
Homotopies associated to PSS type moduli spaces

On a symplectic manifold, consider the three geometric flow categories: a Floer flow category
{\mathbb{F}}
, a Morse flow category
{\mathbb{M}}
, and a pearly flow category
{\mathbb{P}}
. Consider the following diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathbb{\Delta}}^{\mathbb{FF}}}
\scriptstyle{{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{FM}}}
\scriptstyle{{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{FP}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathbb{\Delta}}^{\mathbb{MM}}}
\scriptstyle{{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{MF}}}
\scriptstyle{{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{MP}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathbb{\Delta}}^{\mathbb{PP}}}
\scriptstyle{{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{PF}}}
\scriptstyle{{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{PM}}}

(11.1)

where the arrows are bimodules. Notice that we can compose bimodules in this diagram following the arrows. We say a triangle in this diagram is
commutative
if the composed bimodule from two edges is homotopic to the third bimodule. We will explain 1) why the concatenation
{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{FP}}\circ{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{PF}}
resp.
\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{FP}}\circ\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{PF}}
is homotopic to
{\mathbb{\Delta}}^{\mathbb{FF}}
resp.
\mathbb{\Delta}^{\mathbb{PP}}
, whose construction actually can show all triangle whose concatenation happens at
{\mathbb{F}}
or
{\mathbb{P}}
commutes, 2) why the concatenation
\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{MP}}\circ\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{PM}}
is homotopic to
\mathbb{\Delta}^{\mathbb{PP}}
, and 3) why the concatenation
{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{FM}}\circ{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{MF}}
resp.
\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{PM}}\circ\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{MP}}
is not (at least not obviously) homotopic to
{\mathbb{\Delta}}^{\mathbb{FF}}
resp.
\mathbb{\Delta}^{\mathbb{MM}}
.

11.3.1.
Floer-to-Pearly, Pearly-to-Floer bimodules, and their concatenations

Choose a 1-parameter family of Floer data on the infinite cylinder, parametrized by
\tau\in[0,1)
, given by a Hamiltonian connection
\sigma_{\tau}
supported in a region of length about
-1/\log\tau
when
\tau
is close to
1
, and converges to zero when
\tau\to 0
, and such that when
\tau
is close to
1
, over the long cylinder it is equal to
Hdt
where
H
is a nondegenerate 1-periodic Hamiltonian. Given a pair
x=(\underline{\smash{x}},a_{x}),y=(\underline{\smash{y}},a_{y})
of objects of
{\mathbb{P}}
, consider the moduli space

\left\{(\tau,u)\ \left|\ \begin{array}[]{c}\tau\in[0,1),\ u:{\mathbb{R}}\times S^{1}\to X,\ \overline{\partial}_{\sigma_{\tau}}u=0,\\
\displaystyle\lim_{s\to-\infty}u(s,t)\in W^{\rm us}_{\underline{\smash{x}}},\ \lim_{s\to+\infty}u(s,t)\in W^{\rm st}_{\underline{\smash{y}}},\ \omega(u)=a_{x}-a_{y}\end{array}\right.\right\}.

We consider the pearly compactification at
\pm\infty
and meanwhile allowing
\tau\to 1
, which means the map
u
should break along Hamiltonian orbits as
-1/\log\tau\to\infty
(see Figure
5
, where the neck length
-1/\log\tau
is denoted by
S
).

Figure 5.
The homotopy from
\mathbb{\Delta}^{\mathbb{PP}}
to
\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{FP}}\circ\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{PF}}
.

Then the restriction to the
\tau=1
slice gives moduli spaces of the bimodule concatenation
\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{FP}}\circ\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{PF}}
(concatenation at Floer flow category). On the other hand, the restriction to the
\tau=0
slice gives moduli spaces of the bimodule
\mathbb{\Delta}^{\mathbb{PP}}
. Denote this homotopy by

{\mathbb{H}}^{\mathbb{PF}\tilde{\circ}\mathbb{FP}}

The concatenation at the Pearly flow category and its homotopy to
\mathbb{\Delta}^{\mathbb{FF}}
is a bit more complicated. It is the concatenation of two homotopies.

First, consider the infinite cylinder equipped with a 1-parameter family of Floer data, parametrized by
\tau\in[0,1)
such that when
\tau=0
, the Hamiltonian connection is the flat one
Hdt
, which gives rise to the diagonal bimodule
\mathbb{\Delta}^{\mathbb{FF}}
, while as
\tau
increases, there is a long cylinder of length growing to
\infty
on which the Hamiltonian connection is trivial. Then for each pair of objects
p,p^{\prime}\in{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{F}}
, consider the space

\left\{(\tau,u)\left|\begin{array}[]{cc}\tau\in[0,1),\ u:{\mathbb{R}}\times S^{1}\to X,\ \overline{\partial}_{\sigma_{\tau}}u=0,\\
\displaystyle\lim_{s\to-\infty}u(s,t)=p,\ \lim_{s\to+\infty}u(s,t)=p^{\prime}\end{array}\right.\right\}

We compactify it naturally by adding sphere bubbles, Floer trajectory breakings at both negative and positive ends, and allowing cylindrical breakings when
\tau\to 1
