is different from previous cases. Indeed, consider the moduli space of parametrized holomorphic cylinders

u:{\mathbb{R}}\times S^{1}\to\mathbb{CP}^{d}

of degree
d
. When compactifying this space, the bubbling near the negative and positive ends is treated differently. Near the negative ends, the broken pieces are considered modulo only the real translation; near the positive end, the broken pieces are considered modulo
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, i.e., translations and rotations. The domain of a point in the compactification still has two distinguished points:
z_{-}
and
z_{+}
. Then let
B_{d}^{\mathbb{PM}}
be the subspace in the compactification satisfying 1) the image is not contained in any hyperplane and 2) the map satisfies the positive:

u(z_{+})=[0,0,\ldots,0,1]\in{\mathbb{P}}^{d}.

The structural map of this bimodule only contains the negative part as we view it as a bimodule with one side the trivial flow category: namely, given
d,d^{\prime}
, there is a morphism

{\mathcal{C}}_{d}\times{\mathcal{C}}_{d^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{PM}}\to{\mathcal{C}}_{d+d^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{PM}}.

The construction is similar to before and hence skipped. One can check that the collection of equivariant families of curves
\mathcal{C}_{d}^{\mathbb{PM}}
defines a monotone bimodule.

21.3.2.
Cylinders in
X

We would like to construct monotone bimodules enriched in
\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm NC}(X)
. For the Morse side, there is still the trivial monotone flow category with a single object whose only morphism space is the singleton, i.e., the triple

(X,{\rm Id},{\rm Id})

where
X
is regarded as a normally complex derived orbifold. On the other hand, one has constructed the lift
\widetilde{\mathbb{cyl}}(X)
of
\mathbb{cyl}(X)
to
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}^{\rm NC}_{\rm rig}(X)
. By considering parametrized cylinders in
X
, one can easily formulate a monotone bimodule

\mathbb{cyl}^{\mathbb{PM}}(X)

over
(\mathbb{cyl}(X);\mathbb{o})
consisting of moduli spaces of parametrized cylinders in
X
with two marked points where the compactification at negative and positive marked points is taken differently.

Proposition 21.8
.

Given an AMS lift
\widetilde{\mathbb{cyl}}(X)
of the outercollaring
{}_{\lfloor}\mathbb{cyl}(X)
, there exists a lift of the outercollaring
{}_{\lfloor}\mathbb{cyl}{}^{\mathbb{PM}}(X)
to
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}^{\rm NC}_{\rm rig}(X)
, denoted by
\widetilde{\mathbb{cyl}}{}^{\mathbb{PM}}(X)
, as a monotone bimodule over
(\widetilde{\mathbb{cyl}}(X);\mathbb{o})
.

Sketch of Proof.

It is completely parallel to the proof of Proposition
21.5
.
∎

Corollary 21.9
.

Given an AMS lift
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
of the pearly flow category
{\mathbb{P}}
subject to a fixed outercollaring width and an integral symplectic form, there exists a lift
\tilde{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{PM}}
resp.
\tilde{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{MP}}
of the corresponding collaring of
\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{PM}}
resp.
\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{MP}}
to
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm NC}
as a bimodule over
(\tilde{\mathbb{P}};{}_{\lfloor}\mathbb{M})
resp. over
({}_{\lfloor}{\mathbb{M}};\tilde{\mathbb{P}})
. Moreover, any two such lifts are homotopic.

Proof.

We work over the domain bimodule
\widetilde{\mathbb{cyl}}{}^{\mathbb{PM}}(X)
(and in parallel
\widetilde{\mathbb{cyl}}{\mathbb{MP}}(X)
). Extend thickening data used in the construction of
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
to thickening data over
\widetilde{\mathbb{cyl}}{\mathbb{PM}}(X)
. The construction of NC structures and stable smoothing can all be reproduced in the same way as before. This provides the AMS lifts of outercollarings of
\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{PM}}
and
\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{MP}}
.
∎

21.4.
Pearly-to-Floer and Floer-to-Pearly bimodules

The AMS constructions for the bimodules
\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{FP}}
and
\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{PF}}
are slight variants of that for
\mathbb{\Delta}^{\mathbb{PP}}
. We describe in detail the construction for
\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{PF}}
.

21.4.1.
A new category of fibered spaces

In order to deal with the mixture of fiber products (in the Pearly part) and direct products (in the Floer part), we introduce the space
X^{+}=X\sqcup\{{\rm pt}\}
which is a smooth manifold of mixed dimensions. Then similar to Definition
21.1
, one can consider the category
\underline{\smash{\bf Top}}(X^{+})
whose objects are spaces with continuous maps to
X^{+}
, as well as the categories
\underline{\smash{\bf Kur}}(X^{+})
and
\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}(X^{+})
. Then there are natural functors

\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 13.7361pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-13.7361pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\underline{\smash{\bf Top}}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 40.41243pt\raise-23.76886pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 51.79166pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 95.84721pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\underline{\smash{\bf Top}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 67.014pt\raise-23.76886pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-32.23112pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 37.7361pt\raise-32.23112pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\underline{\smash{\bf Top}}(X^{+})}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 98.34721pt\raise-32.23112pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}}.

Similar functors exist for Kuranishi spaces or derived orbifolds.

Now consider the flow category
\mathbb{Cyl}(X)
(obtained from the monotone flow category
\mathbb{cyl}(X)
), which can be viewed as enriched in
\underline{\smash{\bf Top}}(X^{+})
. Similarly, the Floer flow category
{\mathbb{F}}
associated to a Hamiltonian
H
and an almost complex structure
J
is also enriched in
\underline{\smash{\bf Top}}(X^{+})
. Then the Floer data used for defining the bimodules
\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{PF}}
resp.
\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{FP}}
also induces a bimodule

\mathbb{Cyl}^{\mathbb{PF}}(X)\ {\rm resp.}\ \mathbb{Cyl}{}^{\mathbb{FP}}(X)

over
(\mathbb{Cyl}(X);\mathbb{F})
resp. over
(\mathbb{F};\mathbb{Cyl}(X))
.

Proposition 21.10
.

Given an AMS lift
\widetilde{\mathbb{Cyl}}(X)
of
{}_{\lfloor}\mathbb{Cyl}(X)
to
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm NC}(X)
and an AMS lift
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}
of
{}_{\lfloor}\mathbb{F}
to
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm NC}
, there exists a lift (called an AMS lift) of the outercollaring
{}_{\lfloor}\mathbb{Cyl}{}^{\mathbb{PF}}(X)
resp.
{}_{\lfloor}\mathbb{Cyl}{}^{\mathbb{FP}}(X)
to
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}{}_{\rm rig}^{\rm NC}(X^{+})
, denoted by
\widetilde{\mathbb{Cyl}}{}^{\mathbb{PF}}(X)
resp.
\widetilde{\mathbb{Cyl}}{}^{\mathbb{FP}}(X)
.

Sketch of Proof.

There is no essential difference from the previous cases.
∎

Corollary 21.11
.

Given an AMS lift
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
of the outercollaring of the pearly flow category
\mathbb{P}
and an AMS lift
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}
of the Floer flow category, there exists a lift of
{}_{\lfloor}\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{PF}}
resp.
{}_{\lfloor}\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{FP}}
to
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm NC}
.

Proof.

It follows from the same arguments as we have seen many times, building on Proposition
21.10
. The only difference is that we should insert Morse flow lines on the pearly side to complete the construction.
∎

21.5.
Morse-to-Floer and Floer-to-Morse bimodules

Now we consider the AMS lift of the bimodules
\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{MF}}
and
\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{FM}}
, which are responsible for the PSS and SSP maps claimed in Theorem
E
. The claim of such AMS lifts is stated as Theorem
S
. As the PSS map on the homology level is supposed to be canonical, we also need to compare different choices.

Proof of Theorem
S
.

We sketch the construction for
\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{MF}}
. The other case has no difference other than a simple reversal. See
[
14
, Section 7]
for a more explicit discussion in a slightly different framework.

By removing the Morse flow ray, the Morse-to-Floer bimodule
{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{MF}}
becomes the cigar bimodule
\mathbb{B}^{\rm cigar}
, which is enriched in
{\rm Top}(X^{+})
. Recall that it is a bimodule over
({\mathbb{O}};{\mathbb{F}})
where
{\mathbb{O}}
is the
\Pi
-covering of the trivial flow category. Then the AMS construction is similar to that for the cigar bimodule
\mathbb{B}^{\rm cigar}
. When construct an AMS lift
\tilde{\mathbb{B}}^{\rm cigar}
to
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm NC}
(which is covered by Theorem
P
), especially for smoothing, one can make the evaluation maps at the incoming marked point smooth submersions onto
X
. Hence one obtains a lift, still denoted by
\tilde{\mathbb{B}}^{\rm cigar}
, enriched in
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm NC}(X^{+})
. Then by adding gradient rays, one obtains a lift
\tilde{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{MF}}
to
\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm NC}
as a bimodule over
({}_{\lfloor}\mathbb{M};\tilde{\mathbb{F}})
.

For the second item of Theorem
S
, one also needs to compare different choices of integral actions. This can be done using the double-framed construction introduced in Section
20
in a similar way as proving Proposition
12.3
. We omit the details.
∎

Proof of Theorem
12.6
.

For the concatenation
\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{FF}^{\prime}}\circ\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{F^{\prime}}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}
at the Floer flow category and its homotopy to
\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{F}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}
, the corresponding AMS construction is similar to what is required for establishing the naturality of the continuation map. For the concatenation
\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{FM}}\circ\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{MM}^{\prime}}
at the Morse flow category
\mathbb{M}
, the construction is even simpler. We omit the details.
∎

21.6.
Proof of Theorem
T

We have constructed the lifts of flow categories and bimodules listed in the statement of Theorem
T
. We choose a common outercollaring width and a compatible set of integral actions. To finish the proof of Theorem
T
, one needs to establish corresponding lifts of homotopies involving certain concatenations and gluings. Notice that we only glue at the Floer flow category or the pearly flow category, but not the Morse flow category. We need to construct such homotopies for many triangles of the diagram (
12.1
). However, the constructions for each triangle have no substantial differences. Hence we only give proofs for two typical cases.

21.6.1.
Concatenation at the Floer flow category

We prove the following statement.

Lemma 21.12
.

Fix an outercollaring width and an integral action
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{F}}^{\Omega}
which is compatible with all involved Floer data. Let
\hat{\mathbb{F}}
be an AMS lift of
{\mathbb{F}}
subject to the outercollaring width and
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{F}}^{\Omega}
. Let
\hat{\mathbb{P}}
be an AMS lift of the pearly flow category subject to the same outercollaring width and the integral symplectic form
\Omega
. Let
\hat{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{PF}}
be an AMS lift of
{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{PF}}
and
\hat{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{FP}}
be an AMS lift of
\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{FP}}
. Let
\hat{\mathbb{\Delta}}^{\mathbb{PP}}
be an AMS lift of the diagonal bimodule. Then there exists a concatenation
\hat{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{PF}}\circ\hat{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{FP}}
and a homotopy from this concatenation to
\hat{\mathbb{\Delta}}^{\mathbb{PP}}
.

Proof.

The construction of a concatenation
\hat{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{PF}}\circ\hat{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{FP}}
is no different from other situations involving concatenating at a Floer flow category. The construction of the lift of the homotopy to
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf SKur}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm NC}
also only differs in notation.
∎

21.6.2.
Concatenation at the pearly flow category

The concatenation at the pearly flow category is slightly more complicated. We consider the reversed concatenation
{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathbb{FP}}\circ\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{PF}}
.

Notice that the homotopy between the concatenation
\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{FP}}\circ\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{PF}}
and
\mathbb{\Delta}^{\mathbb{FF}}
is the union of two parts: the first part is when there are gradient flow segments of a positive total length (where the length is a parameter for the homotopy); the second part is when there are no gradient segments. See Figure
6
for an illustration. The boundary between the two parts can be viewed as another concatenation

\mathbb{Cyl}^{\mathbb{FP}}(X)\circ\mathbb{Cyl}^{\mathbb{PF}}(X)

at the flow category
\mathbb{Cyl}(X)
. Notice that one has constructed AMS lifts
\widehat{\mathbb{Cyl}}^{\mathbb{FP}}(X)
and
\widehat{\mathbb{Cyl}}^{\mathbb{PF}}(X)
to
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf SKur}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm NC}
. The concatenation can be constructed in a straightforward way. Then we extend the concatenation in the two directions. One direction is the gluing, which has no essential new features for this case. The other direction is to insert gradient flow segments. This is similar to the cases of
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{\Delta}^{\mathbb{PP}}
. We omit the details.

Part IV
EQUIVARIANT FLOER THEORY AND STEENROD OPERATIONS

Outline of Part 4

Note that we would like to switch to the cohomological convention. The Floer equation will remain the same. However, it will be the equation for the ascending gradient flow equation of the action functional (which differs from the one used previously by a negative sign). Hence the differential increases the action functional. The grading is also changed to the cohomological one so that the pair-of-pants product is degree-preserving. Moreover, we make use of the coherent orientations without explicitly mentioning them.

22.
Morse Theory on Classifying Spaces

The Borel
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-equivariant cohomology of a point with
{\bf F}_{\sf p}
-coefficient is the ring

H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}^{\rm Borel}({\rm pt};{\bf F}_{\sf p})\cong H^{*}(B{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}};{\bf F}_{\sf p})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\bf F}_{\sf p}[\mathtt{U},\uptheta]/\langle\uptheta^{2}=0\rangle,&\ {\sf p}>2;\\
{\bf F}_{2}[\uptheta],&\ {\sf p}=2.\end{array}\right.

where
\mathtt{U}
has degree
2
while
\uptheta
has degree
1
. One usually considers the completion by allowing formal power series rather than polynomials in
\mathtt{U}
and
\uptheta
. We denote the completion by

\widetilde{\bf K}_{{\sf p},0}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\bf F}_{\sf p}{[\![\mathtt{U}]\!]}\langle\uptheta\rangle,&\ {\sf p}>2,\\
{\bf F}_{2}{[\![\uptheta]\!]},&\ {\sf p}=2.\end{array}\right.

By inverting
\mathtt{U}
for
p\geq 3
and inverting
\uptheta
for
p=2
, one get the Laurent version

\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\bf F}_{\sf p}{(\!(\mathtt{U})\!)}\langle\uptheta\rangle,&\ {\sf p}>2,\\
{\bf F}_{2}{(\!(\uptheta)\!)},&\ {\sf p}=2.\end{array}\right.

This ring is not the cohomology of any natural topological space. However, it can be realized as the cohomology of a certain flow category which will be convenient for establishing the Tate equivariant theories. We will also use the notation

{\bf K}_{\sf p}:={\bf F}_{\sf p}{(\!(\mathtt{U})\!)}

for
{\sf p}>2
, dropping
\uptheta
, in later discussions.

22.1.
An equivariant Morse flow category

We provide a concrete Morse model for equivariant cohomology of a point. We will use this Morse model throughout all the equivariant constructions.

22.1.1.
{\sf p}=2
case

Consider the “real
S^{\infty}
”

S^{\infty}_{\mathbb{R}}:=\Big\{\eta=(\eta_{0},\eta_{1},\ldots)\ |\ \eta_{l}\in{\mathbb{R}},\ \eta_{l}=0\text{ for }\forall l\gg 0,\ \sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\eta_{l}^{2}=1\Big\}

which is a contractible infinite CW complex. The group
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
acts freely by changing
\eta_{l}
to
-\eta_{l}
with quotient being
\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}
, which is a model for the classifying space of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
. As
S_{\mathbb{R}}^{\infty}
is the colimit of finite-dimensional spheres, we can define notion of smoothness in the naive way. For example, a function on
S_{\mathbb{R}}^{\infty}
is smooth if its restriction to each truncation
S^{k}\subseteq S_{\mathbb{R}}^{\infty}
is smooth.

Consider the function

f^{\mathbb{E}}:S_{\mathbb{R}}^{\infty}\to{\mathbb{R}},\ f^{\mathbb{E}}(\eta)=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}l\eta_{l}^{2}.

Its trunctions to each
S^{k}
is a Morse function. The critical point set is

{\rm crit}f^{\mathbb{E}}=\{\eta=\pm w_{l}\ |\ \eta_{j}=\pm\delta_{jl},\ l=0,1,\ldots\}.

It is easy to see that the standard metric
g^{\mathbb{E}}
on
S^{\infty}
makes it Morse–Smale. Define the grading by

|\pm w_{l}|=l.

The Tate equivariant theory suggests to consider a formal “negative extension” of
S_{\mathbb{R}}^{\infty}
. Define

\widehat{S}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\infty}=\Big\{\eta=(\eta_{l})_{l\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\ |\ \eta_{l}\in{\mathbb{R}},\ \eta_{l}=0\text{ for }\forall|l|\gg 0,\ \sum_{l\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\eta_{l}^{2}=1\Big\}.

This is no longer a CW complex; however, all structures should be understood in the sense of limits/colimits. For each pair of nonnegative integers
m,n
, there is a finite truncation

S^{m,n}_{\mathbb{R}}:=\Big\{\eta\in\widehat{S}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\infty}\ |\ l<-m\ {\rm or}\ l>n\Longrightarrow\eta_{l}=0\Big\}.

By abuse of notation, we can extend the Morse function to

f^{\mathbb{E}}:\widehat{S}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\infty}\to{\mathbb{R}},\ f^{\mathbb{E}}(\eta)=\sum_{l=-\infty}^{\infty}l\eta_{l}^{2}.

Then with respect to the standard Riemannian metric, the pair is Morse–Smale on any such finite truncation.

One can formally write down an equivariant Morse flow category
{\mathbb{E}}
as follows. The set of objects
{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{E}}
is the set of critical points of
f^{\mathbb{E}}
on
\widehat{S}^{\infty}_{\mathbb{R}}
. Between
x,y\in{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{E}}
, one has the morphism space being the compactified moduli space
M_{xy}^{\mathbb{E}}
of gradient flow lines from
x
to
y
. Then this is a flow category enriched in
\underline{\smash{\bf SMan}}
. This flow category satisfies the unobstructedness condition (Definition
7.1
) and local finiteness condition (Definition
7.2
) with respect to the function
f^{\mathbb{E}}
.

Moreover, the flow category is obviously equivariant with respect to a natural Novikov group
{\mathbb{Z}}
action. Here on
{\mathbb{Z}}
we define both the energy and the index function by

\lambda(n)=\mu(n)=n.

The associated Novikov field is

\Lambda_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}^{\mathbb{Z}}:={\mathbb{F}}_{2}{(\!(\uptheta)\!)}=\tilde{\bf K}_{2}.

The
{\mathbb{Z}}
-action on
{\mathbb{E}}
is induced by the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-action on
\widehat{S}^{\infty}_{\mathbb{R}}

n\cdot(\eta_{l})_{l\in{\mathbb{Z}}}:=(\eta_{l-n})_{l\in{\mathbb{Z}}}

which satisfies

\displaystyle f^{\mathbb{E}}(n\cdot\eta)=f^{\mathbb{E}}(\eta)+\lambda(n),\

\displaystyle\ |n\cdot\eta|=|\eta|+\mu(n).

In characteristic
2
, one does not need orientation. Then by the abstract construction of Floer complexes (see Subsection
7.2
and Proposition
7.30
), one obtains a differential on

C({\mathbb{E}}):=CM(f^{{\mathbb{E}}})=\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}a_{i}w_{i}\ |\ a_{i}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2},\ w_{i}\in{\rm crit}f^{\mathbb{E}},\ \lim_{i\to\infty}f^{{{\mathbb{E}}}}(w_{i})=+\infty\Big\}

which is a
\widetilde{\bf K}_{2}
-module.

One can compute the cohomology easily. For between any two critical points of
f^{\mathbb{E}}
of adjacent indices, there is exactly one gradient trajectory between them. Hence in each degree
l
,
C^{l}({\mathbb{E}})
is 2-dimensional while the cohomology is trivial.

However, this trivial cohomology is not what we need. Notice that the function
f^{\mathbb{E}}
(as well as the metric) is invariant under the natural
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-action on
\widehat{S}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\infty}
. One can consider the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-invariant part

C({\mathbb{E}})_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}\subseteq C({\mathbb{E}})

which is 1-dimensional in each degree. The differential is trivial in characteristic
2
, hence the cohomology is also 1-dimensional. Therefore, one has

H(C({\mathbb{E}})_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}})\cong\Lambda_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}^{{\mathbb{Z}}}\cong\widetilde{\bf K}_{2}

as
\widetilde{\bf K}_{2}
-modules. Later we will show that the ring structure is also identical to
\widetilde{\bf K}_{2}
.

22.1.2.
{\sf p}>2
case

Consider the complex version of the infinite-dimensional sphere. Denote

S_{\mathbb{C}}^{\infty}=\Big\{\eta=(\eta_{0},\eta_{1},\ldots)\ |\ \eta_{l}\in{\mathbb{C}},\ \eta_{l}=0\text{ for }\forall l\gg 0,\ \sum_{l=0}^{\infty}|\eta_{l}|^{2}=1\Big\}.

This is a well-defined CW complex and is the universal
S^{1}
-bundle over
\mathbb{CP}^{\infty}
, a model for the classifying space of
U(1)
. By regarding
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
as a subgroup of
S^{1}
,
S_{\mathbb{C}}^{\infty}
is also the universal
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-bundle over
B{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
.

Now we define the formal negative extension

\widehat{S}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\infty}:=\Big\{\eta=(\eta_{l})_{l\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\ |\ \eta_{l}\in{\mathbb{C}},\ \eta_{l}=0\text{ for }\forall|l|\gg 0,\ \sum_{l\in{\mathbb{Z}}}|\eta_{l}|^{2}=1\Big\}.

The
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-action on
S_{\mathbb{C}}^{\infty}
extends to
\widehat{S}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\infty}
. Moreover, there is a free
{\mathbb{Z}}
-action on
\widehat{S}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\infty}
by

n\cdot(\eta_{l})\mapsto(\eta_{l-n}).

(22.1)

Define an
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-invariant function
f^{{\mathbb{E}}}:\widehat{S}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\infty}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

f^{{\mathbb{E}}}(\eta)=\left(\sum_{l\in{\mathbb{Z}}}l|\eta_{l}|^{2}\right)+\epsilon\sum_{l\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\rm Re}(\eta_{l}^{{\sf p}}),

(22.2)

where
\epsilon
is a sufficiently small positive number. It is a smooth function in the sense that its restriction to each finite truncation is a smooth function. It also respects the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-action (
22.1
) in the following way:

f^{{\mathbb{E}}}(n\cdot\eta)=f^{{\mathbb{E}}}(\eta)+n.

If we set
\epsilon=0
,
f^{{\mathbb{E}}}
is a Morse–Bott function with critical submanifolds being the circles

S_{l}^{1}=\{\eta\in S^{\infty}\ |\ |\eta_{l}|=1\}.

After turning on
\epsilon
, the circle
S_{l}^{1}
splits into nondegenerate critical points

w_{2l}^{0},\ldots,w_{2l}^{{\sf p}-1},\ w_{2l+1}^{0},\ldots,w_{2l+1}^{{\sf p}-1}

where the subindices indicate the number of positive eigenvalues of the Hessian. One can explicitly write down the coordinates of critical points. For the ones with even degrees, we have

w_{2l}^{0}=(0,\ldots,0,1,0,\ldots),\ w_{2l}^{s}=\tau^{s}w_{2l}^{0},\ s=1,\ldots,{\sf p}-1,

where the only nonzero coordinate of
w_{2l}^{0}
is the
l
-th one and
\tau=e^{\frac{2\pi{\bf i}}{{\sf p}}}
is the generator of
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
. For the ones with odd degrees, we have

w_{2l+1}^{0}=(0,\ldots,0,e^{\frac{\pi{\bf i}}{{\sf p}}},0,\ldots,0),\ w_{2l+1}^{s}=\tau^{s}w_{2l+1}^{0},\ s=1,\ldots,{\sf p}-1.

If we define the degree of
w_{k}^{m}
be
k
, then the action by
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
in (
22.1
) shifts the grading by
2n
.

Lemma 22.1
.

There exists a Riemannian metric
g^{{\mathbb{E}}}
on
\widehat{S}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\infty}
satisfying the following conditions.

(1)

g^{{\mathbb{E}}}
is
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-invariant and invariant under the shift map (
22.1
).

(2)

(f^{{\mathbb{E}}},g^{{\mathbb{E}}})
is Morse–Smale (when restricted to any finite truncation of
\widehat{S}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\infty}
).

(3)

If we consider the Morse complex associated to
(f^{{\mathbb{E}}},g^{{\mathbb{E}}})
(restricted to any sufficiently large finite truncation of
\widehat{S}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\infty}
), then the Morse differential, in terms of generators, is

\displaystyle\ d(w_{2l}^{i})=w_{2l+1}^{i}-w_{2l+1}^{i+1},\

\displaystyle\ d(w_{2l-1}^{i})=w_{2l}^{0}+\cdots+w_{2l}^{{\sf p}-1}.

Proof.

See
[
62
, Section 4]
which treated the case on
S_{\mathbb{C}}^{\infty}
. One can then use the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-action to extend the metric to the negative part of
\widehat{S}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\infty}
. The expression of the differential can also be found in
[
59
, Section 2]
, and we refer the reader to
[
12
, Appendix A]
for a comparison between the Morse model and the cellular model used in
[
59
]
.
∎

Therefore, one can define a flow category
{{\mathbb{E}}}
whose objects are critical points of
f^{{\mathbb{E}}}
and whose morphism spaces are the moduli space of (broken) trajectories between critical points. One can make
{{\mathbb{E}}}
a
{\mathbb{Z}}\rtimes{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-equivariant Novikov flow category. Indeed, define

\displaystyle\ \lambda:{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{R}},\ \lambda(a)=a,\

\displaystyle\ \mu:{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{Z}},\ \mu(a)=2a.

They are
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-invariant. Hence the triple
({{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}\times{\mathbb{Z}},\lambda,\mu)
is a Novikov group (Definition
7.21
). Next, one can make the Morse flow category
{{\mathbb{E}}}
associated to
(f^{{\mathbb{E}}},g^{{\mathbb{E}}})
a
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}
-equivariant Novikov flow category. Define the action and grading by

\displaystyle\ {\mathcal{A}}(p)=f^{{\mathbb{E}}}(p),\

\displaystyle\ |w_{k}^{s}|=k.

Then one can see

\displaystyle\ {\mathcal{A}}(ap)=f^{{{\mathbb{E}}}}(p)+\lambda(a),\

\displaystyle\ |ap|=|p|+\mu(a).

Then, by the general construction of complexes in Section
7
, there is an associated
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded complex

C({\mathbb{E}}):=CM(f^{{\mathbb{E}}})=\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}a_{i}w_{i}\ |\ a_{i}\in{\bf F}_{\sf p},\ w_{i}\in{\rm crit}f^{{\mathbb{E}}},\ \lim_{i\to\infty}{\mathcal{A}}(w_{i})=+\infty\Big\},

which is a graded module over
\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}^{\mathbb{Z}}\cong{\bf K}_{\sf p}
.

One can compute the chain complex easily from Lemma
22.1
. One can see that the total cohomology of
C({\mathbb{E}})
is trivial. If we consider the
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-invariant part, then in degree
k
, one has

C^{k}({{\mathbb{E}}})_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}={\bf F}_{\sf p}\langle w_{k}^{0}+\cdots+w_{k}^{{\sf p}-1}\rangle

and the differential is trivial in characteristic
{\sf p}
. Hence the cohomology of the
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-fixed part is identified with

H(C({\mathbb{E}})_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}})\cong\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}

as
{\bf K}_{\sf p}
-modules. We will identify below its multiplicative structure using Morse flow trees.

22.1.3.
Equivariant cohomology of free orbits

We consider a simple case of equivariant localization. Let
A
be a finite set equipped with a
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-action. Then one has a trivial product
A\times{\mathbb{E}}
as a flow category whose object set is
A\times{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{E}}
. Then one can form the complex

C(A\times{\mathbb{E}}):=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}C({\mathbb{E}})

which has a
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-action.

Lemma 22.2
.

Let
A_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}\subseteq A
be the subset of
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-fixed points. Then

H(C(A\times{\mathbb{E}})_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}})\cong\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}^{A_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}}.

Proof.

One only needs to consider the cases when
A
contains a single element and when
A
is a
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-torsor. In the former case, the cohomology is
\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}
. In the latter case, one can explicitly calculate the differential and show that the cohomology is trivial.
∎

22.2.
Ring structure using Morse model

We define the Morse-theoretic multiplicative structure on
H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}({\rm pt})\cong\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}
. In both
{\sf p}=2
and
{\sf p}>2
cases, we denote by
S^{\infty}
the real or complex infinite-dimensional sphere and
\widehat{S}^{\infty}
its negative extension.

Let
H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}^{\rm Borel}({\rm pt})
be the Borel equivariant cohomology in
{\bf F}_{\sf p}
-coefficients, which can be obtained from the above Morse model by truncating at the nonnegative levels, i.e., via a Morse flow category on
S^{\infty}\subseteq\widehat{S}^{\infty}
. We will use Morse flow trees in
S^{\infty}
to define a ring structure on
H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}^{\rm Borel}({\rm pt})
and then extend to the Tate version.

Let
T_{2,1}
denote the tree which is the disjoint union of two incoming edges
T_{1}\cong T_{2}\cong(-\infty,0]
and an outgoing edge
T_{\infty}\cong[0,+\infty)
. Let
s\in T_{2,1}
denote the coordinate on each edge; when there is no ambiguity,
s
corresponds to a real number. To achieve transversality, we choose a
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-invariant family of vector fields
Y_{s}
on
S^{\infty}
parametrized by
s\in T_{2,1}

(1)

Y(s)=0
when
|s|\gg 0
.

(2)

For each finite truncation
S^{n}\subseteq S^{\infty}
,
Y|_{S^{n}}
is tangent to
S^{n}\subseteq S^{\infty}
and
\nabla Y
is sufficiently small in the normal direction of
S^{n}
.

Let
Y_{i}
denote the restriction of
Y
to
T_{i}\subseteq T_{2,1}
. A
Y
-perturbed flow tree
is a triple
\eta=(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{\infty}):T_{2,1}\to S^{\infty}
which solves the equation

\eta^{\prime}(s)=\nabla f^{{\mathbb{E}}}(\eta(s))+Y_{s}(\eta(s))

subject to the incidence relation

\eta_{1}(0)=\eta_{2}(0)=\eta_{\infty}(0).

Each flow tree converges to a triple of critical points of
g
at
\infty
of the edges.

Given a triple of critical points
w_{1},w_{2},w_{\infty}\in{\rm crit}(f^{{\mathbb{E}}})\cap S^{\infty}
, let
S^{n}\subseteq S^{\infty}
be the smallest truncation which contains all of them. One can see that a flow tree connecting
w_{1},w_{2},w_{\infty}
is contained in
S^{n}
. We say that a
Y
-perturbed flow tree is regular for
(w_{1},w_{2},w_{\infty})
if it is regular as a flow tree contained in
S^{n}
.
26
26

26

In fact if it is regular in
S^{n}
, then it is regular in any larger truncation.

For a generic perturbation
Y
and each triple of critical points
w_{1},w_{2},w_{\infty}
, one obtains a regular moduli space of solutions, whose mod
p
count induces a
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-equivariant map

CM(f^{{\mathbb{E}}}|_{S^{\infty}})\otimes CM(f^{{\mathbb{E}}}|_{S^{\infty}})\to CM(f^{{\mathbb{E}}}|_{S^{\infty}}).

Hence after restriction to the
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-invariant part, one obtains

H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}^{\rm Borel}({\rm pt})\otimes_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}^{\rm Borel}({\rm pt})\cong CM(f^{{\mathbb{E}}}|_{S^{\infty}})^{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}\otimes_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}CM(f^{{\mathbb{E}}}|_{S^{\infty}})^{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}\to CM_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(f^{{\mathbb{E}}}|_{S^{\infty}})^{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}\cong H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}^{\rm Borel}({\rm pt}).

The usual Morse theoretic technique can be used to show that the bilinear map is independent of the choices of
Y
, and satisfies associativity. Hence
H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}^{\rm Borel}({\rm pt})
is equipped with a structure of a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded, associative and supercommutative algebra over
{\bf F}_{\sf p}
.

We know for compact manifolds, the ring structure defined by counting Morse flow trees coincides with the ordinary cohomology ring. Applying this fact to any finite truncation
S^{n}
, one can see the above Morse-theoretic definition of the ring structure coincides with the ordinary one on

H^{\rm Borel}_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}({\rm pt})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\bf F}_{\sf p}{[\![\uptheta]\!]},&\ {\sf p}=2,\\
{\bf F}_{\sf p}{[\![\mathtt{U}]\!]}\langle\uptheta\rangle,&\ {\sf p}>2\end{array}\right.

By inverting
\uptheta
or
\mathtt{U}
, one obtains a ring structure on the Tate equivariant cohomology of a point, which coincides with the standard one on
\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}
.

Later we will construct cohomology groups which become modules over
\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}
. The module structure is always defined via a certain Morse-theoretic construction first as a module over the positive truncation, then extended by inverting the corresponding variable
\uptheta
or
\mathtt{U}
.

23.
Equivariant Morse and Floer Cohomology

The purpose of this section is to discuss both an algebraic and Morse-theoretic approach to equivariant cohomology. The former is used for constructing the quasi-Frobenius map (cf. Equation (
23.3
)) and the latter uses the Morse flow category discussed in the previous section to define the
\uptheta
-action on equivariant cohomology.

23.1.
Tate Equivariant Cohomology

23.1.1.
The algebraic construction

Fix a prime
{\sf p}
. The coefficient ring will be
{\bf F}_{\sf p}
unless otherwise declared. Let
D
be a
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-equivariant cochain complex with differential
d_{D}
. Let
\tau:D\to D
be the action of the generator
\tau\in{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
. Define cochain complexes of
{\bf K}_{\sf p}
-modules

C_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(D):=D\otimes\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}D\otimes{\bf F}_{2}{(\!(\uptheta)\!)},&\ {\sf p}=2,\\
D\otimes{\bf F}_{\sf p}{(\!(\mathtt{U})\!)}\langle\uptheta\rangle,&\ {\sf p}>2.\end{array}\right.

where the differential
d_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}
is defined as follows.

(1)

When
{\sf p}=2
,
d_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}
is
\uptheta
-linear and is determined by

d_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(x\otimes\uptheta^{k})=d_{D}(x)\otimes\uptheta^{k}+(1-\tau)(x)\otimes\uptheta^{k+1}.

(23.1)

(2)

When
{\sf p}>2
,
d_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}
is
\mathtt{U}
-linear and is determined by

\begin{split}d_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}(x\otimes 1)&\ =d_{D}(x)\otimes 1+(-1)^{|x|}(1-\tau)(x)\otimes\uptheta,\\
d_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}(x\otimes\uptheta)&\ =d_{D}(x)\otimes\uptheta+(-1)^{|x|}(1+\tau+\cdots+\tau^{p-1})(x)\otimes\mathtt{U}.\end{split}

(23.2)

Here
|x|
is the cohomological grading of
x
.

The resulting cohomology, called the
(Tate) equivariant cohomology
of
D
, is denoted by

H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(D)

which is naturally a
{\bf K}_{\sf p}
-module.
In fact the equivariant cohomology
H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(D)
can be made a module over
\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}
in a non-obvious way (unless
{\sf p}=2
). We choose to construct the module structure in the Morse-theoretic approach. We will not use the above algebraic definition formally, but only provide it for algebraic intuition.

Remark 23.1
.

If we work with
D\otimes{\bf K}_{\sf p}
equipped with the same differentials (
23.1
) and (
23.2
), then we obtain the Borel-equivariant cohomology. We mostly stick with the Tate setting because of intended applications.

The equivariant complex is functorial. Namely, if
f:D\to D^{\prime}
is a
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-equivariant chain map, then it induces a chain map

f_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}:C_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(D)\to C_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(D^{\prime}).

If
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
are
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-equivariantly chain homotopic, then there is a corresponding
{\bf K}_{\sf p}
-linear chain homotopy from
f_{0,{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}
to
f_{1,{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}
.

There are two special cases of the above construction which will appear in this paper soon. The first special case is when the
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-action on the complex
D
is trivial. In this case

H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(D)\cong H(D)\otimes\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}.

Notice that this is a priori only an isomorphism of
{\bf K}_{\sf p}
-modules but not
\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}
-modules.

The second special case is the
{\sf p}
-fold tensor power. Let
D
be a non-equivariant cochain complex of
{\bf F}_{\sf p}
-vector spaces. The tensor power

(D^{\otimes{\sf p}},d_{D}^{\otimes{\sf p}})

has the
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-action by permuting the factors as follows: let
\tau\in{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
be the generator, then
\tau
acts on the
{\sf p}
-fold tensor product by

\tau(x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{\sf p})=(-1)^{|x_{1}|(|x_{2}|+\cdots+|x_{\sf p}|)}x_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{\sf p}\otimes x_{1}.

Then one has the chain complex

C_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(D^{\otimes{\sf p}}).

One can calculate the Tate equivariant cohomology by the so-called quasi-Frobenius map. This map is not induced from any chain-level construction. We first define the
quasi-Frobenius map

qF:H(D)\to H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(D^{\otimes{\sf p}})

(23.3)

in the case when the differential of
D
is trivial. In this case,
H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(D)\cong H(D)\otimes\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}
. Define

qF(x)=\underbrace{x\otimes\cdots\otimes x}_{{\sf p}}\in C_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(D^{\otimes{\sf p}})\ \forall x\in H(D)\subseteq H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(D).

The image is contained in the kernel of
d_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}
hence induces a map to
H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(D^{\otimes{\sf p}})
. Moreover, we can see that the cohomological map is linear over
{\bf F}_{\sf p}
.

In general, when the differential of
D
is nontrivial, there exists a
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-equivariant chain homotopy equivalence

(H(D),0)\sim(D,d_{D}).

Then we define
qF
as the composition

\textstyle{H(D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(H(D)^{\otimes{\sf p}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(D^{\otimes{\sf p}})}

Moreover, any two
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-equivariant chain homotopy equivalences between
H(D)
and
D
are equivariantly homotopic. Hence the resulting map

qF:H(D)\to H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(D^{\otimes{\sf p}})

is well-defined. Furthermore, the following functoriality property of
qF
holds.

Lemma 23.2
.

The quasi-Frobenius map is functorial. Namely, if
f:D_{1}\to D_{2}
is a cochain map, then there is a commutative diagram

\textstyle{H(D_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{qF}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(D_{1}^{\otimes{\sf p}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}
\textstyle{H(D_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{qF}
\textstyle{H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(D_{2}^{\otimes{\sf p}})}

(23.4)

This functoriality implies that the quasi-Frobenius map is an isomorphism with field coefficients.

Proposition 23.3
.

There is a
{\bf K}_{\sf p}
-linear extension of
qF
which is an isomorphism

H(D)\otimes\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}\to H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(D^{\otimes{\sf p}}).

Proof.

By the definition of
qF
and the above lemma, one only needs to verify this for the case when the differential of
D
is trivial. The fact that
qF
induces an isomorphism follows from
[
62
, Lemma 3.1]
.
∎

23.1.2.
Novikov coefficients

Now we extend the above algebraic constructions to complex of modules over Novikov fields. First recall that when
\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2}
are Novikov groups, then one has a completed tensor product

\Lambda^{\Pi_{1}}\widehat{\otimes}\Lambda^{\Pi_{2}}\cong\Lambda^{\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{2}}.

Then if
D_{1}
resp.
D_{2}
are non-Archimedean normed over
\Lambda^{\Pi_{1}}
resp.
\Lambda^{\Pi_{2}}
, then one has a completed tensor product

D_{1}\widehat{\otimes}D_{2}

which is a non-Archimedean normed vector space over
\Lambda^{\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{2}}
.

The above equivariant construction requires the following specialization. Let
(\Pi,\lambda,\mu)
be a Novikov group, which is typically the one defined by (
1.1
). In the equivariant setting we consider another standard one which is

({\mathbb{Z}},\lambda_{\sf p},\mu_{\sf p})

where
\lambda_{\sf p}:{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is the canonical inclusion while

\mu_{\sf p}(a)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}a,&\ {\sf p}=2,\\
2a,&\ {\sf p}>2\end{array}\right.

Now let
D
be a complex of
\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi}
-vector spaces. Then the completed tensor product
D\widehat{\otimes}\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}
is a vector space over the Novikov field

\Lambda^{\Pi\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}}:=\Lambda^{\Pi\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}}_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}c_{i}T^{a_{i}+b_{i}}\ \left|\ \begin{array}[]{l}c_{i}\in{\bf F}_{\sf p},\ a_{i}\in\Pi,\ b_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}},\\
\displaystyle\inf_{i}\omega(a_{i})>-\infty,\ \inf_{i}b_{i}>-\infty,\ \lim_{i+\infty}\omega(a_{i})+b_{i}=+\infty\end{array}\right.\right\}

We typically write
T^{b_{i}}
as
\uptheta^{b_{i}}
(when
{\sf p}=2
) or
\mathtt{U}^{b_{i}}
(when
{\sf p}>2
). Then this Novikov field consists of formal infinite
{\bf F}_{\sf p}
-linear combinations of monomials
T^{a_{i}}P_{i}(\uptheta)
or
T^{a_{i}}P_{i}(\mathtt{U})
where
P_{i}
is a Laurent series with bounded pole orders while
\omega(a_{i})\to+\infty
.

We can define the equivariant differential on the tensor product
D\widehat{\otimes}\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}
using the same formula as before. However, most of the time, we will enlarge the Novikov field via

\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}}\hookrightarrow\Lambda_{{\bf K}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi}

where the latter does not require bounded pole orders for the coefficient of
T^{a_{i}}
. Then define

\left(C_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(D):=D\widehat{\otimes}\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p},d_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}\right)

where the differential is still defined in the same way.

One often needs to take a more convenient field extension. Consider

\Lambda_{{\bf K}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi}

where
{\bf K}_{\sf p}
is the field of Laurent series in
\uptheta
(for
{\sf p}=2
) or
\mathtt{U}
(for
{\sf p}>2
). Elements in
\Lambda_{{\bf K}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi}
are formal infinite
{\bf F}_{\sf p}
-linear combinations of monomials
T^{a_{i}}P_{i}
where
P_{i}
is a Laurent series without bound on pole orders, or equivalently, formal infinite
{\bf K}_{\sf p}
-linear combinations of
T^{a_{i}}
with
\omega(a_{i})\to+\infty
. Then one often needs to use

C_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(D)\underset{\Lambda^{\Pi\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\otimes}\Lambda_{{\bf K}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi}.

We can also consider
C_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(D^{\otimes{\sf p}})
when
D
is a non-equivariant cochain complex of
\Lambda^{\Pi}
-modules. We consider the completed tensor power (over
{\bf F}_{\sf p}
)

\overbrace{D\widehat{\underset{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}{\otimes}}\cdots\widehat{\underset{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}{\otimes}}D}^{{\sf p}}

which is a module over

\Lambda^{\Pi^{\sf p}}:=\Lambda^{\Pi}\widehat{\underset{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}{\otimes}}\cdots\widehat{\underset{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}{\otimes}}\Lambda^{\Pi}.

Consider the natural morphism of Novikov monoids (see Definition
7.21
)

\textstyle{\Pi\oplus\cdots\oplus\Pi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Pi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{R}}^{k}}

where the vertical arrows are
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{\sf p})\mapsto a_{1}+\cdots+a_{\sf p}
. Then there is an induced ring morphism

\Lambda_{R}^{\Pi^{{\sf p}}}\to\Lambda_{R}^{\Pi}.

Denote

D^{\widehat{\otimes}{\sf p}}:=\left(\overbrace{D\widehat{\underset{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}{\otimes}}\cdots\widehat{\underset{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}{\otimes}}D}^{{\sf p}}\right)\underset{\Lambda^{\Pi^{{\sf p}}}}{\otimes}\Lambda^{\Pi}

which is a module over
\Lambda_{R}^{\mathbb{\Pi}}
. Then we have the chain complex

C_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(D^{\widehat{\otimes}{\sf p}}),

whose differential is defined similarly using (
23.1
) and (
23.2
). In this situation, we still have the quasi-Frobenius map well-defined on the cohomology level

qF:H^{*}(D)\to H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}^{*}(D^{\widehat{\otimes}{\sf p}}).

However it is only linear over
{\bf F}_{\sf p}
but not the Novikov field
\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi}
. Instead, one has Frobenius linearity in the Novikov variable

qF(T^{a}x)=T^{{\sf p}a}qF(x).

23.1.3.
Quantitative results

One considers a key quantitative property of the quasi-Frobenius map. Let
D
be a Floer-type complex over
\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi}
(Definition
7.34
). Then the tensor product

D^{\widehat{\otimes}{\sf p}}

is also a Floer-type complex over
\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi}
. The equivariant complex, after tensoring with
\Lambda_{{\bf K}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi}
, becomes a Floer-type complex over this larger Novikov field

C_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(D^{\widehat{\otimes}{\sf p}})\otimes\Lambda_{{\bf K}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi}.

Then for each
\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}
, define the filtered cohomology

H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}^{\leq\tau}:=H((C_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(D^{\widehat{\otimes}{\sf p}})\otimes\Lambda_{{\bf K}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi})^{\leq\tau}).

Via localization, one can prove the following result.

Lemma 23.4
.

For each
\tau
, there exists a quasi-Frobenius map

qF:H^{\leq\tau}(D)\to H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}^{\leq{\sf p}\tau}(D^{\otimes{\sf p}})

satisfying

(1)

For
\tau<\sigma
, the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{H^{\leq\tau}(D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{qF}
\textstyle{H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}^{\leq{\sf p}\tau}(D^{\otimes{\sf p}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{\leq\sigma}(D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{qF}
\textstyle{H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}^{\leq{\sf p}\sigma}(D^{\otimes{\sf p}})}

(2)

After tensoring with
\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}
, for each
\tau
,
qF
induces an isomorphism of
\Lambda_{{\bf K}_{\sf p},0}^{\Pi}
-modules

qF:H^{\leq\tau}(D)\otimes\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}\to H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}^{\leq{\sf p}\tau}(D^{\otimes{\sf p}}).

Proof.

See
[
62
]
.
∎

23.2.
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-equivariant Morse complexes

23.2.1.
Equivariant Morse cohomology for
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-manifolds

Let
M
be a compact oriented manifold equipped with a
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-action. Let
f:M\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-invariant Morse function. One would like to define a
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-equivariant Morse cochain complex
CM_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(f)
.

First we assume that there exists a
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-invariant Riemannian metric
g
on
M
making
(f,g)
Morse–Smale. Namely, one can achieve equivariant transversality. Then one obtains a cochain complex
CM(f,g)
which has a
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-action. Hence the previous algebraic construction provides a way to obtain an equivariant Morse complex

CM_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(f):=C_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(CM^{*}(f,g)).

In fact the equivariant complex can also be obtained via Morse model. Consider the product function

F:M\times\widehat{S}^{\infty}\to{\mathbb{R}},\ F(x,w)=f(x)+f^{\mathbb{E}}(w)

and the product metric, which provides a Morse flow category
{\mathbb{M}}\#{{\mathbb{E}}}
. Consider the associated Morse cochain complex

CM(F)

which coincides with the tensor product
CM(f)\otimes{\mathbb{E}}
. This complex has a
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-action given by the
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-action on
CM(f)
and the
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-action on
{\mathbb{E}}
. Then define

CM_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(f):=(CM(f)\otimes{\mathbb{E}})_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}

i.e., the
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-fixed part.

Lemma 23.5
.

The complex
CM_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(f)
coincides with the algebraically defined complex
C_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(D)
.

Proof.

Define a linear map

CM(f)\otimes\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}\to CM(F)_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}

as follows.

(1)

When
{\sf p}=2
, define

x\otimes\uptheta^{k}\mapsto x\otimes q_{k}^{+}+\tau(x)\otimes q_{k}^{-}

where
q_{k}^{\pm}
are the two critical points of
f^{{\mathbb{E}}}
and
\tau
is the action on
CM(f)
.

(2)

When
{\sf p}>2
, define

\begin{split}x\otimes\mathtt{U}^{k}&\mapsto\sum_{l=1}^{p}(\tau^{l}x)\otimes w_{2k}^{l},\\
x\otimes\mathtt{U}^{k}\uptheta&\mapsto\sum_{l=1}^{p}(\tau^{l}x)\otimes w_{2k+1}^{l}\end{split}

where
w_{i}^{l}
are the critical points of
f^{{\mathbb{E}}}
.

One can check that this linear map is a bijective cochain map.
∎

The two special cases mentioned above also arises in the Morse-theoretic context. First, suppose
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
acts trivially on
M
. Then a Morse–Smale pair
(f,g)
is equivariantly transverse, resulting in the identification

HM_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(f,g)\cong HM(f,g)\otimes\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}.

On the other hand, if
(f,g)
is Morse–Smale on
M
, then the
{\sf p}
-fold power

(f^{{\sf p}},g^{\oplus{\sf p}})

is Morse–Smale on
M^{{\sf p}}
. One obtains the cochain complex

CM(f^{\sf p})\otimes C({\mathbb{E}})

whose
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-invariant has cohomology
HM_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(f^{\sf p},g^{\otimes{\sf p}})
. When
M
is compact, Morse-theoretic technique proves that the resulting cohomology is independent of the choice of
(f,g)
and coincides with
H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(M^{\sf p})
.

Now assume that one does not have equivariant transversality. Let
M
be equipped with a
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-action,
f:M\to{\mathbb{R}}
is a
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-invariant Morse function. Still consider the product

F:M\times\widehat{S}^{\infty}\to{\mathbb{R}}.

Now the
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-action on
M\times\widehat{S}^{\infty}
is free, allowing us to achieve equivariant transversality. There are many concrete choices of perturbations. For example, one can perturb the function
f
to allow it to depend on
w\in\widehat{S}^{\infty}
, i.e., a
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-invariant function
(w,x)\mapsto f_{v}(x)
on
\widehat{S}^{\infty}\times M
such that
f_{v}(x)=f(x)
when
(v,x)
is close to a critical point of
g\times f
. One can further ensure that the set of critical points is unchanged under the perturbation provided that the perturbation is sufficiently small in
C^{1}
-norm. Then one can consider the coupled flow line equation for
(\xi,\eta):{\mathbb{R}}\to M\times\widehat{S}^{\infty}
:

\displaystyle\ \eta^{\prime}(s)=\nabla f_{\zeta(s)}(\eta(s)),\

\displaystyle\ \zeta^{\prime}(s)=\nabla g(\zeta(s)),

which has again an obvious translation symmetry. Define the Morse cochain complex as the (completed) tensor product

CM(f)\otimes CM(g)

whose differential counts rigid perturbed flow lines. Define

CM_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(f):=(CM(f)\otimes CM(g))^{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}

as a subcomplex, whose chain homotopy type is independent of the perturbation.

By using perturbed flow trees in
\widehat{S}^{\infty}
, one can also define a module structure of
HM_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(f)
over
\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}\cong H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}({\rm pt})
induced by a chain-level bilinear map

CM_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(f)\otimes_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}CM_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(g)\to CM_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(f).

More precisely, choose a perturbation
Y
over the tree
T_{2,1}
which can be used to define the ring structure on
\widehat{S}^{\infty}
. Choose another generic family of functions

f_{s,v}:M\to{\mathbb{R}}

parametrized by
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
v\in S^{\infty}
such that 1)
f_{s,v}=f
near
s=0
and 2)
f_{s,v}=f_{v}
for
|s|\gg 0
and 3)
f_{s,\gamma v}(\gamma x)=f_{s,v}(x)
for all
\gamma\in{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
. Consider pairs
(\xi,\eta)
where
\eta=(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},\zeta_{\infty}):T_{2,1}\to S^{\infty}
is a
Y
-perturbed flow tree and
\xi:{\mathbb{R}}\to M
solves the equation

\xi^{\prime}(s)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\nabla f_{s,\zeta_{1}(s)}(\xi(s)),&\ s\leq 0,\\
\nabla f_{s,\zeta_{\infty}(s)}(\xi(s)),&\ s\geq 0\end{array}\right.

The coefficients of the equation on
\xi
is smooth. We then impose critical point asymptotics
\xi(\pm\infty)\in{\rm crit}(f)
. By choosing a generic
f_{s,v}
, one obtains equivariant transversality and hence defines a chain map

\big(CM(f)\otimes CM(g)\big)\otimes CM(g)\to CM(f)\otimes CM(g).

Its restriction to the
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-invariant part gives

CM_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(f)\otimes CM_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(g)\hookrightarrow(CM(f)\otimes CM(g)\otimes CM(g))^{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}\to CM_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(f)

hence a bilinear map

HM_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(f)\otimes_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}\to HM_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(f).

One can further show that this map defines a
\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}
-module structure on
HM_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(f)
using a standard TQFT argument.

One can further define a multiplicative structure on
HM_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(f)
via the cochain-level map

CM_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(f)\otimes_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}CM_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(f)\to CM_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(f)

via gradient flow trees in
M\times\widehat{S}^{\infty}
, whose associativity can be verified in the standard way. Lastly, because the multiplicative structure respects the
\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}
-module structure, hence it descends to a map

HM_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(f)\underset{\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}}{\otimes}HM_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(f)\to HM_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(f).

24.
Quantum Steenrod Operations—Proof of Theorem
H

The classical Steenrod operation can be viewed as a
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-equivariant extension of the map
x\mapsto x^{\sf p}
in cohomology in characteristic
{\sf p}
. Proposed by Fukaya
[
31
]
, the quantum Steenrod operation deforms the classical one in analogy to the case that the quantum cup product deforms the classical cup product. Under topological restrictions such as monotonicity, the quantum Steenrod operation has been constructed by Seidel
[
60
]
, Wilkins
[
72
]
, and Seidel–Wilkins
[
59
]
(see also the recent survey by Wilkins
[
73
]
).

The use of the FOP transverse perturbations allows us to define integer, hence
{\bf F}_{\sf p}
-counts of pseudoholomorphic curves, leading to a definition of the quantum Steenrod operation on all compact symplectic manifolds. In this section we go over the construction using the Morse model.

24.1.
Geometric setup

24.1.1.
The flow categories and bimodules associated to quantum Steenrod operation

Fix a prime
{\sf p}
. Let
(X,\omega)
be a compact symplectic manifold and
J
be a compatible almost complex structure. We abbreviate the data
(X,\omega,J)
by
X
. We can reduce the consideration to a fixed
J
using argument such as continuation maps. Therefore we will not address the dependence on
J
. Recall

\Pi=\frac{\pi_{2}(X)}{{\rm ker}\omega\cap{\rm ker}c_{1}}.

Let
(f,g)
be a Morse–Smale pair on
X
which defines a Morse flow category
\underline{\smash{\mathbb{M}}}
. Let
{\mathbb{M}}
be the trivial product
\underline{\smash{\mathbb{M}}}\times\Pi
, which is naturally a
\Pi
-equivariant flow category. Its objects are denoted by

x=(\underline{\smash{x}},a_{x})\in{\rm Ob}\underline{\smash{\mathbb{M}}}\times\Pi.

Let
\mathbb{E}
be the
{\mathbb{Z}}\times{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-equivariant flow category associated to the Morse–Smale pair on
\widehat{S}^{\infty}
. Let
\underline{\smash{\mathbb{M}}}\#{\mathbb{E}}
be the “coupling” of
\underline{\smash{\mathbb{M}}}
and
{\mathbb{E}}
, which is a flow category with object set

{\rm Ob}(\underline{\smash{\mathbb{M}}}\#{\mathbb{E}})={\rm Ob}\underline{\smash{\mathbb{M}}}\times{\mathbb{E}}.

The morphism space between two objects is the moduli space of flow lines in
X\times\widehat{S}^{\infty}
. Denote

\mathbb{M}\#{\mathbb{E}}=(\underline{\smash{\mathbb{M}}}\#{\mathbb{E}})\times\Pi.

This flow category is equivariant with respect to the free action by

\mathbb{\Pi}_{\sf p}=\Pi\times{\mathbb{Z}}\times{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}=:{\mathbb{\Pi}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{{\sf p}}.

We describe a multimodule
{\mathbb{X}}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
over

(\underbrace{\mathbb{M},\ldots,\mathbb{M}}_{{\sf p}},{\mathbb{E}};{\mathbb{M}}\#{\mathbb{E}})

as follows. Consider the Riemann surface
\Sigma^{\mathbb{QSt}}
which is identified with
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
with
{\sf p}+1
marked points, where the
{\sf p}
negative markings are at all
{\sf p}
-th roots of unity

z_{1},\ldots,z_{\sf p}\in{\mathbb{C}}\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}\cup\{\infty\}=\mathbb{CP}^{1}

and the positive marking is
z_{\infty}=\infty
.

Consider pairs

(u,v)

where
u:\Sigma^{\mathbb{QSt}}\to X
is a
J
-holomorphic sphere in
X
with these
{\sf p}+1
special markings and
v:{\mathbb{R}}\to S^{\infty}
is a Morse trajectory of
{{\mathbb{E}}}
in
S^{\infty}
. For each tuple of objects

(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\sf p},w)\in{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{M}}\times\cdots\times{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{M}}\times{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{E}},\ p^{\prime}=(x^{\prime},w^{\prime})\in{\rm Ob}{\mathbb{M}}\#{\mathbb{E}}

let

\mathring{M}_{x_{1}\cdots x_{\sf p}w;p^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{QSt}}

be the space of such pairs
(u,v)
such that

\int_{S^{2}}u^{*}\omega=a_{x_{1}}+\cdots+a_{x_{\sf p}}-a_{x^{\prime}}

satisfying the constraints

u(z_{i})\in W^{\rm us}_{\underline{\smash{x}}_{i}},\ i=1,\ldots,{\sf p},\ u(z_{\infty})\in W^{\rm st}_{{\underline{\smash{x}}}^{\prime}},\ {\rm and}\ \lim_{s\to-\infty}v(s)=w,\ \lim_{s\to+\infty}v(s)=w^{\prime}.

Let
M_{x_{1}\cdots x_{\sf p}w;p^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
be the compactification of
\mathring{M}_{x_{1}\cdots x_{\sf p}w;p^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
compactified in the following way: at markings, one takes the ordinary Gromov compactification; the Morse breakings at negative ends are allowed independently at different edges while the breakings on the positive ends are required to be “synchronized.”

Proposition 24.1
.

The spaces
M_{x_{1}\cdots x_{\sf p}w;p^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
define a Novikov multimodule, denoted by
{\mathbb{X}}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
, over
({\mathbb{M}},\ldots,{\mathbb{M}},{{\mathbb{E}}};{\mathbb{M}}^{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}})
, which is equivariant with respect to the morphism of Novikov groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overbrace{\Pi\times\cdots\times\Pi}^{{\sf p}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{R}}^{{\sf p}}\times{\mathbb{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi\times{\mathbb{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A_{1},\ldots,A_{{\sf p}},a)\mapsto(A_{1}+\cdots+A_{{\sf p}},a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(t_{1},\ldots,t_{{\sf p}},s)\mapsto(t_{1}+\cdots+t_{{\sf p}},s)}

(24.1)

Moreover, as a weak bimodule over
({\mathbb{M}}\times\cdots\times{\mathbb{M}}\times{{\mathbb{E}}};\mathbb{M}^{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}})
, it is
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-equivariant.

Proof.

This follows from the usual description of compactifications of moduli spaces of broken Morse trajectories and our choice of Novikov group actions. Note that the sphere bubbles in the Gromov compactification do not introduce new strata.
∎

Now we state the technical theorem leading to the definition of the quantum Steenrod operation. Notice that the involved flow categories are already enriched in the category of stratified smooth manifolds, in particular, the category
\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm NC}
. Fix an outercollaring width.

Theorem 24.2

(The AMS construction for quantum Steenrod operation)
.

Fix an outercollaring width.

(1)

For any integral symplectic form
\Omega
tamed by
J
, there exists a class of lifts (called AMS lifts) of the outercollaring
{}_{\lfloor}{\mathbb{X}}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
to
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm NC}
, as a multimodule over
({}_{\lfloor}{\mathbb{M}},\cdots,{}_{\lfloor}{\mathbb{M}},{}_{\lfloor}{{\mathbb{E}}};{}_{\lfloor}{\mathbb{M}}^{{\mathbb{E}}})
, which is equivariant with respect to the morphism (
24.1
) and which is
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-equivariant as a weak bimodule.

(2)

Any two AMS lifts of
\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
are equivariantly homotopic. Namely, if
\tilde{\mathbb{X}}_{0}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
and
\tilde{\mathbb{X}}_{1}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
are both AMS lifts of
{}_{\lfloor}\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
, then there exists a
\mathbb{\Pi}_{\sf p}
-equivariant lift of the trivial homotopy from
{}_{\lfloor}{\mathbb{X}}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
to
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm NC}
.

The proof of this theorem is postponed to the end of this section. We first derive the consequence, i.e., the definition of the quantum Steenrod operation.

Remark 24.3
.

One can also prove the corresponding result for the comparison of using different
J
’s. We omit the detailed statement.

24.2.
Steenrod operations

As the flow categories involved are already transverse, they themselves can be regarded as their FOP transverse perturbations. Then by Theorem
N
, one can find a
(\Pi\times{\mathbb{Z}})\rtimes{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-equivariant FOP transverse perturbation of
\tilde{\mathbb{X}}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
, whose isotropy free locus
\mathring{\mathbb{X}}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
is a multimodule enriched in
\underline{\smash{{\bf\Psi}{\bf Man}}}
. Notice that equivariant feature of Theorem
N
guarantees that all existing symmetries can be respected by the FOP transverse perturbation.

Then by Proposition
7.18
and Proposition
7.32
, one obtains a chain map

\widetilde{\Phi}^{\mathbb{QSt}}:=\left(\left(\overbrace{C({\mathbb{M}})\widehat{\underset{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}{\otimes}}\cdots\widehat{\underset{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}{\otimes}}C({\mathbb{M}})}^{{\sf p}}\right)\widehat{\underset{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}{\otimes}}C({{\mathbb{E}}})\right)\underset{\Lambda^{\Pi^{{\sf p}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\otimes}\Lambda^{\mathbb{\Pi}}\to C({\mathbb{M}}\#{{\mathbb{E}}}).

The restriction to the
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-invariant part is denoted by
\Phi^{\mathbb{QSt}}
.

Proposition 24.4
.

Up to chain homotopy, the map
\widetilde{\Phi}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
does not depend on choices.

Proof.

This follows from Part (2) of Theorem
24.2
and the homotopy case of Theorem
N
.
∎

It reduces to a linear map between homology, which, in
{\bf F}_{\sf p}
-coefficients, reads

\Phi^{\mathbb{QSt}}:H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(C(\mathbb{M})^{\otimes{\sf p}})\to H(\mathbb{M}^{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}).

One can see that

H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(C^{*}(\mathbb{M})^{\otimes{\sf p}})\cong H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(CM(f,g)^{\otimes{\sf p}})\otimes\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi}.

Then by pre-composing with the quasi-Frobenius map (
23.3
) one obtains a map in homology

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 24.342pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-24.342pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{HM(f,g)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 28.30986pt\raise 6.07222pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.71112pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{qF}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 48.342pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 48.342pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(CM(f,g)^{\otimes{\sf p}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 155.46355pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 155.46355pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}(C({\mathbb{M}})^{\otimes{\sf p}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 222.06766pt\raise 6.20831pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.20831pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\Phi^{\mathbb{QSt}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 245.97054pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 245.97054pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H(C({\mathbb{M}}\#{\mathbb{E}}))}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Notice that the quasi-Frobenius map is compatible with continuation maps. To see that the composition is independent of the choice of the Morse–Smale pair
(f,g)
, we can concatenate the multimodule
\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
with Morse bimodules interpolating different choices of the Morse–Smale pairs, therefore we obtain the following statement using standard ideas.

Lemma 24.5
.

Let
{\mathbb{M}}^{\prime}
be another Morse flow category on
X
associated to a different Morse–Smale pair
(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})
. Then the following diagram commutes.

\textstyle{HM^{*}(f,g)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}^{*}(C^{*}(\mathbb{M})^{\otimes{\sf p}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{*}(C^{*}(\mathbb{M}\#{\mathbb{E}}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{HM^{*}(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}}^{*}(C^{*}({\mathbb{M}}^{\prime})^{\otimes{\sf p}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{*}(C^{*}(\mathbb{M}^{\prime}\#{\mathbb{E}}))}

Here vertical arrows are isomorphisms induced from continuation maps.

Notice that

H^{*}(C^{*}({\mathbb{M}}\#{\mathbb{E}}))\cong H^{*}(X;\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}}^{\mathbb{\Pi}}\langle\uptheta\rangle).

Then the above lemma shows that, after tensoring with
\Lambda_{\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi}
, one obtains a canonical map

QSt_{{\sf p}}:H^{*}(X;{\bf F}_{\sf p})\to H^{*}(X;\Lambda_{\widetilde{\bf K}_{\sf p}}^{\Pi}).

By construction, the chain map
\widetilde{\Phi}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
does not decrease the
\mathtt{U}
-grading, hence it indeed defines a map for each degree
k
:

QSt_{{\sf p}}:H^{k}(X;{\mathbb{F}}_{2})\to\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}H^{2k}(X;\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{2}{[\![\uptheta]\!]}}^{\Pi}),&\ {\sf p}=2,\\
H^{{\sf p}k}(X;\Lambda_{{\bf F}_{\sf p}{[\![\mathtt{U}]\!]}}^{\Pi}\langle\uptheta\rangle),&\ {\sf p}>2.\end{array}\right.

This is called the
quantum Steenrod operation
. The proof of the claim that the classical part of
QSt_{{\sf p}}
(which comes from constant holomorphic spheres) coincides with the classical Steenrod operation is the same as the semi-positive case considered by Seidel–Wilkins
[
59
]
. This finishes the proof of part (1) of Theorem
H
.

24.3.
AMS construction

We would like to construct
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-equivariant AMS lifts which is also equivariant with respect to the monoid morphism (
24.1
). It suffices to construct the lift for the corresponding moduli space of
{\sf p}+1
-marked curves, from which the flow multimodule is constructed by taking fiber products with moduli spaces of Morse trajectories.

24.3.1.
The domain moduli

We first consider the moduli space of domain curves. For each
d\geq 0
, consider

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Sigma^{\mathbb{QSt}}}(\mathbb{CP}^{d},d)

of holomorphic maps from the special marked curve
\Sigma_{\sf p}^{\mathbb{St}}
to
\mathbb{CP}^{d}
of degree
d
. Let

B_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{\Sigma^{\mathbb{QSt}}}(\mathbb{CP}^{d},d)

be the open subset whose images are not contained in any hyperplane. Then
B_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
is a
{\mathcal{G}}_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}:=PGL(d+1)
-invariant open subset. Moreover, there is an action by
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
which permutes the incoming marked points. Then one has the object

{\mathcal{C}}_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}=({{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}\times{\mathcal{G}}_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}},B_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}},C_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}})\in{\rm Ob}\underline{\smash{\bf Curve}}.

Since this is not stratified, we do not need to consider outercollaring or rigidifications at this moment. We can equip
{\mathcal{C}}_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
a
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}\times G_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
-equivariant stable complex structure, hence an object of
\underline{\smash{\bf Curve}}^{\mathbb{C}}
, using the complex analytic structure.

24.3.2.
The thickenings

Choose an integral symplectic form
\Omega
which is tamed by
J
. Consider the space of maps and framed maps

\displaystyle\ {\rm Map}_{d}(C_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}},X),\

\displaystyle\ {\rm Map}_{d}^{\rm fr}(C_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}},X)

whose elements are
(\phi,u)
resp.
(\phi,u,F)
where
\phi\in B_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
,
u:C_{\phi}\to X
is a smooth map satisfying
\int u^{*}\Omega=d
, and
F
is a framing associated to
u
and the integral symplectic form
\Omega
.
One can choose a
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-equivariant group reduction scheme

\lambda_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}:{\rm Map}_{d}^{\rm fr}(C_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}},X)\to Q_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}:=T_{1}({\mathcal{G}}_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}/G_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}),

for example, using the formula (
15.6
). On the other hand, choose a thickening datum

\iota_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}:W_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}\to\Gamma_{c}(\mathring{C}_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}\times X,\Omega^{0,1}_{\mathring{C}_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}/B_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}}\otimes TX)

where
W_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
is a unitary representation of
G_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}\cong PU(d+1)
such that
\iota_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
is equivariant with respect to both the
G_{d}
-action and the
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-action.

Then one has the thickened moduli space

V_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}:=\left\{(\phi,u,F,e)\ \left|\begin{array}[]{c}(\phi,u,F)\in{\rm Map}_{d}^{\rm fr}(C_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}},X),\ e\in W_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}},\\
\overline{\partial}u+\iota_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}(e)=0,\ \phi=\phi_{F}\in B_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}\end{array}\right.\right\}

The obstruction bundle

E_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}\to V_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}

is the trivial bundle with fiber
W_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}\oplus Q_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
and the Kuranishi section is

S_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}(\phi,u,F,e)=(e,\lambda_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}(\phi,u,F)).

The obtained topological Kuranishi space is denoted by

K_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}:=(G_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}},V_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}},E_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}},S_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}})

which also has a
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-action commuting with
G_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
.
Notice that there is a
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-equivariant evaluation map

{\rm ev}:V_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}\to X^{{\sf p}+1}

at the markings, where
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
acts on the first
{\sf p}
copies of
X
by permutation.

One still have the relative smoothness of the forgetful map

V_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}\to B_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}.

One can then also follow the approach of Section
16
(which is much simplified here) to equip the vertical tangent bundle an NC structure. See
[
13
, Section 6.2]
for related setups in Gromov–Witten theory.

Since we are working with a single space without boundary or corners, one can apply the smoothing strategy of
[
3
]
to obtain a stable
G_{d}\times{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-smoothing of
V_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
; the stabilization factor needed for the smoothing can be absorbed into the thickening space
W_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
. Moreover, one can make the evaluation map
{\rm ev}
a smooth submersion at the expense of introducing further stabilization.

Then eventually one can use the stable complex structure of
B_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
and the NC structure on vertical tangent bundles to construct a
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}\times G_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
-equivariant NC structure on
V_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
. Then one obtains an object of
\underline{\smash{\bf SKur}}^{\rm NC}
written as

K_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}=({{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}\times G_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}},V_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}},E_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}},S_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}})

with an equivariant smooth evaluation map

{\rm ev}:V_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}\to X^{{\sf p}+1}.

24.3.3.
The Kuranishi and derived orbifold multimodule

Now we will insert Morse constraints and couple with the diagonal bimodule of
{{\mathbb{E}}}
. For critical points
\underline{\smash{x}}_{1},\ldots,\underline{\smash{x}}_{\sf p},\underline{\smash{x}}^{\prime}
of
f^{\mathbb{M}}
,
w,w^{\prime}
of
f^{{{\mathbb{E}}}}
, consider

{\rm ev}^{-1}(W_{\underline{\smash{x}}_{1}}^{\rm us}\times\cdots\times W_{\underline{\smash{x}}_{\sf p}}^{\rm us}\times W_{x^{\prime}}^{\rm st})\times\mathring{M}_{w;w^{\prime}}^{{\mathbb{\Delta}}^{{\mathbb{E}}}}

where
W_{\underline{\smash{x}}_{i}}^{\rm us}
resp.
W_{\underline{\smash{x}}^{\prime}}^{\rm st}
is the uncompactified unstable resp. stable manifold and
\mathring{M}_{w;w^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{\Delta}^{{\mathbb{E}}}}
is the uncompactified moduli space of parametrized trajectories in
\widehat{S}^{\infty}
connecting
w
and
w^{\prime}
. Then we compactify by allowing breaking as follows: at negative ends, the breakings at different gradient rays/lines are independent; at the positive end, the breaking is regarded as breaking in the product space
X\times S^{\infty}
, therefore “synchronized.” Let the compactified space be

V_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}(\underline{\smash{x}}_{1}\cdots\underline{\smash{x}}_{\sf p}w;(\underline{\smash{x}}^{\prime},w^{\prime})).

Then one obtains a stratified smooth Kuranishi space

K_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}(\underline{\smash{x}}_{1}\cdots\underline{\smash{x}}_{\sf p}w;(\underline{\smash{x}}^{\prime},w^{\prime}))

whose symmetry group is reduced to
G_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
.

We define a lift of the Steenrod multimodule to
\underline{\smash{\bf SKur}}^{\rm NC}
, denoted by
\hat{\mathbb{X}}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
. Given objects
x_{i}=(a_{i},\underline{\smash{x}}_{i})
where
a_{i}\in\Pi
and
\underline{\smash{x}}_{i}\in{\rm crit}f
,
i=1,\ldots,{\sf p}
and
x^{\prime}=(a^{\prime},\underline{\smash{x}}^{\prime})
, denote
d=\Omega(a^{\prime}-a_{1}-\cdots-a_{\sf p})
and

K_{x_{1}\cdots x_{{\sf p}}w;(x^{\prime},w^{\prime})}^{\mathbb{QSt}}:=K_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}(\underline{\smash{x}}_{1}\cdots\underline{\smash{x}}_{\sf p}w;(x^{\prime},w^{\prime}))\in\underline{\smash{\bf SKur}}^{\rm NC}.

The multimodule structural maps are obtained in a very simple way as we only need to consider broken Morse trajectories. Hence one obtains a multimodule
\hat{\mathbb{X}}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
over
({\mathbb{M}},\ldots,{\mathbb{M}},{{\mathbb{E}}};{\mathbb{M}}\#{\mathbb{E}})
. One is readily to verify that it is a lift of
{\mathbb{X}}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
and satisfies the expected equivariance property.

Finally one takes group quotient by
G_{d}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
and obtains a lift to
\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}^{\rm NC}
, denoted by
\tilde{\mathbb{X}}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
. One can do the outercollaring as the last step, providing the desired AMS construction claimed in Theorem
24.2
.

Lastly, if we make a different set of choices, then the trivial homotopy from
{\mathbb{X}}^{\mathbb{QSt}}
to itself can be lifted to
{}_{\lfloor}\underline{\smash{\bf dOrb}}_{\rm rig}^{\rm NC}
in the equivariant way, between the AMS lifts of two different choices. This finishes the proof of Theorem
24.2
.

24.4.
Comparing to Seidel–Wilkins’ definition in semi-positive case

When
(X,\omega)
is semi-positive, Seidel–Wilkins
[
59
]
defined their quantum Steenrod operations using the classical approach. We prove that our definition agrees with theirs.

Theorem 24.6
.

When
(X,\omega)
is semi-positive, the quantum Steenrod operation defined above agrees with the definition of Seidel–Wilkins.

Remark 24.7
.

Strictly speaking, we are referring to the operations denoted by
QSt
instead of
Q\Sigma_{b}
in
[
59
]
, where the latter is featured more in recent studies of arithmetic properties of quantum connections on semi-positive symplectic manifolds (cf.
[
19
,
18
]
,
[
40
,
39
]
,
[
10
,
9
]
). We can extend our
QSt_{\sf p}
to define analogs of
Q\Sigma_{b}
using our framework and prove that it reduces to the Seidel–Wilkins operation when
(X,\omega)
is semi-positive. Moreover, such an extension is covariantly constant under the quantum connection defined using FOP perturbations. We will not discuss such extensions in this manuscript.

24.4.1.
The construction of Seidel–Wilkins

The quantum Steenrod operation defined in
[
59
]
is based on the classical transversality via adding
{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-equivariant inhomogeneous terms parametrized by
S^{\infty}
. We extend the perturbation to
\widehat{S}^{\infty}
. Consider inhomogeneous terms

\nu_{S^{2},\eta,z,x}^{\rm eq}:T_{z}S^{2}\to T_{x}X\ {\rm for\ }(\eta,z,x)\in\widehat{S}^{\infty}\times S^{2}\times X

which satisfies

\nu_{S^{2},\sigma(\eta),z,x}^{\rm eq}=\nu_{S^{2},\eta,\sigma(z),x}^{\rm eq}\circ D\sigma:T_{z}S^{2}\to T_{x}M\ \ \forall\sigma\in{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}.

Then one can consider the moduli space of maps
u:S^{2}\to X
satisfying the perturbed Cauchy–Riemann equation. More precisely, consider pairs
(\eta,u)
where
\eta\in\widehat{S}^{\infty}
and
u:S^{2}\to X
such that

\overline{\partial}_{J}u(z)+\nu_{S^{2},\eta,z,u(z)}^{\rm eq}=0.

We modify Seidel–Wilkins’ formulation slightly using the Morse model. For a comprehensive comparison with the original Seidel–Wilkins’ setup, which is based on finding explicit cycles representing generators if
H^{*}(B{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}};{\mathbb{F}}_{p})
, we refer the reader to
[
12
, Appendix A]
. Recall that one has identified critical points of
f^{{\mathbb{E}}}
. For each pair of critical points
w,w^{\prime}\in{\rm crit}f^{{\mathbb{E}}}
, denote

\mathring{\Delta}_{w;w^{\prime}}:=\Big\{\eta:{\mathbb{R}}\to\widehat{S}^{\infty}\ |\ \eta^{\prime}(s)=\nabla f^{{\mathbb{E}}}(\eta(s))=0,\ \lim_{s\to-\infty}\eta(s)=w,\ \lim_{s\to+\infty}\eta(s)=w^{\prime}\Big\}.

Then elements of
\mathring{\Delta}_{w;w^{\prime}}
is determined by its evaluation at
s=0
. On the other hand, one has

\widehat{S}^{\infty}=\bigsqcup_{w,w^{\prime}}\mathring{\Delta}_{w;w^{\prime}}.

Then given critical points
\underline{\smash{x}}_{1},\ldots,\underline{\smash{x}}_{{\sf p}},\underline{\smash{x}}^{\prime}
of
f:X\to{\mathbb{R}}
and
a\in\Pi
, consider the moduli space

\mathring{\mathcal{M}}_{\underline{\smash{x}}_{1}\cdots\underline{\smash{x}}_{{\sf p}};\underline{\smash{x}}^{\prime}}^{\rm SW}(a;\mathring{\Delta}_{w;w^{\prime}})

which consists of pairs
(u,\eta)
where
u:S^{2}\to X
is a smooth map and
\eta\in\mathring{\Delta}_{w;w^{\prime}}
satisfying

\overline{\partial}_{J}u(z)+\nu_{S^{2},\eta,z,u(z)}^{\rm eq}=0

and

u(z_{i})\in W^{\rm us}_{\underline{\smash{x}}_{i}},\ u(z_{\infty})\in W^{\rm st}_{\underline{\smash{x}}^{\prime}}.

Seidel–Wilkins proved that for a generic inhomogeneous term
\nu_{S^{2}}^{\rm eq}
, whenever the expected dimension of the above moduli space is zero or one, the defining perturbed
J
-holomorphic map equation is transverse.

Lemma 24.8
.

(cf.
[
59
, Lemma 4.1]
) For a generic
\omega
-compatible almost complex structure
J
and a
{\mathbb{Z}}\times{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\sf p}}
-equivariant inhomogeneous term
\nu_{S^{2},\eta,z,x}^{\rm eq}
, any moduli space
\mathring{\mathcal{M}}_{\underline{\smash{x}}_{1}\cdots\underline{\smash{x}}_{\sf p};\underline{\smash{x}}^{\prime}}^{\rm SW}(a;\mathring{\Delta}_{w;w^{\prime}})
is transverse provided that its expected dimension is at most
1
. Moreover,

(1)

When the expected dimension is zero,
\mathring{\mathcal{M}}_{\underline{\smash{x}}_{1}\cdots\underline{\smash{x}}_{\sf p};\underline{\smash{x}}^{\prime}}^{\rm SW}(a;\mathring{\Delta}_{w;w^{\prime}})
consists of finitely many points.

(2)

When the expected dimension is one,
\mathring{\mathcal{M}}_{\underline{\smash{x}}_{1}\cdots\underline{\smash{x}}_{\sf p};\underline{\smash{x}}^{\prime}}^{\rm SW}(a;\mathring{\Delta}_{w;w^{\prime}})
admits a natural compactification which is a compact 1-dimensional manifold with boundary.

Proof.

The only difference between our case and the case of
[
59
]
is that there is no requirement for
{\mathbb{Z}}
-symmetry in
[
59
]
. However, this can be achieved as the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-action is free.
∎
